Part I
Histories of the Present


13
 1
 The starting point for the question of the present is Foucault’s triple
reading, which today is very well known, of the Kantian text  What is
Enlightenment? . Foucault had been interested in the text since 1978,
carrying out an extensive analysis at a talk given that year at the  Societé
Française de  Philosophie . In this first reading, the attempt to distin-
guish critique on the one hand – as way of finding the limits of our
capacity to know – and the  Aufklärung on the other – as an attitude
towards our own present – is still made in terms of a strong historical
analysis. Foucault explicitly announces his intention to do ‘a history of
this critical attitude’; and indeed, in the first reading, Foucault traces at
least four moments, characterizing these in terms of their specificity of
thought and practices, stressing their differences: the Christian pastoral,
the explosion of the art of government ‘starting from the XV and the
XVI Century’, the moment of the Enlightenment as the emergence of
the ‘art of not being governed’ and then, in relation to a present more
contemporary to Foucault, a certain number of references including,
most notably, to the Frankfurt School. In similar fashion, alongside these
chronological references which delineate his analysis, Foucault puts into
play a differentiation of places – through the opposition ‘in Germany/in
France’ – as if it is a question of redoubling the history of the systems of
thought with a geography. Some years later it is precisely this quite tradi-
tional schema of Foucault’s work – proceeding through periodizations
 1
‘What Are We At the Present Time?’
Foucault and the Question of the
Present
 Judith  Revel


14 Judith Revel
(and together, through a mutual differentiation of these periods) on the
basis of the way in which they structure their economy of representa-
tions and practices – that seems to be inflected and reformulated along
another axis: the relationship to our present. Indeed, in the two texts
published in 1984 – one (published) in the US, the other in France – the
mode of analysis shifts.
 This appears on the surface to be a matter of engaging in the same
exercise as in 1978, tracing the history of the way in which this event
called ‘ Aufklärung ’ emerged at a certain time; it is an event that has rede-
fined the practice of philosophy – and of the modernity within which
that practice was situated – as a problematization of contemporary
reality [ actualité ]. In the text published in the US, the first pages are dedi-
cated, in a quite expected way, to the contextualization of Kant’s text
(with reference made here to Mendelssohn and Lessing) and to classi-
fying the ways in which philosophy had thought, until that time, about
the theme of the present. Foucault finds three main forms, associated
with three figures, that are in turn emblematic of three moments of the
history of thought: Plato, Augustine and Vico. In the same way, in the
text published in France in May 1984 – an extract from his lecture given
at the Collège de France on 5 January 1983 – Foucault contends, appar-
ently with no ambiguity, that
 The  Aufklärung is a period, it is a period which designates itself, formu-
lates its own motto, its own precept, and says what it has to do, as much
in relation to the general history of thought, reason, and knowledge
as in relation to its own present and to the bodies and forms of knowl-
edge, ignorance, illusion, and institutions, etcetera in which it can
recognize its historical situation. [ ... ] In this question of the  Aufklärung
we see one of the first manifestations of a certain way of philosophizing
which has had a long history over the following two centuries. After
all, it does seem to me that one of the major functions of what is called
‘modern’ philosophy – whose beginning and development can be situ-
ated at the very end of the eighteenth century and in the nineteenth
century – one of its essential functions is questioning itself about its
own present reality [ actualité ] (Foucault, 2010: 14–15).
 The quotation is clear, and the number of markers pointing to the
need for a precise work of historicization – ‘period’, ‘general history’,
‘historical situation’, ‘long history’ and ‘beginning’ – exist to reassure
us: we are fully within the Foucaultian methodology. It is the historical
context – which does not exclude a certain thickness of time: we talk


‘What Are We At the Present Time?’ 15
here of a medium length periodization since it is a question of describing
a phenomenon ‘that has had a long history for two Centuries’ that is
once again at the core of the reading suggested by Foucault. Besides, it is
always against the background of a differentiation between consecutive
epistemic forms that it is possible to retrace the emergence of the system
of thought typical of a certain age. Thus, just as the analysis of madness
in the Classical Age hinges on a quick recall of the figures of the mad
during the Renaissance; just as  The Order of Things starts with a descrip-
tion of the way in which the mediaeval thought still used to enmesh
mythology and science, contrary to what was being constructed within
the great realm of the scientific rationality and the taxonomy of knowl-
edges associated with it; in a similar way,  Discipline and Punish needs to
start with the torture of Damiens in order to measure the distance that
the disciplines had established with regard to the very idea of the exem-
plar punishment, in order to privilege the notion of surveillance, of the
social orthopaedic and of the achievement of productive performances.
 Here, in Foucault’s analyses, it is always also a question of working on
the basis of the opposition between economies of thought. Thus, this
game of oppositions is constructed starting from a historical periodiza-
tion. But Foucault credits Kant himself with this method of differenti-
ation between big epistemic ‘blocs’, that is analysis which sets out to
measure the gaps that the historical inquiry enables us to retrace: ‘It is
in the reflection on today as difference in history and as motive for a
particular philosophical task that the novelty of this text appears to me
to lie’ (Foucault, 2003: 309). What Kant finds out, Foucault suggests, is
first of all the way in which the monogram of the  Aufklärung is at the
same time the philosophical care of the present and the procedure of
differentiation which allows us to understand what we are on the very
basis of what we are not anymore. Up to this point, everything happens
as usual. However, it is not so simple.
In those same texts there is something at stake that is completely unex-
pected and that contradicts or troubles this procedure that we mentioned
in its functioning. This factor consists precisely in a radical unhooking
from the work of periodization and historicization.
 2
 Let’s listen to Foucault himself here:
 I know that modernity is often spoken of as an epoch, or at least as
a set of features characteristic of an epoch; situated on a calendar,


16 Judith Revel
it would be preceded by a more or less naive or archaic premoder-
nity, and followed by an enigmatic and troubling ‘postmodernity’.
And then we find ourselves asking whether modernity constitutes
the sequel to the Enlightenment and its development, or whether
we are to see it as a rupture or a deviation with respect to the basic
principles of the eighteenth century. Thinking back on Kant’s text,
I wonder whether we may not envisage modernity as an attitude
rather than as a period of history. And by ‘attitude,’ I mean a mode of
relating to contemporary reality [ actualité ]; a voluntary choice made
by certain people; in the end, a way of thinking and feeling; a way,
too, of acting and behaving that at one and the same time marks a
relation of belonging and presents itself as a task. No doubt, a bit like
what the Greeks called an ethos (Foucault, 2003: 309).
 There are two points to be reformulated about this long quotation.
What Foucault describes as a possible posture of research (‘I know that
modernity is often spoken of as an epoch, or at least as a set of features
characteristic of an epoch; situated on a calendar’) could be said also
with reference to his own work. How did Foucaultian analysis progress
if not precisely defining periodizations (epochs, ages) built on the basis
of ‘characteristic marks’ in order to formulate their specificity? However,
it seems that Foucault wants to get rid of precisely this. Here, it is no
longer a question of simply limiting the work of analysis to an archae-
ology – the reconstruction of an isomorphism within history, namely,
of a layer of past knowledges and practices: in other words, a system
of thought built on a precise periodization; or to a genealogy, which,
starting from the results of the archaeology, consists of an inquiry made,
through differentiation, in the direction of our system of thought: an
interrogation of the difference between past(s) and present. Rather, it is
a question of introducing a third term: an ‘attitude’.
 This attitude seems to be built beyond – or, in any case, independently
of – this work of periodization that until this point founded the very
possibility of the Foucaultian method. It no longer appears dependent
on a history, and it instead operates at another level: it involves an
ethics, Foucault tells us. But this ethics is not an object of archaeological
inquiry, as it might be supposed to be – the meticulous reconstruction
of the partitions enabling, at some point, a certain way of thinking and
practising the ethics to emerge. Instead, it comes out of any possible
specific reference to this or that configuration of thought. In the other
version of the reading of  What is Enlightenment? (from the 5 January
lecture), Foucault turns the same idea around:


‘What Are We At the Present Time?’ 17
 After all, the  Aufklärung , both as singular event inaugurating European
modernity and as a permanent process which manifests itself in the
form of the history of reason, the development and establishment
of forms of rationality and technology, the autonomy and authority
of knowledge, all of this, this question of the  Aufklärung – or of
reason and the use of reason as a historical problem – seems to me
to have run through all philosophical thought from Kant up to now
(Foucault, 2010: 20).
 Therefore, according to Foucault, the Kantian text invites a kind of ques-
tioning that traverses history rather than merely depending upon it.
Everything happens as if it were necessary to reproduce different layers
of thought and to render visible their specific characteristics – namely,
to highlight the differences between different systems of past thought
(archaeology); then, the basis of this work raises the problem of our own
historical situation, or of our own system of representation, by stressing
the difference between our own system of thought and previous ones
(genealogy). All this in order to do an effort today, on the contrary, to
think; henceforth, a form of philosophical interrogation that, taking the
contemporary reality [ actualité ] as a material, interrogates itself less on
the basis of existing difference between different pasts, or in relation to
those pasts, than on the constancy of a ‘permanent process’, as Foucault
puts it. And it is precisely here that the reading of Kant goes, at least in
part, beyond its own context. Once again ‘the  Aufklärung is more than
simply an episode in the history of ideas’. 1
 What is this ‘exit from history’ based on? This is the second point
we need to examine. It seems that, first of all, it is something linked
to the displacement of the process of historical differentiation that, as
emphasized above, was central to the Foucaultian methodology. To reit-
erate, the archaeological approach reconstructed the historical consist-
ence of an isomorphism of thought on the basis of what it differentiates
itself through its emergence – in this regard let’s call it the model of
 The Order of Things . Conversely, the genealogical approach consisted
in extending this work of differentiation based on historical-epistemic
blocs towards our present, with the precise intention of understanding
the system of thought in which we are situated. More specifically, this
constituted the general model of the courses given by Foucault at the
Collège de France during the late 1970s: in these years, the inquiry
on governmentality undertaken by Foucault is built on the upstream
periodization initially announced – the Modern Age – in order to
locate a caesura within it – the emergence of the arts of government,


18 Judith Revel
linked by Foucault to the emergence in the sixteenth century of an anti-
Machiavellian literature – and downstream, on a close questioning of
the dispositives of biopolitical governmentality in its different variants
of neoliberalism in the twentieth century – particularly in Germany and
the US.
 In the first instance, we had the differences between past(s) and past(s)
and, in the second, differences between past(s) and present. History was
still the tool of differentiation, that is to say, as an instrument to circum-
scribe objects of thought and carve up different periods. Here, on the
contrary, it is the link of the ‘work of the difference’ – or of what I have
referred to elsewhere as the work of the thought of discontinuity – to
history that is transformed. The suggestion comes probably from Kant or
more precisely from the way in which Foucault chooses to read Kant:
 Criticism indeed consists of analysing and reflecting upon limits. But
if the Kantian question was that of knowing what limits knowledge
has to renounce transgressing, it seems to me that the critical question
today has to be turned back into a positive one: in what is given to us
as universal necessary obligatory what place is occupied by whatever
is singular contingent and the product of arbitrary constraints ? The
point in brief is to transform the critique conducted in the form of
necessary limitation into a practical critique that takes the form of a
possible transgression (Foucault, 2003: 315).
 This turning point is so important that Foucault, in the same text, comes
back to it three pages later, using an almost identical formulation:
 The critical ontology of ourselves has to be considered not, certainly,
as a theory, a doctrine, nor even as a permanent body of knowledge
that is accumulating; it has to be conceived as an attitude, an ethos,
a philosophical life in which the critique of what we are is at one and
the same time the historical analysis of the limits that are imposed on
us and an experiment with the possibility of going beyond them [ de
 leur franchissement possible ] (Foucault, 2003: 319).
 Drawing on Foucault’s expression quoted above, the ‘difference
in history’ (Foucault, 2003: 309) is played out between the present
and what could deny it at any time, positing it outside of itself and
opening it up to something other than what is already there. The
difference is by now what could be imagined between a present


‘What Are We At the Present Time?’ 19
that we belong to and a future and that is, at least in part, ours to
construct. From this point of view Kant is useful to the extent that
we bend the critical approach to the historical analysis – to which
 What is Enlightenment? belongs, as well as transcendental philosophy
and ‘philosophical journalism’. 2 But this approach is obviously not
enough. In this displacement of the question of the critique from the
standpoint of the present, there remains a lack. Missing here is the
idea of a difference – that is, of a possible discontinuity – existing at
the core of the present.
 3
 The introduction of the theme of the ‘possible difference’ is introduced
via two elements central to the Foucaultian reflection. The first is merely
lexical: it consists for Foucault in detaching the theme of the present
from that of contemporary reality ( actualité) , distinguishing one from
the other. This detachment between the two terms corresponds to a
logic whose specificity is, here, absolutely clear. The present is to the
contrary that which at any moment might interrupt this strange ‘state
of equilibrium’ grounded in the emergence of a specific and histor-
ical system of thought that we could describe as the ‘already there’ of
knowledges and practices, like an order of the discourse, of the repre-
sentations and of the ways of action that are already instantiated and
in which we find ourselves already situated. The present is of course
all but an instantaneous moment, since it implies an average medium-
term historical temporality. For instance, we could claim, leaving aside
the quarrels between certain contemporary philosophers – that we are
the offspring of this modernity in which the Enlightenment is situated:
our present is still, at least in part, that of modernity. On the contrary
contemporary reality [ actualité ] works as an instance of rupture, as a
process of differentiation, as the instantiation of discontinuity within
a historical layer of continuity: it is a tipping point, a rupture of the
present.
 Indeed, in Foucault’s two readings of  What is Enlightenment? , the
hesitation between the present (that is the ongoing historical-epis-
temic determination) and contemporary reality (the tipping point of
that determination) – or at least the movement of going back and
forth between two different qualifications of our relationship to
the present state of things – is evident. On the one hand there is, of
course, the necessity of a ‘historical ontology of ourselves’ (Foucault


20 Judith Revel
2010: 15); but on the other hand, there is also an ‘an ontology of
present reality (ontologie de l’actualité) [ ... ] an ontology of ourselves’
(Foucault, 2010: 21). Between the two terms, through a tension estab-
lished between the two poles, Foucault is led at the same time, to
construct a commentary based on the Kantian text and push beyond
the reference to the Aufklärung. Here, I would like to consider further
this twisting of Kant’s term, beginning with the need to introduce
the possibility of difference at the very core of our present. Moreover,
the very possibility of this ‘third relationship to history’ is precisely
predicated on this twist.
 4
 If on the one hand, the analysis of the present and of the way in which
philosophy reached its own maturity makes of it the object of its
reflection the text analysed by Foucault is still  What is Enlightenment? ,
in the last pages of one of the two analysis – the Lecture of the 1983
Course at the Collège de France – it is another Kantian text that is
summoned up in an unexpected way. Set within the interpretation of
the 1984 text, the last pages centre on the  Conflict of Faculties , precisely
at the moment when the turn towards contemporary reality [ actualité ]
is introduced – namely, as a problematization of the way in which we
can or cannot instantiate a discontinuity in respect to that which we
historically belong to. As Foucault stresses just before introducing this
other ‘use’ of Kant starting from the text of 1798, ‘the question will
no longer be one of his adherence to a doctrine or a tradition, or of his
membership of a human community in general, but a question about
him being part of a present, about his membership of a particular “we”
if you like, which is linked, to a greater or lesser extent, to a cultural
ensemble characteristic of his contemporary reality’ (Foucault, 2010:
13). Therefore, this possible turn, this questioning of the present
state of things which can produce an interruption concerns not only
our knowledges and our practices. It also immediately includes the
question of the subject-form itself in its collective inflection (‘we’),
namely, also in its political dimension. Consequently the reference to
the  Conflict of Faculties , explicitly introduced by Foucault as a ‘sequel
of the 1784 text’ (Foucault, 2010: 15), acquires its full sense: it intro-
duces on the one hand the idea of the possibility of discontinuity,
and on the other hand a political stake. This is essentially done by
introducing an almost unannounced and new to Foucault’s thought:
the revolution.


‘What Are We At the Present Time?’ 21
 Indeed, Foucault quotes Kant as follows:
 There is an extremely interesting text on this: ‘no matter whether
the revolution of a gifted people, which we have seen carried out in
our time [Kant is therefore referring to the French Revolution; M.F.]
succeeds or fails, no matter whether it piles up misery and atrocities’
to the point, he says, ‘that a sensible man, who could hope to see it
through successfully at the second attempt, would nonetheless decide
never to make the experiment at this price’ (Foucault, 2010: 17–18).
 And Foucault follows this up with the following comment:
we have to consider the Revolution, which in any case always risks
returning to the old ways, [ ... ] as an event, as a sort of event whose
content is unimportant, but whose existence in the past constitutes a
permanent virtuality, the guarantee for future history of the non-for-
getfulness’ (Foucault, 2010: 19). The second dissertation of the  Conflict
of Faculties – which seeks to discuss the conflict between the faculty
of philosophy and the faculty of law starting from the question as
to whether there is ‘a constant progress for mankind?’ – is made to
conform here to what Foucault is attempting to articulate. It does not
matter if a revolution succeeded or not, if it is marked by mistakes
and digressions, or if it comes paradoxically to stand for a restoration
which erases all its traces, at least for a certain time. The revolution is
not a historical fact: it is neither a date nor a set of circumstances – and
even less, perhaps, does it constitute what Foucault calls a ‘content’.
It does not matter if in its operation the revolution is good or bad,
favourable or cruel, just or unjust, successful or failed. The revolution
is akin to an ever-present virtuality, a bifurcation that can be deployed
at any time, enacting the permanent possibility of a discontinuity. It
embodies precisely this doubling of the present and of the contempo-
rary reality [ actualité ] that Foucault strives to to spot.
 It would be very interesting to read in parallel the pages to the commen-
tary on the Conflicts of Faculties with certain texts that Foucault, some
years before, dedicated to the Iranian revolution – in particular ‘Useless to
Revolt’ violent critiques which reproached Foucault for the inconsequent
enthusiasm he felt in the face of the Iranian revolution (that is to say, the
revolution which was rapidly becoming the dictatorship of the Imams)
actually relies on the same argument of his analysis on the idea of revolu-
tion in the text on 1984, starting from Kant: the revolution is a virtuality


22 Judith Revel
of difference, the opening of a bifurcation within history – namely, an act
of freedom, independent of the concrete form in which] this embodies
and of the effects that it can engender. (Foucault, 2000). But let’s go back
to Foucault’s reading of Kant. Here, Foucault is finally able to conclude:
 The other present reality [ actualité ] encountered by Kant, the
Revolution – the Revolution as at once an event, rupture, and
upheaval in history, as failure, and almost necessary failure, but with
at the same time a value, an operational value in history and the
progress of the human species [ ... ] these two questions – ‘What is
 Aufklärung ?’ and ‘What is the Revolution?’ – which are the two forms
in which Kant poses the question of his own present reality [ ... ] What
is the present field of our experiences? What is the present field of
possible experiences? (Foucault, 2010: 20).
 At the end of this ‘trajectory’, it is the idea of this rupture that contem-
porary reality [ actualité ] represents, from the very inside of present
historical determinations, which leads Foucault to go back to our rela-
tionship with the  Enlightenment , and to propose an overcoming of it,
or a twist. First of all, the  Kantian critique is somehow torn out of its
own philosophical place. Instead of taking the shape of a necessary
limitation of the understanding (‘what can I know?’) and by return, of
the opening of the fields of the practical reason and of the aesthetic
judgement, it is entirely reabsorbed and reshaped, starting with a
Kantian thought of history; namely, under the twofold perspective of
a diagnostic which concerns the present ( philosophy at the stage of its
maturity interrogates on the its own historical moment), on the one
hand, and of an openness to the discontinuity of this present, on the
other (the theme of the revolution). The critical challenge, conceived
as a process of restriction (of possible understanding), is consequently
transformed into a proposition of openness referred to by Foucault
in the passage quoted above as ‘the present field of possible experi-
ences’. The revolution as a virtuality always at stake within history.
This twisting, which is a philosophically powerful gesture, will provoke
very quickly some harsh reactions from a more classical neokantian
reading: it is undoubtedly a forcing [ forçage ] from the standpoint of
Kantianism itself. Nevertheless, the virtues that Foucault attributed
to the idea of ‘uses’ are well-known, and conversely, the intellectual
sadness he felt in the face of the  diktats of a history of philosophy
conceived in terms of an absolute devotion or of an exegetic ortho-
doxy. ‘Let’s leave to their pity those who do not want the lively legacy


‘What Are We At the Present Time?’ 23
of Enlightenment to endure in its integrity. This pity is of course the
most touching betrayal. It is not Enlightenment’s left over that are to
be preserved; what is being preserved is the notion of this event and its
meaning’. 3 On the basis of the doubling between the historical conti-
nuity – the present – and the evenemential discontinuity  – the ‘revo-
lution’, the contemporary reality (actualité) – this twisting is entirely
directed towards the construction of a reopening of the history ahead,
on the cusp of a ‘today’ that opens out towards what doesn’t already
exist. Foucault clearly sums this up thus:
 Criticism indeed consists of analysing and reflecting upon limits. But
if the Kantian question was that of knowing what limits knowledge
has to renounce transgressing, it seems to me that the critical question
today has to be turned back into a positive one: in what is given to us
as universal necessary obligatory what place is occupied by whatever
is singular contingent and the product of arbitrary constraints ? The
point in brief is to transform the critique conducted in the form of
necessary limitation into a practical critique that takes the form of a
possible transgression (Foucault, 2003: 315).
 Some sentences later Foucault provides further clarification:
 And this critique will be genealogical in the sense that it will not
deduce from the form of what we are what it is impossible for us to
do and to know; but it will separate out, from the contingency that
has made us what we are, the possibility of no longer being, doing, or
thinking what we are, do, or think (Foucault, 2003: 315–316).
 Therefore, discontinuity is produced as both rupture and as overcoming,
as an interruption and as a process of constitution. It is, literally, a trial –
I insist on this term because we find it again in the lectures dedicated to
the Cynics. This trial is not simply a refusal of the present state of things.
Moreover, it never entails an exit from history or ignores the weight
of historical determinations, and it does not imply to consider us as if
we were not the product of a specific history. Instead, it always presup-
poses a precise ‘archaeological’ cartography of the system of thought
we belong to, in the specific historical moment which is ours and that
which we call the present – the present state of the world. 4 It is also
always a constitution, a production. It is this constituent dimension
which represents the openness of the history to what Foucault names
‘the possible crossing-over [ franchissement ]’ (Foucault, 2003: 315).


24 Judith Revel
 5
 This overcoming [ franchissement ] might lead us to conclude that a certain
return is at stake, a return to themes that in the early 1960s circulated
in Foucault’s research, – in particular under the influence of Georges
Bataille’s work – we could recall here the astonishing homage, ‘Preface
to Transgression’, published in 1963 in the Journal  Critique , which for
a while seemed crucial to the way in which Foucault was thinking the
relationship to the limit (Foucault, 1977). But, in 1984 the ‘possible
overcoming’ [ franchissement ] arises as an inverted figure of transgression:
it is not given as a relation to a limit perceived as a double of the dialec-
tics – the transgression cannot be understood other than in relation to
the limit and, conversely, the limit is confirmed through its transgres-
sion. Rather, this ‘possible over-crossing’ [ franchissement ] represents an
opening which takes the form both of a historical analysis and of an
experimentation  at the edge of this history. As Foucault clearly states, ‘we
have to move beyond the outside-inside alternative; we have to be at the
frontiers’ (Foucault, 2003: 315).
 It is in this sense that this ‘critical-historical attitude’ (Foucault, 2003:
316), which is also always an experimental attitude, must take the form,
according to Foucault, of a ‘historical-practical test’ (Foucault, 2003:
316). We never exit from history, but we always experiment inside it.
That is, we invent from the very inside of a present state of historical
determinations of an  already-there of history. And this invention will
touch on two specific domains, which are at once linked – one impli-
cating the other and vice versa – and distinct: subjectivity, on the one
hand, and life, or modes of life, on the other hand. It is precisely in these
two laboratories that for Foucault, from now on, it will be necessary to
work; and today it is by focusing on these two laboratories that we can
try to carry on his work.
 Notes
 1 .  My translation. This passage is from the French original version of  The
Government of Self and Others (lecture of 5 January 1983) but it has not been
translated in the English version.
 2 .  See ‘Pour une morale de l’inconfort’ (Foucault, 2001). The reference to Kant
opens a text on the book of Jean Daniel,  L’Ere des ruptures (Grasset, 1979) and
Foucault immediately comments on: ‘Who are we today? [ ... ] But I think that
this question is finally the work of the journalist’ (2001: 783).
 3 .  This passage, included in the original French text,  Le  Gouvernement de  soi et des
 autres , is not included in the English translation.


‘What Are We At the Present Time?’ 25
 4 .  And Foucault clarifies: ‘archaeological – and not transcendental – in the
sense that it will not seek to identify the universal structures of all knowl-
edge [connaissance] or of all possible moral action, but will seek to treat the
instances of discourse that articulate what we think, say, and do as so many
historical events’ (Foucault, 2003: 315). Therefore, the Kantian project is
plunged into history and the work of critique becomes that of historiciza-
tion – before opening the perspective and the discontinuity as interruption
and constitution.
 References
 Foucault, M. (1977) ‘A Preface to Transgression’, in Bourchard, D. (ed.)  Language,
Counter-Memory, Practice: Selected Essays and Interviews , Ithaca: Cornel University
Press, pp. 29–52.
 Foucault, M. (2000) ‘Useless to Revolt?’, in Faubion, J. D. (ed.)  Power: Essential
Works of Foucault 1954–1984, Vol. 3 . New York: New Press, pp. 449–53.
 Foucault, M. (2001) ‘Pour une morale de l’inconfort’, in  Dits et  Ecrits, II , Paris:
Gallimard, pp. 783–87.
 Foucault, M. (2003) ‘What is Enlightenment?’, in Rabinow P. (ed.)  Ethics: Essential
Works of Michel Foucault 1954–1984, Vol. 1 , New York: New Press, pp. 303–20.
 Foucault, M. (2010)  The Government of Self and Others: Lectures at the  Collège de
France 1982–1983 , Gros, F. (ed.), Basingstoke: Palgrave Macmillan.


26
 Pinning down Foucault’s relationship to Marx and Marxism is a mani-
festly maddening enterprise. The French philosopher’s vaunted hostility
to all forms of ‘affiliation’ (Foucault, 2012: 101) was particularly intense
when it came to what Sartre – an early philosophical nemesis and later
ally in activism – deemed the untranscendable horizon of our times.
The many interviews where Foucault was coaxed into confronting
this matter head-on are replete with feints, ironies and provocations,
suffused with a profound hostility towards the demand that he position
himself, practically and theoretically, vis-à-vis the long-dominant form
of oppositional social thought (Foucault, 1991a, 1996, 2000a, 2000b,
2012a). As with postwar intellectuals in general, but perhaps even more
intensely, this relationship was nothing if not over-determined: by the
overbearing presence of the French Communist Party (of which Foucault
had been a member between 1950 and 1953); by Foucault’s judgments
about Eastern Bloc policies; by intellectual rivalries and alliances (with
Althusser, Sartre and Deleuze, the  nouveaux philosophes ); and by the
shifting articulations between philosophy and political intervention,
with Foucault, for instance, pushing to radicalize Maoist conceptions of
popular justice in the early ’70s (Foucault, 1980), while resonating with
the growing anti-communist consensus in the second half of the decade
(Christofferson, 2004; Dews, 1979).
 The picture becomes even more complex if we factor in not just the
temporal determinants of conjuncture but the spatial ones of address:
‘Marxism’ signified markedly different things to Foucault whether
talking to his Trotskyist Tunisian students (Chitty, 2012) or reporting
on the Iranian revolution, lending solidarity to Warsaw Pact dissidents
or responding to the Black Panthers’ struggles against the carceral order
of US racial imperialism (Toscano, 2013). And the reception of Foucault
2
 What Is Capitalist Power?
Reflections on ‘Truth and
Juridical Forms’
 Alberto  Toscano


What is Capitalist Power? 27
was also profoundly inflected by the mutable meanings of ‘Marxism’ in
different situations: contrast, for example, the way in which his ‘micro-
physics of power’ was feted by partisans of  autonomia in Italy and roundly
attacked by theorists close to the Italian Communist Party (Rella, 1977),
with the curious way in which his concepts came to relay those of a  sui
generis British Althusserianism in the 1980s and 1990s. Reflecting on
Foucault’s legacy – to employ a very un-Foucaultian term – in order to
fashion a critical history of our present would also require a reckoning
with what it means to now revisit this elusive relation (one that often
appears as a  non -relation).
 Limiting ourselves to the life of the mind alone, ours is a predicament
in which the political question of Marxism is arguably under- rather
than over-determined; today, fruitful as their historical reconstruction
may be, Foucault’s sallies and evasions are of less moment. A superfi-
cial, or indeed quantitative, survey of writing in the humanities and
social sciences would suggest that Foucault has ‘won’. Or rather, a kind
of ambient Foucaultianism seems to have become the untranscendable
horizon of our own academic present – witness the inflated fortunes
of ‘biopolitics’; the rise of governmentality to the status of a paradigm
in those (post-)colonial contexts Foucault largely ignored; the prefer-
ence for resistance over revolution; the ‘historical nominalism’ which
baulks at any thinking of totality and trades in discontinuity, archives
and events.
 Now, it seems evident that, barring very select exceptions, the ‘appli-
cation’ of Foucault, or even worse the ‘affiliation’ to Foucault, has
produced very few works or research programs that can genuinely claim
to have creatively ‘repeated’ the achievements of his masterworks. Now
that so many of Foucault’s concepts have become the  lingua franca of
so many university programs, it is perhaps time to consider how he,
too, to paraphrase Adorno, should be defended against his disciples;
how his precariously forged (and often happily abandoned) intellectual
tools should not be allowed to congeal into a stultifying  doxa , through
a process of ‘ideologisation’ that – not so incidentally – often relies on a
removal of any Marxian trace from his work (Legrand, 2004: 28).
 Inasmuch as contemporary critical Marxist theory has shed much of
the political halo that elicited Foucault’s hostility and the theoretical
anti-Marxism of the 1980s and 1990s – much of which drew a peculiar
authority from Foucault – continues to lose its sheen, while the persist-
ence of capitalism defies its nominalist disavowal, a renewed encounter
between Foucault and Marx(ism) has much to recommend it. And indeed,
some of the more interesting uptakes of Foucault’s work, especially as


28 Alberto Toscano
concerns biopolitics, have rightly abandoned the dismissals of a theory
of capital stemming from a superficial Foucaultianism (Thacker, 2005;
Rajan, 2006).
 In this chapter, I wish neither to trace the fraught place of Marx
and Marxism in Foucault’s intellectual trajectory nor to engage in a
no doubt forced, meta-theoretical marriage between Marxism and
Foucaultianism as frozen research programs. Rather, I want to turn to
the moment in which Foucault appears ‘closest’ to Marx – the period
of  Discipline and Punish ’s intellectual gestation – to tease out how his
reflections on the relationship between power and capitalism may speak
to our own present. This is a present understood as one in which the
political problem of ‘superfluous populations’ (or more starkly ‘surplus
humanity’) – arguably the point of contact and friction between Marx
and Foucault – is at the centre of any oppositional agenda. Speaking
to leftist Brazilian students in 1973 – no doubt the audience and the
moment were determinant then, too – Foucault proposed a kind of
complementarity between his analytics of power/knowledge and
a Marxian theory of capitalism. I want to dwell on the fecundity of
Foucault’s formulation in these lectures – later published as  ‘Truth and
Juridical Forms’ – but also to reflect on Foucault’s curious omission of
Marx’s own contribution to thinking that crucial biopolitical category,
population.
 It should be noted that had Foucault tackled Marx’s account of
‘superfluous populations’ in the first volume of  Capital , he might have
found himself forced to qualify the sweeping claim in  The Order of
Things , according to which Marx’s critique of political economy was
circumscribed – along with its object and rival – to a nineteenth-cen-
tury  episteme , their controversies amounting to nothing more than
‘storms in a children’s paddling pool’ (2002b: 285). The thesis that
populations were not a naturalizable, external variable or limit in the
accumulation of capital but an immanent one – critical to capital’s
contradictory dynamic but also to its perennial demand for polit-
ical interventions – was not in paradigmatic continuity with either
Malthus or Ricardo, and indeed, it opened up radically different vistas
on the relation between life, labour and history. Now, Foucault, who
had already stressed that in the domain of politics Marx  did embody
a rupture (1996: 21), had seemingly left behind such claims about the
‘closure’ of Marxism by the time he came to explore the emergence
of punitive society. If anything, his account there – perhaps due to
pedagogical shorthand – becomes almost  vulgarly historical-materi-
alist in delineating the function of power over bodies for capitalism


What is Capitalist Power? 29
(and capitalists). In what follows, I want to consider how positing the
centrality of the capitalist mode of production to the strategies and
devices of power allows us to reframe Foucault’s contribution and to
reflect on its relevance to our moment.
 The declarations in  Discipline and Punish about the interrelation
between the accumulation of men and the accumulation of capital are
well known (1991b: 221). So is Foucault’s distinction, within the domain
of biopower, between a biopolitics of populations and an anatomo-poli-
tics of bodies (Foucault, 1978: 139–41). The latter, approached from the
vantage of the shaping and binding of the worker’s body to the produc-
tive apparatus, is evidently the most potent contribution of  Discipline
and Punish to the ongoing attempt to think of the different ways in
which capital, regardless of how much it ‘really subsumes’ labour, does
not have living labour-power in corporeal form ready-made, but it
demands labour’s social and political production – often by political
agencies which capture and internalize this indispensable ‘externality’,
this life which is not always already labouring for capital (Kawashima,
2009). But, as Stéphane Legrand has compellingly argued, in  Discipline
and Punish Foucault’s ‘method of abstraction’ – compounded perhaps
by an allergy to Marxian ‘affiliations’ – seemed to leave undetermined
the articulation and compatibility between the economic and the disci-
plinary and seemed not to present a synthesis of the rationalities of
different forms of power. From this method stems a number of prob-
lems, among them the tendency to give general significance to forms
of power intelligible only within particular economic conjunctures, but
also, and perhaps more problematically, the problem of treating the
relationship between the practices and rationalities of carceral, educa-
tional and industrial forms of discipline in the registers of isomorphy
or analogy.
 In  Discipline and Punish , Foucault writes of surveillance that it is a
‘decisive economic operator’ (1991b: 175)  internal to the apparatus of
production  and part of disciplinary power – this is after all the lesson
of the chapters on the factory and the working day in the first volume
of  Capital . But, for Legrand, this lesson stops short of positing a deter-
mination-in-the-last-instance within an over-determined situation. The
problem arises again in terms of the ‘utility’ of discipline, which differs
radically between different institutions (the ‘uses’ of a docile body in
a school and a factory are  not the same) and their respective ration-
alities, meaning that in order for these to gel an overarching utility is
called upon – which is, surreptitiously, the utility  for capital . Otherwise
‘discipline’ runs the risk of offering a fictional synthesis, a generality


30 Alberto Toscano
(like ‘population’ in Marx’s 1857 introduction to the  Grundrisse ) without
explanatory purchase.
 It should be noted that these critiques of the ‘amphibology’ in  Discipline
and Punish are far less germane to ‘Truth and Juridical Forms’, which
puts the question of capitalism front and centre. For Legrand, a proper
estimation of Foucault’s ‘forgotten’ Marxism would require stepping
back to the Collège de France course on  La Société Punitive (1972–3). For
Legrand, instead of the ‘false synthesis’ of discipline, Foucault’s course
provides an account of the  genetic link between the prison form and the
wage form, in which the shaping of the body and the capture of labour-
 time are crucial. The research program that Foucault sketches and largely
abandons in this course concerns the physical, material and political
conditions for the existence of abstract time. A function of carceral disci-
pline is the ‘introduction of time into the system of capitalist power and
the system of penality’ (Foucault, in Legrand 2004: 34) – a theme that
brings Foucault into contact with the seminal work of E. P. Thompson
(Thompson, 1991). What is at stake is ‘the relation of the time of life
to political power’ (Foucault, in Legrand 2004: 35) – a power over and
by time (which we could also link to the centrality of controlling capi-
talist circulation to the emergence of these disciplinary practices, on
which more in a moment). The objective is ‘to subject [ assujetir ] the
time of human existence to the temporal system of the production of
the capital’ (Foucault, in Legrand 2004: 40).
 In ‘Truth and Juridical Forms’, whose fourth and fifth lectures (my
concern here) overlap with the concurrent course,  The Punitive Society
(Elden, 2013; Rodrigues, 2011), what is perhaps most evident is the
centrality of politics, broadly construed, to the reproduction of capital.
Foucault’s formulations here are dense and suggestive, and they move
beyond the reciprocal implication of the economic-capitalistic and the
disciplinary-political articulated later in  Discipline and Punish , where
we read: ‘it would not have been possible to solve the problem of the
accumulation of men without the growth of an apparatus of production
capable of both sustaining them and using them; conversely, the tech-
niques that made the cumulative multiplicity of men useful accelerated
the accumulation of capital’ (1991b: 221). In the Brazil lectures, Foucault
does not rest content with the potentially misleading homonymy of
‘accumulation’ (a moment’s reflection will suggest that capital and
‘men’ are not accumulated in analogous ways), but he writes of the
imbrication between the microphysics of power and the extraction of
surplus-value:


What is Capitalist Power? 31
 I don’t think we can simply accept the traditional Marxist analysis,
which assumes that, labor being man’s concrete essence, the capi-
talist system is what transforms that labor into profit, into hyper-
profit [ sur-profit ] or surplus value. The fact is, capitalism penetrates
much more deeply into our existence. That system, as it was estab-
lished in the nineteenth century, was obliged to elaborate a set of
political techniques, techniques of power, by which man was tied to
something like labor – a set of techniques by which people’s bodies
and their time would become labor power and labor time so as to
be effectively used and thereby transformed into hyperprofit. But
in order for there to be hyperprofit, there had to be an infrapower
[ sous-pouvoir ] (2002a: 86).
 This is a remarkable passage for several reasons. It challenges a
 humanist-Marxist anthropology of labour, not on speculative or
epistemic grounds but through an immanent Marxian critique. The
wrenching of bodies and populations from subsistence and custom – a
theme, that of so-called primitive accumulation, which could be further
explored via  The History of Madness – and their binding to the produc-
tive apparatus represents a profoundly violent and novel phenomenon,
whose gravity is elided by projecting the productive body back into the
body-in-general and the proletarian back into human nature.
 Foucault’s challenge to his Marxist contemporaries here is far more
serious and lasting than political polemic: they do not take capitalism
seriously enough, nor do they read Marx carefully enough. Yet against
a genealogy of purely political contingency in an irreparably multiple
social field, what we have here are the internal requirements of capi-
talism as a system – or even a totality. Such a totality can come together
only through a myriad of molecular operations and through the inter-
nalization of powers and strategies produced elsewhere (for instance,
among self-governing religious groups). These practices cannot be
simply hived off into a tidy sphere of state or political, as opposed to
economic, power. But, and this is especially crucial for today’s theoret-
ical reflection, capitalism is not here a mere name, bundling together
heterogeneous practices. It is a historically determinate system, with a
logic, imperatives and contradictions (or, in a more Foucaultian lexicon,
problems).
 As we observe the politics internal to the functioning of factory, to
the shaping of the labouring bodies themselves and to the penetra-
tion of capitalist imperatives throughout multifarious institutions,


32 Alberto Toscano
familiar distinctions between politics and economy, including ones
pedantically observed by some Marxists, are redrawn. Foucault further
explains:
 A web of microscopic, capillary political power had to be established
at the level of man’s very existence, attaching men to the produc-
tion apparatus, while making them into agents of production, into
workers. This binding of man to labor was synthetic, political; it was
a linkage brought about by power. There is no hyperprofit without an
infrapower. I speak of ‘infra-power’, for what’s involved is the power
I described earlier, and not the one traditionally called ‘political
power’. I’m referring not to a state apparatus, or to the class in power,
but to the whole set of little powers, of little institutions situated at
the lowest level. What I meant to do was analyze this infrapower as a
condition of possibility of hyperprofit (2002a: 86–7).
 It is thus clear that in ‘Truth and Juridical Forms’, like in  La Société
Punitive lectures, the binding of the body to machines and capital –
the  political synthesis of living labour-power – is not just a general form
common to different institutions; as Legrand notes, it is anchored to the
requirements of the capitalist mode of production (2004). It is perhaps
inevitable that when turning to the production and reproduction of
labouring bodies and populations, Foucault, while rightly stressing the
‘unnatural’ and historically unprecedented character of industrial capi-
talism’s emergence, is also obliged to adopt a systemic, even functionalist
view, in which what determines the persistence of particular techniques
and modalities of knowledge is their utility for capital or indeed for capi-
talists. As late as 1978, in an interview with a Japanese Marxist, Foucault
made the following statement, which nicely captures the tenor of ‘Truth
and Juridical Forms’: ‘reconfirming the functional unity of the forms of
appearance of power, which are connected to Marx’s own statements
[ la parole de Marx lui-même ], strikes me as a worthy endeavor’ (quoted in
Chitty, 2012). Contrary to the ‘historical nominalism’ that so suffuses
much of Foucault’s oeuvre (Balibar, 1992; Flynn, 2005), capitalism is
here an indisputable presence. If Foucault questions its character as the
social totality he does so only to argue that it goes even deeper, through
the hooks and tendrils of discipline, than a dogmatic historical materi-
alist could ever suspect.
 It is not the ubiquity of capital or its determining role in shaping
social power that Foucault here disputes; rather it is a presupposition
common to many Marxists that the mechanisms whereby this power


What is Capitalist Power? 33
is exerted are secondary – matters of ideological legitimation or blunt
physical force which can be ‘read off’ the requirements of accumulation
and not indispensable and constitutive dimensions of what capitalism
 is . This concern is coded here in terms of an emphasis on the indispen-
sability of  politics . We do not just have a ‘social synthesis’ (Sohn-Rethel,
1978), as embodied in the form of commodity exchange and its depend-
ence on the extraction of surplus-value from abstract labour; we have a
 political synthesis, a  binding of bodies to machines, without which the
capital-labour relation is an empty schema. In other words, as scholars
focusing on the violence of continued so-called primitive accumula-
tion have stressed, capital is not its own condition; it is not the subject
of some kind of automatic autopoiesis. Correlatively, inasmuch as the
‘synthesizing’ of living labour-power requires particular configurations
of power/knowledge, these are not merely supplementary of capitalist
relations of production; they are constitutive of them – accordingly
requiring a revision in the very notion of ‘ideology’. It is in this sense
that Foucault sees the juridical form of the ‘examination’, which follows
the feudal ‘inquiry’ as a form of power/knowledge which functions ‘at
the level of capitalist production and hyper-profit’ (2002a: 87).
 But can the tale of the political synthesis of capitalism stop here, at
the rightly feted inquiry into the destructive creation of the labouring
body? There are at least two other dimensions of Foucault’s inquiry
which directly touch on Marxian problematics and must be considered
in order for this synthesis truly to be multi-dimensional: circulation and
populations.
 In ‘Truth and Juridical Forms’ the binding of the working body to
the machine – the primary capitalist function of disciplinary power –
is accompanied by a different problem, to which the excess of penal
power over juridical reason also responds: the need to secure stocks and
capital goods in an economy in which the circulation of material forms
of capital takes on increasing weight. Incidentally, we can note here
that the error ascribing to Foucault an interest in  Volume 2 of  Capital ,
on the basis of comments in ‘The Mesh of Power’, where he is in fact
referring to the second  book in the French edition of volume 1 (Foucault,
2012b; Chitty, 2012; Read, 2012), could be creatively rectified: it is in
fact in  Capital, Volume 2 ’s insistence on the significance of circulation
that perhaps unexpected links could be made to a political economy
of security sketched by Foucault in his Collège de France lectures of the
late 1970s, as well as in the earlier inquiries into the punitive society,
where the  police is seen to emerge precisely to respond to the problem
of capital stocks and proletarian illegality – exemplarily so in Patrick


34 Alberto Toscano
Colquhoun’s seminal 1795 text on policing in London:  Treatise on the
Police of the Metropolis .
 When, in ‘Truth and Juridical Forms’, Foucault reflects on the reason
for the emergence of voluntary societies preoccupied with moral regula-
tion (from the Society for the Reform of Manners to the Society for the
Suppression of Vice), as well as self-defence militias and private police
forces, he perceives them, in part, as a response to an economic transfor-
mation that foregrounded the accumulation of stocks, especially at ports
and warehouses, and the build-up in fixed capital (machines). With the
emergence of a ‘new mode of material investment of wealth’ in which
‘capital was no longer monetary’, the great problem, for state and capi-
talists alike, is how ‘to set up control mechanisms that would make it
possible to protect this new material form of wealth’ (Foucault 2002a:
68–9). This is the moment of the emergence of capitalist power over a
‘field of circulation’, which means ‘not only [the] material network that
allows the circulation of goods and possibly of men, but also the circula-
tion itself, that is to say, the set of regulations, constraints, and limits, or
the facilities and encouragements that will allow the circulation of men
and things in the kingdom and possibly beyond its borders’ (Foucault,
2009: 325–6).
 That Foucault also signals the significance of surging urban and
proletarian resistance, as well as of individuals’ dangerous ‘potentiali-
ties’ in eliciting these agencies of moral power and knowledge, together
with their eventual integration within the penal apparatus, suggests
we should qualify the criticism of  Discipline and Punish which faults
it for accepting ‘the apparent separation between increased wealth
attendant upon greater capital investment and the armed attack
upon the illegalities of the lower strata of society’ (Linebaugh, 2006:
417). What’s more, in ‘Truth and Juridical Forms’ the class coordi-
nates are much starker: the shift in the later eighteenth-century from
diffuse (voluntary) to concentrated, (state or state-like) disciplinary
and control mechanisms is also understood as a moment in which
‘moral control was exerted by the upper classes, the holders of power,
over the lower, poorer strata, the popular strata. It thus became an
instrument of power for the wealthy over the poor, for the exploiting
over the exploited, which conferred a new political and social polarity
on these agencies of control’ (2002a: 63). Coercive power is in many
respects concentrated and centralized into the state, from its more
diffuse origins. The coercive system is the ‘political instrument of
the control and continuation of relations of production’ (Foucault


What is Capitalist Power? 35
in Legrand 2004: 37), and it is innervated by an obsession with the
illegality of the dissipation of living labour-power.
 Here, ‘Truth and Juridical Forms’ resonates with the strong affirma-
tion of class struggle as critical to politics from one of Foucault’s inter-
views, so dissonant with the now customary view whereby his thinking
of power provincialized class struggle and volatilized capitalism: ‘I
label political everything that has to do with class struggle, and social
everything that derives from and is a consequence of the class struggle
expressed in human relationships and institutions. [ ... ] I also want to
give politics the meaning of a struggle for power; but it’s not power
understood only as government or state, but economic power as well’
(1996: 104). Although  Discipline and Punish arguably does not provide
an adequate synthesis of how diverse strategies and devices of control
are composed into an emergent form of capitalist power – combining
the binding of bodies, the securing of stocks and the managing of popu-
lations, in this horizon of class struggle – I think it is possible to recon-
struct this problematization of capitalist power in Foucault’s writings,
lectures and interviews of the 1970s.
 Critical to such a reconstruction of the emergence and crystallization
of capitalist power is an investigation of Foucault’s biopolitical blind
spot, namely, his scanting of the Marxian conceptualization of super-
fluous populations – a fulcrum of debate among social thinkers trying to
think through the manner in which the history of our present is deeply
bound to the disciplining, control and exclusion of a classed, racialized
and gendered surplus, to the managing of ‘wageless life’ (Denning, 2010;
Endnotes and Benanav, 2010; Gilmore, 2006; Toscano, 2014). Such work
has been put on a very promising track by Guillaume Sibertin-Blanc
(2010 and 2009), who has compellingly argued that biopolitical ration-
ality is  under condition – in the sense that its periodization and modalities
cannot be explained internally; they require that biopolitics be thought
in terms of Marx’s tendential law of relative surplus population, as
formulated in  Capital, Volume 1 :
 The same causes which develop the expansive power of capital, also
develop the labour-power at its disposal. The relative mass of the
industrial reserve army thus increases with the potential energy of
wealth. But the greater is the mass of a consolidated surplus popula-
tion, whose misery is in inverse ratio to the amount of torture it has
to undergo in the form of labour. The more extensive, finally, the
Lazarus-layers of the working class, and the industrial reserve army,


36 Alberto Toscano
the greater is official pauperism.  This is the absolute general law of capi-
talist accumulation (Marx, 2011: 707).
 The problems of binding body to machine, of securing stocks against
theft, of dampening down the menace of insurrection and of fostering
the circulation and accumulation (but also the devaluation) of humans
and things – none of these are thinkable without the profoundly contra-
dictory engine of social change which Marx encapsulates in this law, a
law that requires both the absolute increase in the labouring population
and the augmenting of its surplus portion, itself ‘inclusively excluded’
in different modalities and at different speeds.
 By contrast with the conciliatory, ‘progressive’ image of the dialectic
that Foucault insistently repudiated, this conception of surplus popula-
tions is intrinsically contradictory and refractory to closure – the kind of
contradiction which recurrently demands the aforementioned political
syntheses, which provide a fractious mobile ground for the mutable
and ever-incomplete strategies of capitalist power. The dependency of
capital on a living labour-power already involves two crucial contra-
dictions (Sibertin-Blanc, 2010: 2–3) that make intelligible many of the
‘solutions’ of disciplinary and later control societies. First, the contra-
diction between mobility (of stocks, money-flows and living labour)
and fixity (of workers and fixed capital), which defines so much of the
spatial dynamic of capital. From the factory to the docks, it is in light
of this contradiction that the mutations of ‘the police’ can be tracked
and understood. We could go even further and see in this contradiction
a neglected rationale for the partition of biopower into a biopolitics of
populations (preoccupied with securing movement and flows) and an
anatomo-politics of bodies (driven by the need to attach the worker to
the productive apparatus). The second contradiction, repeatedly indi-
cated by Foucault, involves the strategic imperative of capitalist power:
to maximize the forces of the body in their economic utility and to
minimize their capacity for possible resistance, be it individual or collec-
tive. We can infer from here a deeper contradiction or rather antago-
nism: the insurgencies, acts of sabotage, resistances and class struggles
that shadow and elicit the responses of capitalist power.
 But we can also move from here to the contradictions  internal to a
divided proletariat. The emphasis on the political synthesis required
for the binding of bodies to capital also suggests that Foucault can be
employed, alongside Marx’s theorization of populations, to offset an
idea of the proletariat as a kind of homogeneously voided subject. The
oppressive layering of the superfluous populations, the negative horizon


What is Capitalist Power? 37
of an absolute surplus population and the painstaking, legal and physical
work of separating proletarians from one another (only to bind them in
assemblages of productive impotence) all speak against a presupposi-
tion of univocal proletarian subjectivity. One of the dimensions of this
dis-homogeneity is underscored by Sibertin-Blanc and involves the
interiorization of state power by the working class, as it perceives and
reproduces its own difference from its accursed share: the superfluous,
the lumpen (Sibertin-Blanc, 2009: 94–100; Kawashima, 2009). Structural
antagonisms can thus be ‘incorporated outside’ the direct capital-labour
relation; this is not a secondary feature of the relationship between
capitalist and proletarians but a constitutive condition. As Foucault
remarked in a 1972 roundtable, ‘the divide is continuously reproduced
and refashioned between the working class and the nonworking world,
since it was thought that their contact could potentially be the catalyst
of riots’ (Foucault, 1999: 95). There is an  internal heteronomy in the class
struggle, an internal border of the working class, which we can see at
work in writing that takes the question of surplus populations as central
to the mediations of ‘race’, gender and class (Toscano, 2014).
 The population of what Marx called virtual paupers is both an
economic and a political (which is also to say a legal and punitive)
problem, one that largely conditions the development of the complex,
and in some respects contradictory, apparatus of biopower. Accordingly,
if we take the production and reproduction of the disposition to produce
as crucial to the emergence of an intrinsically capitalist punitive society,
then we must also link this emergence to the question of superfluous or
surplus populations as the macro-problem of an emergent capitalism.
The reproduction of the capital-labour relations both presupposes and
repeats the forcible  separation of people from means of subsistence (a
primitive accumulation of labouring bodies whose wandering and flight
must be curtailed and channelled into the factory or into the appropriate
institutions of non-work). The accumulation of men of which Foucault
wrote in  Discipline and Punish should thus be linked to the ‘primitive
accumulation of superfluous populations’ (Sibertin-Blanc, 2009: 87).
 This goes some way to explaining why, according to Sibertin-Blanc,
the development of the capital-relation has a strong  counter-biopolitical
dynamic (2010: 4), constantly shadowed by difficulties in reproduc-
tion. These difficulties are both immanent to the logic of capital – as
evidenced by the ‘general law of capital accumulation’ – and unpredict-
ably conjunctural and subjective – as in workers’ revolt and resistance.
But the point stands: without thinking through the Marxian ques-
tion of superfluous populations, the crystallization of biopower in the


38 Alberto Toscano
nineteenth century remains a historical mystery or an effect of political
voluntarism: ‘biopolitical rationality was only able to condition, as
Foucault explains, the development of capitalism, under the constraint
of the law of relative over-population, or under its condition’ (2010:
7). Biopolitics, as Sibertin-Blanc concludes, is  under condition of capital.
It is only through this lens, which goes beyond archivally mappable
arrangements of power and knowledge, that we can include within a
discussion of biopolitics the ‘negative planning’ of famines and other
artificial, populational catastrophes within capitalist (and non-capitalist)
contexts (2010: 8). Biopolitical rationality cannot be hived off from the
contradictory dynamics of capital, as encapsulated in Marx’s ‘law’ of
populations; nor, for the same reason, can it be tidily framed by a perio-
dizing perspective, in which one could speak of an age of biopolitics or
of the epochal passage from sovereignty to biopower or from discipline
to control. This is not to say that we cannot map tendencies, as Sibertin-
Blanc does in thinking together Marx’s account of superfluous popula-
tions and Foucaultian biopolitics, discerning a secular mutation in the
economy of class violence, with the transformation by  incorporation and
 displacement of direct political violence into law and social relations of
production (2009: 93).
 The fact that a theory of capital is indispensable to a cartography of
capitalist power is explicit in Foucault’s avowal that the relations of force
explored in  History of Madness and  Discipline and Punish alike take their
cue from ‘those economic and demographic processes which appear
clearly at the end of the 16 th century, when the problem of the poor, of
the homeless, of fluctuating populations, is posed as an economic and
political problem; and an attempt is made to resolve it with an entire
arsenal of implements and arms’ (Foucault, 1996: 259). But it is even
more evident perhaps in ‘Truth and Juridical Forms’ when, after wryly
noting that the panopticon embodies a capitalist utopia of the kind
which differs from socialist ones in having ‘the unfortunate tendency to
be realized’ (2002a: 75), Foucault also specifies that its realization is not
so much a matter of implementing the model as a matter of its diffu-
sion, in often imperceptible and molecular forms. The catalyst for this
implosion and pollination or fractalization (De Angelis, 2006) of the
panoptical utopia is  crisis . Foucault speaks of the realized utopian spaces
of Benthamite capitalism:
 The economic cost of these institutions immediately proved too
heavy, and the rigid structure of these prison factories soon caused
many of them to collapse. Ultimately, they all disappeared. Indeed, as


What is Capitalist Power? 39
soon as there was a production crisis and it was necessary to discharge
a certain number of workers, to readjust production, as soon as the
rhythm of production accelerated, those enormous firms, with a
fixed number of workers and equipment set up on a permanent basis,
revealed themselves to be utterly un-serviceable (2002a: 76).
 The above is more proof, if proof were needed, of Sibertin-Blanc’s thesis,
according to which biopolitics is under condition of capital.
 Building on the arguments advanced by Legrand and Sibertin-Blanc,
I have been arguing that it is possible to move beyond a stale contest
between Foucault and Marxism to a reconstruction of a theory of capi-
talist power – of the shifting articulation of sub-power and surplus-
value – that would put the political production and reproduction
of living labour-power (of bodies and populations) at its centre. The
‘controlled insertion of bodies into the machinery of production and
the adjustment of phenomena of population to economic processes’
(Foucault, 1978: 141), or biopower for short, was certainly indispensable
to the full emergence and reproduction of capitalism. But, as Foucault
suggested in ‘Truth and Juridical Forms’, it was the imperatives of accu-
mulation and the problems of capitalist control that called forth the
strategies and techniques covered by biopower. While Foucault’s atten-
tion to the ‘political synthesis’ of labouring proletarian bodies is crucial,
we must also remember the kind of macro, tendential processes that
Marx himself captured under the ‘general law of capital accumulation’.
These did and continue to provide the  conditions for particular strategies
and tactics of capitalist power.
 Capitalism involves the orthopraxy, the  dressage , of proletarian
bodies. But it also bodies forth the systematic and largely impersonal
production of surplus populations. Attention to the latter also allows us
to confront the discrepancy between the labour-time of (ideally) docile
bodies and a temporality of crisis that is often violently  indifferent to the
time(s) of the body or of social reproduction. Foucault’s attention to the
reproduction of productive bodies should thus be accompanied by an
attention to what we could call the alien physiology of capital – in other
words, to linking Foucault’s suggestive explorations into the genesis and
reproduction of  capitalism in its heartlands, with an attention to the
logic of  capital . Ironically, it is at this higher level of abstraction that – as
recent efforts to link the social form of value to the problem of ‘surplus
humanity’ detail (Endnotes and Benanav, 2010) – a more concrete inter-
vention into the history and ontology of our present can take root. In
the end, beyond Foucault’s (anti-Marxist) polemics, we should attend


40 Alberto Toscano
to Foucault’s (Marxian) problematizations, especially as they point to
critical conceptual and practical problems, ones that make plain the
intractably contradictory character of the bourgeois mode of produc-
tion: What is the role of politics in economic coercion? How do we link
the history and genealogy of capitalism to the logic of capital? What is
the relationship between proletarian subjectivity and the stratification
of working classes? What patterns and causalities can be discerned in
the making and unmaking of working bodies and populations? How can
bodies be bound, circulation be secured and populations be controlled?
Or: What is capitalist power?
