Part III
Troubling Subjectivities


131
 The starting point for this chapter is my astonishment concerning
Foucault’s analysis of what he terms pastoral power (Foucault, 2001;
Foucault, 2007). Defining this as a ‘major’ form of power, Foucault,
up to the point when he tackles specifically the question of Christian
pastorate, always analyses the pastoral exclusively in terms of the pastor.
It is a question of his action and the motives and principles that guide
this action. The pastoral is defined from the point of view of the ‘ethics’
in which this exercise of power is rooted and also from the point of view
of the pastor’s subjectivity or of the modes of subjectivation which are
typical for the kind of power he makes use of. Foucault  never takes the
pastorate into account from the point of view of the herd, of the flock
itself. The herd, the sheep, are constantly and exclusively considered as
the sheer object of pastoral conduct, that is, of the pastor’s care. This
means that the animal image acquires a fairly literal sense here. The
herd does not speak; the flock (considered as an entity to be led) lacks
any ability to ‘interact’ with the designs and actions of the pastor; and
this for the simple and good reason that its condition is purely animal.
It continues to be defined by its animality or animal nature. Everything
happens as if, in the original form of pastoral power, the humans are
ruled (or rather, led),  as if they were animals [ à l’égal d’animaux ], since
no form of subjectivity, no kind of access to language and no ability
to adopt counter-behaviours [ contre-conduites ] manifests itself from the
side of the ‘herd’. One might even wonder if, for Foucault, it would
be appropriate in this context to allude to relations between rulers and
‘ruled’ (people). The flock is led more than it is ruled. For Foucault, the
relation between rulers and ‘ruled’ involves the presupposition of some
form of interaction and something like a circulation of the discharge
of power which consists in trying to ‘let do/make something happen’
8
 Human Pastorate and ‘La Vie Bête’
 Alain  Brossat


132 Alain Brossat
[‘ faire faire ’]. In other words, actions and behaviours by those in ‘power’
are responded to by the development of counter-behaviours, resistances,
talks and actions, which represent the reverse angle shot on power
belonging to the ‘ruled’.
 There is nothing of that here. It is useful to recall briefly the descrip-
tion of pastoral power by Foucault in terms of both its basis and prin-
ciples. At origin, the pastorate is a relationship between God and the
humans. It constitutes an ancient structure found among the Assyrians,
the Egyptians and the Hebrews rather than the Greeks. In these civiliza-
tions, according to Foucault’s reading, God’s relation to his people is
(defined as) similar to the relation a pastor has to his herd (Foucault,
2001: 956). Via a process of transposition, this pastoral structure of
the relationship between God and humans becomes a model of power
for the relationship between a shepherd as guide and the humans as a
people or population He is guiding.
 Foucault insists on the common traits of this form of power: it is
neither repressive nor authoritarian: the shepherd does not reign, as a
Greek king does, over a city or a territory. He leads; he guides the human
herd; he keeps a watchful eye over it; he takes care that it does not get
lost or perish. The pastoral is thus a form of power that aims at care: the
shepherd does not reign over the herd ‘for himself’ but, on the contrary,
cares exclusively ‘for the others’, taking them into consideration not
only as a whole, but also as individuals, but also as individuals each
requiring care . All of his care is devoted to the herd, but, according to
the well-known parable in Exodus 10:11, the shepherd also has to make
sure that  none of the sheep composing the herd gets lost or sick, to the
extent that he would have to abandon the herd for a time in order to
track down a single lost sheep.
 The work Foucault dedicates to this original form of the pastorate
aims at describing what might be termed the shepherd’s office. The
herd does not even exist by itself: it ‘exists through the immediate pres-
ence and the direct action of the shepherd’ (Foucault, 2001: 957). In
‘Omnes et Singulatim’, as well as in  Security, Territory, Population , all
the descriptions made by Foucault of this form of power in its original
form are exclusively centred on the figure of the pastor. At stake here
is the way in which he gathers (together), directs the herd, takes care
of their safety and well-being, and treats them in a well-balanced and
personally benevolent way. It is for these very reasons that he is quali-
fied as knowing the herd both  as a whole and  in detail . Foucault also
insists on the newness of this form (of power): ‘The societies which
have appeared at the end of Ancient times on the Western side of the


Human Pastorate and ‘La Vie Bête’ 133
European continent have invented a great number of different political
forms. [ ... ] But only they have developed a strange technology of power,
treating the huge majority of the humans as a herd, with a handful of
pastors’ (Foucault, 2001: 958).
 Thus, it is worth noting two sources of astonishment in my reading
of Foucault here.
 First, in both texts, and to some extent in opposition to common
sense, Foucault stresses the specificity of pastoral power – its ‘individual-
izing’ [ individualisant ] character – a power which is  individually benevo-
lent , a power which treats each individual benevolently. All the more
surprising then is the complete lack of any consideration for what the
‘individual sheep’ might be or, indeed, is in terms of singular individ-
uals. How is the ‘care’, the benevolence and the ‘devotion’ mentioned
by Foucault manifested if there is no exchange or interaction occurring
between the shepherd and each individual being cared for? How can
such a relationship be established between, let’s say, the lost sheep, the ill
sheep and the intractable sheep, if they remain short of language? Is the
individualizing ‘game’ of the pastor limited to the simple task of identi-
fying which sheep have ‘a problem’ based on physical characteristics or
their erratic course? How does the pastor begin to take responsibility for
this problem if there is nothing to the order of a personal relationship
established between himself and such ‘sheep’? In this respect, Foucault’s
text appears to offer us no clues here.
 My second cause for astonishment concerns the biblical reference. One
simply has to open the Old Testament, frequently referred to by Foucault
in these texts, in order to notice that the human ‘herd’ there does, in
fact, constitute a real ‘presence’, demonstrating the ability to manifest
itself, defining itself as more than the pure and simple object of a pastor’s
care. Indeed, in referring to a book such as Exodus one will notice that
this text deals first and foremost with the way that a vertical relation
is established between the Hebrews, as a people, and their God, with
Moses being ascribed to the hazardous and imposing role of go-between
and representative of God’s power (‘the Eternal’) by his people. So, the
‘pastoral structure’ mentioned by Foucault can be easily detected in this
text. The Hebraic God (actually) conducts his human herd through the
desert; he is (actually) a real presence, addressing himself to his flock;
he guides, he shows, he reassures and he exhorts. But, contrary to what
is suggested in Foucault’s analysis, the flock is also a  people : it reveals,
for better or the worse, a collective mood; it makes its very own moves
which often contravene the injunctions of God, as delivered to them by
Moses. These are people who often speak in order to express their sense


134 Alain Brossat
of discouragement, anger, impatience, frustrations, people who question
the ‘instructions’ delivered by Moses, people who doubt, who err, who go
astray all along this initiatory path, the flight from Egypt. Here, one only
needs to look at the episode of the Golden Calf. (Exodus, 31:32)
 So, it appears here that the pastoral form, in its original form, is not
at all incompatible with the presence of a herd that can be defined not
only as a collective,  living group but as an  articulate one too – that is,
as possessing their own unique subjective capacities. As long as they
are slaves of Pharaoh, the Hebrews remain a group living in darkness.
Yet prior to sealing their alliance with God, an alliance that will grant
subsequent access to their very own spirit and genius, prior to arriving
at this point, their condition cannot be reduced to some kind of mute
and stupid animality. Here, we have a people who are mistreated and
often lost but these are a human people, nevertheless. And their exist-
ence as such cannot be reduced to what Moses’ mission assigns to them:
to become God’s only people/to become God’s chosen people [ le Peuple
de Dieu ].
 In ‘Omnes et Singulatim’, Foucault’s argument is bolstered by a
detailed analysis of Plato’s dialogue,  The Statesman, in order to show that
the pastoral form of power is essentially alien to the way of thinking
of the Ancient Greeks. Foucault reminds us that in this dialogue Plato
pays particular attention to the model of the statesman as weaver and
not as shepherd. He thus reminds us how Plato argues that one cannot
give a satisfactory definition of the form of power exerted within the
 polis by opposing the conduct of animals to that of humans, by taking
the species (the kinds of living) that require leadership as a criterion. For
Plato, as Foucault insists, it is by analysing  what the shepherd does , and
not by examining which kind of living species composes the herd or is
supposed to compose it, that one is able to decide whether or not the
king constitutes some kind of a pastor. The emphasis placed on this point
sheds some light on the ‘miss/lack/absence/omission’ of the question of
subjective existence and the ‘moods’ of the herd, human or animal, by
Foucault. This distinction appears to be meaningless since the analysis
of the pastoral figure in its original form is completely focused upon the
role of the shepherd, presented as if this form of power were exerted in
an entirely asymmetrical way, establishing a one-sided relation between
a subject and (living) object.
 Clearly, this figure appears to be some kind of exception or deroga-
tion to the general conditions of the analytics of power that is usually
presented by Foucault, as exemplified in  The Will to Knowledge . Here,
Foucault emphasizes the freedom of the ‘ruled’ and their ability to catch


Human Pastorate and ‘La Vie Bête’ 135
power at its own game by producing different forms of counter-powers.
Yet in the texts that constitute the focus of this chapter, no answer is
given to this issue.
 In  The Statesman , pastoral power is defined, Foucault reminds us, as
a form of power that consists in a total, a full taking charge of the care
of the herd by the shepherd. He cares for its subsistence, its health, its
breeding and even its recreation. This seems to constitute a kind of  total
biopolitics before its emergence. Nevertheless, Plato insists, as is well-
known, that in the Greek  polis all these functions are not entrusted to
one man only (regardless of appellation – statesman, king, leader, and
so on) but to various subjects – the physician, the baker, the farmer,
the musician, etc. The task of the statesman [ l’homme politique ] which
differs from that of the pastor, is not to take charge of all these ‘mainte-
nance’ tasks but to bring together different characters and constitutions
in order to compile the living ‘into a  community based on concord and
friendship’. It is from this statement that the image of weaving cloth
made from different materials is derived. Neither God nor shepherd,
the statesman is entrusted with the task to secure the  unity of the polis , a
task, one might add, that is already enough trouble for him [ qui suffit à
sa peine ] (311a; 311b).
 But the interesting thing here is that, for Foucault, a ‘total and full’
pastorate, that is, the practice of power based on the global and multi-
polar care for all dimensions of the herd’s life, does not seem to require
more than the superior skill of a wise shepherd – something like a God,
he says; and this forecloses the form of the City since the latter is peopled
and led by humans exclusively. Even when he takes into consideration
(before subsequently rejecting) the hypothesis that the pastorate would
have been transplanted from Eastern topographies to European ones
and to the political space  par excellence – the  polis – Foucault never poses
the question of the subjective condition of the herd. But how does one
begin to imagine such a multilateral government of the human herd if
this herd is simply considered as mute animal, no matter how well or
ill it is treated?
 So, in Foucault’s analysis, everything happens as if the mute herd,
deprived of all subjectivity, were considered not exactly as ‘bare life’
but as ‘vie bête’ or ‘stupid life’ and were destined to be transfigured in
quality, to undergo complete transformation through some kind of
 coup de théâtre : a turn of events which are difficult to account for and
that, in my opinion, occur with the capture of the great pastoral form
by Christianity and its reorganization within it. In Foucault’s analysis,
everything happens, as if a subjectivity, an access to language, were


136 Alain Brossat
granted to the herd, with these becoming its attributes once the magic of
the Christian pastoral [ la pastorale ] had transfigured this form of power
coming from time immemorial, from remote Eastern civilization.
 The paradox of this metamorphosis is unquestionable. If we read
Foucault carefully, we notice that the sheep finds access to speech,
becoming a subject by the very condition of being involved in a
‘relation based on total and individual dependence’, where a ‘link of
personal submission’ is constituted between itself and its spiritual guide
(Foucault, 2001: 964) Moreover, the forms of individualization set up
by the Christian pastorate imply that the shepherd (the priest) not only
guides and supervises exterior forms of behaviour but intrudes on the
space of subjectivities – he must know what happens in the soul of
each member of the herd. Surely, this can be established only on the
condition that the subjectivities are taken into account (desire, feelings,
moods, thoughts, etc.) and that narratives emanate from and are prof-
fered by the ‘ruled’ subjects.
 The pivotal point of this shift can be located in the dual technique
installed by the Christian Church of soul searching and soul supervision
[ direction de conscience ] and also its entry point, confession. Confession
is located at the centre of a general device of subjectivation, of a ‘let
speak’ device [ un dispositif de prise de parole ] which implies that the
herd is now emerging as a fully-fledged actor in the great perform-
ance of power. With this unique device, unprecedented in the history
of human civilizations, at once imposing and terrifying, the herd not
only gains access to discourse and various forms of subjectivation, but,
also Foucault suggests, enters a ‘game’ that transforms it into the second
pole of the machinery of power, by now firmly established in its own
right [ à part entière ]. ‘[A] strange game, whose elements are life, death,
truth, obedience, individuals, identity’ (Foucault, 2001: 966, my transla-
tion). A strange combination thus emerges, a combination of the basic
requirements of the pastorate (the herd has to be  led , it is intended, by
nature, for it to be led, it has to obey and be docile) on the one hand
and, on the other, the singular dynamics that are at work within the
Christian pastorate (the building up, in the relation between the one
who leads and the other who is led, of conditions both of interaction
and reflexivity).
 A ‘grey zone’ thus appears between what tends to drive back the sheep
to the original and structural conditions of the pastorate (the bluntly
asymmetric character of the relation between a divine or human guide
and a ‘guided’ entity – whether human or animal) and the access of the
herd to a form of reflexivity, notably moral, that enables it to enact all


Human Pastorate and ‘La Vie Bête’ 137
kinds of gestures of resistance, if not escape moves out of the circle of
the pastorate.
 Foucault’s claim here is that this element of  indiscernibility between
these two poles actually represents the specificity of what he calls
‘modern powers’ in the West. This indiscernibility brings these forms
of power to manifest themselves both in the guise of monstrous under-
takings that push the herd into the abyss of all kinds of mass destruc-
tion and disaster and in the form of the promotion of a subject which,
having access to this condition of reflexivity or self-awareness has the
capacity to escape from its immemorial heteronomy. At this point there
emerges, of course, the possibility of counter-behaviours, acts of resist-
ance, insurrection, and so on.
 Indeed, it is precisely this uncertainty or indetermination that
constitutes the defining characteristic of contemporary biopolitics.
Contemporary biopolitics appears in close proximity to the model
that Plato turns down in  The Statesman in favour of that of the royal
weaver, since the biopolitical actually consists in taking care of abso-
lutely all aspects of the life or lives of the human herd via the figure of
the pastor with a thousand faces, acting in a coordinated way (a kind of
demiurge within society and state). Obviously, in present-day society,
taking charge of life is the business not so much of various specialized
bodies, each acting without regard for others but rather of a general
mechanism which is at once fully integrated and differentiated. And, as
Foucault makes clear in  The Will to Knowledge , it is this form of  biopolitics
or  biopower which has simultaneously stripped back [ refoulé ] and over-
determined [ surdéterminé ] traditional forms of power relations related to
classical sovereignty or to the ideal of community based on a compact
axiology shared by all its members.
 So,  more than ever , the relationship between the rulers and the ‘ruled’
appears to be connected to the great pastoral form, and it is not without
reason that the questions of health and safety and security (those which
deal with the immunization and the maintenance of bodies) appear
to be the major object of the contemporary government of the living.
Moreover, this is notably to the detriment of the classical forms of polit-
ical life which are related to the shaping and the institutionalization
of conflict. Today’s proliferation of medical and police imagery within
the vocabulary of government and power provides a further clue to this
factor of over-determination of all political devices and rationalities or
power apparatuses by the pastoral form as adapted to the conditions of
late modernity.


138 Alain Brossat
 However, at the very same time, the paradox of this contemporary
pastorate is as follows: the more the process of globalization and homog-
enization continues unimpeded, the more it appears to ‘leak’ from the
other end. As the pastoral dimension, that is folded into the govern-
ment of the living, becomes increasingly accentuated by this govern-
ment, a series of aporias emerge most notably when it comes up against
the logic of the market. Immunization of the living and market freedom
are unhappy bedfellows.
 If we are to remain true to Foucault’s hypothesis, the reason for this
paradox is the ‘arrival’ of Christianity which has left an indelible imprint
on the constitution of the subject in its relation to power and, more
precisely, on the way it comes to terms with power relations. In the
‘mechanism’ of confession, there is both a relation of obedience estab-
lished and the constitution of a subject with its ‘own interior space’, and
these are inseparable; submission and the learning of freedom are here
indiscernible. In the same way, in contemporary biopolitical spaces, an
enigmatic circle takes form: the more the global pastorate widens its
scope and improves and varies its techniques, the more the ‘objectifi-
cation’ procedures of the herd and its behaviours are multiplied; the
more numerous and differentiated the messages which are addressed
to it become, the greater the proliferation, on the other hand, of the
possibilities for reacting and interacting, for setting up discursive spaces
in which pastoral conduct will be taken up, if one may say so, from a
reverse angle. Whereas soul searching and confession imply that a space
is opened up to the discursive abilities of the sheep, in the same way,
in contemporary biopolitics, disciplinary ‘normalization’ is not enough:
increasingly, a good biopolitical government appeals to the subject’s
judgement, to its responsibility, to its ability to make plans for the future,
and so on. Today’s government appeals less to discipline than to safety
and security techniques or mechanisms, seeking public assent for these.
This means that vanishing lines or ‘lines of flight’ [ lignes de fuite ] out of
the original conditions of pastoral power appear again and again. Each
time such a space looms on the horizon in which those who are ‘led’ are
promoted (if not, properly speaking  instituted ) as subjects, when their
discursive abilities are called upon, instead of consenting and assenting,
they balk; instead of ‘understanding’ what is ‘explained’ to them, they
reason by themselves; instead of walking as a herd, they go wander off;
and, even if they do not resist openly, they become, simply by doing
this,  ungovernable .
 From this point of view, it is easy to observe how the ‘return’ of poli-
tics in its more classical forms – that is, division openly exhibited in


Human Pastorate and ‘La Vie Bête’ 139
the market place –  at the very heart of biopolitical forms is a common
phenomenon in our societies. It was not so long ago that the South
Korean population, after having elected a committed conservative as
president, demonstrated in the streets by the dozens of thousands when
this man decided to permit the importation of beef from the US again
despite repeated warnings from relevant health authorities. The classic
form of conflict between those at the top and those below returns again
and again in contemporary democracies and is located at the heart of
the biopolitical. The public fury in South Korea over the failure of the
authorities to respond adequately during and after the ferry disaster of
April 2014 demonstrates this further.
 So far my intention has been to counter all kinds of extremely pessi-
mistic discourses that foretell the ‘agony of politics’ every morning for
the benefit of the proliferation of technologies meant for the implemen-
tation of ‘taming’ and domestication, or even enslavement of the human
herd. However, having mentioned all this, I would like to propose one
final twist. This requires an awareness that the way in which Foucault
defines what one might term the ‘basic pastorate’, as brutal, crude and
oversimplifying as it initially appears, by presenting a straightforward
asymmetrical relation between a pastor, who is neither king not sover-
eign but who is certainly the exclusive depositary of the understanding
of the power relation established between the shepherd and his flock,
draws our attention to this absolute disparity between shepherd and
flock. This is a disparity and difference which can be found in any kind
of pastoral form or pastoral power regardless of the level of ‘sophistica-
tion’ with which such power is organized and not as a trace or a remnant
but, actually, as a structural and structuring element. From this point of
view, it is essential to remember that, contrary to what Plato claims, the
issue of the question of heterogeneity between the human condition
and that of the animal, perceived in terms of a notion of quality or lack
of quality, remains permanent and primordial.
 In all kinds of pastorates, ancient, modern or contemporary, genuine
human qualities are assigned entirely to the guide. Moreover, the reason
why it does not matter much if the ‘ruled’ element is human or animal
now becomes clear. Something essential within the pastoral relation
drives the herd back to animality, to ‘la vie bête’ (which is, I stress again,
something quite different from ‘bare life’ as articulated by Arendt and,
after her, Agamben). 1
 This ‘something’, one can name in different ways, the simplest and
the most illuminating doubtless consists in recalling that the pastoral
form excludes completely any principle or procedure tending to the


140 Alain Brossat
equalization of rulers and ruled; and this for the good reason that
the knowledge about conduct persists in being, in any circumstance,
completely heterogeneous to the kind of knowledge(s) that the rulers
and pastors expect from the ‘ruled’, so that the latter can be led or, better,
become  leadable [ guidable ]. In the corrupt language of contemporary
‘governance’, this radical difference in status between the alleged intel-
ligence or expertise of the ones who guide and the ‘ruled’ entity mani-
fests itself in the high frequency of  pedagogic images within the ruling
authority’s discourse. The human herd is a reluctant and absentminded
class – in the school(-life) sense of the term – to whom he, the ruler as
teacher, has to explain again and again, in simplified terms, what it has
to know so that the herd continues to be  leadable . It is beyond any ques-
tion that the knowledge of those who are led be deemed equal to that of
the pastors and even if such a thing could be imagined, it would obvi-
ously not be desirable according to the terms of the ‘social police’. The
‘political’ pedagogy required for the wise conduct of the herd consists
in making available – for each and every member of the flock – only the
amount of knowledge necessary for its own conduct.
 Once again here, the imagery borrowed from medicine is useful. In
the same way that the physician explains to the patient only that which
is absolutely necessary in order that the patient follows the treatment
that has been prescribed to him, the statesman endeavours to dissemi-
nate among the public only ‘useful’ and simplified messages, messages
concerned with reassurance rather than truth. We only need consider
the financial crisis of recent years in this respect.
 The reason for these simplified messages is that the faculty of judge-
ment of the ‘ruled’ to which the rulers appeal persists in being that of
 intelligent animals . These intelligent animals are what we, conversely,
call ‘la vie bête’ – that is, basically, life which can take shape and last
under only the conditions of the pastorate, provided that it is led. In this
sense, what at the beginning of this chapter was presented as some kind
of a blunder by Foucault actually constitutes one of the blinding effects
of his art of philosophizing with a hammer, his ability to articulate, as
he often does, suggestions which are, properly speaking, astounding.
In the case of his accounts of pastoral power, this is a proposition that
consists of reminding us bluntly that the stakes of modern biopoli-
tics are to impose, over the long term, a total and basic indistinction
between animal and human herd. This distinction is something we have
long suspected. Indeed, excellent scholars have taken steps to convince
us that the animals, since they are also living, must too have the same
‘right to have rights’ which  we have. From the moment the axis of any


Human Pastorate and ‘La Vie Bête’ 141
kind of politics is the defence and the promotion of life, the hidden
trend of the modern pastorate surges up again. Yet, here, it is only the
humanity of the pastors that is guaranteed. As for the herd, this remains,
after all,  living form and matter , the qualification of which remains up for
discussion.
 Note
 1 .  See most notably, Arendt, 1958: 286; Agamben 1998: 1
 References
 Agamben, G. (1998)  Homo Sacer: Sovereign Power and Bare Life . Stanford, CA:
Stanford University Press.
 Arendt, H. (1958)  The Origins of Totalitarianism . Cleveland, OH: The World
Publishing Company.
 Foucault, M. (1990)  The Will to Knowledge: The History of Sexuality, Vol. 1 . London:
Penguin.
 Foucault, M. (2001) ‘“Omnes et singulatim”: vers une critique de la raison poli-
tique’, in  Dits et Ecrits II, 1976–1988 . Paris: Gallimard, pp. 953–80.
 Foucault, M. (2007)  Security, Territory, Population: Lectures at the Collège de France
1977–1978 , Senellart, M. (ed.), Basingstoke: Palgrave Macmillan.
 Plato (1995)  The Statesman , Annas, J. and Waterfield, R. (eds) Cambridge, UK:
Cambridge University Press.


142
 Introduction: Books versus cigarettes
 On 2 November 1971, René Pleven, the French Minister of Justice,
announced that families would no longer be allowed to send Christmas
food packages to inmates. This was seen as a particularly oppressive
and callous response to a spate of protests inside French prisons which
had culminated in the death of two hostages at Clairvaux prison in
September of that year. 1 Newspapers and magazines were also banned
in some prisons in order to curtail the circulation of various left-wing
journals.
 In November 2013, Chris Grayling, UK Secretary of State for Justice
and Tory Party MP, announced that those serving prison sentences in
the UK would no longer be entitled to receive packages from the outside.
Again, this announcement was timed to be right before Christmas.
Inmates were prevented from receiving books from friends, family or
even libraries and charities. They were also banned from receiving gifts
and handmade cards from their children. As with the Christmas food
packages in France, one of the reasons given here was the risk of smug-
gling contraband into prisons.
 In both cases, public intellectuals and writers vehemently denounced
the bans. Yet, the authors who have spoken out against Grayling’s recent
legislation have tended to take the gesture at face value, reading it as
a symbolic act on the part of Grayling, an act of zero tolerance which
fails to take into account the role of literacy and reading in rehabili-
tating offenders. However, if we identify this practice of banning pack-
ages and, more specifically, books from prison as an age-old technique
9
 Beyond Slogans and Snapshots: The
Story of the  Groupe d’Information
sur les Prisons
 Sophie  Fuggle


Beyond Slogans and Snapshots 143
associated with the management and control of inmates, we should
resist the knee-jerk reaction of condemning such a technique as archaic
and draconian. Instead, pursuing a similar line of enquiry as Angela
Davis in her critique of US prison reforms, 2 we should ask, what is really
at stake in Grayling’s ban? It may well be that this ban does not stem
from a failure to appreciate the value of reading or the importance
of maintaining contact with friends and family on the outside. Nor,
perhaps, is this simply about issuing a warning to would-be criminals
about the harsh deprivations they will face in prison. How might such
legislation form part of a serious modification to the myth of rehabili-
tation long maintained as part of UK discourses of incarceration and
detention?
 The role and function of incarceration in countries such as France
and the UK is shifting. Such shifts are mostly imperceptible, and when
the media does pick up on changes to legislation or new measures such
as the ban on smoking, much of the response produced by the intellec-
tual Left tends to provide a smokescreen rather than a serious analysis
and confrontation to such measures and their underlying discourses.
Grayling’s book ban, when linked to the workfare programs endorsed
inside and outside prison by his party, raises wider questions about
the role of education in an increasingly unequal system. Who should
have access to certain forms of education? Who, by contrast, should be
restricted to training and apprenticeships? How does one ensure those
unable, unwilling or unqualified to work are channelled back into the
labour market or, as in the US, safely warehoused for extensive and
indefinite periods of time?
 This chapter takes these questions as its starting point along with
the failure of intellectuals and radical and mainstream media, together
with the public, to fully engage with the stakes and implications of
incarceration in late capitalist society on anything beyond a superficial
level. In calling for different questions and voices here, I propose that
the work and legacy of the  Groupe d’Information sur les Prisons (GIP)
embodies a moment where the conditions and function of the prison
was, albeit briefly, seriously challenged and problematized. On the one
hand, inmates and their families were given a voice previously denied
to them. On the other hand, this was also a moment in which intel-
lectuals, the media and the public came to recognize their role in the
debate.
 In considering the legacy of the GIP, our focus must always be the
history of the present (Foucault, 1977: 31). This chapter will make a
case for the continued relevance of the work of the GIP, taking stock of


144 Sophie Fuggle
its methodology, its publications and its outcomes. At stake here is the
way this moment belongs to the history of  our present. The comparative
successes and failures of the work of the GIP function here as a  point
de repère [landmark] for reflecting on the current tensions and issues
surrounding incarceration in the opening decades of the twenty-first
century.
 Today’s prison book ban brings us back to the notion of the ‘word’ –
back to the importance of reading as well as writing and of finding one’s
own voice through the voices of others. In considering this focus on the
word and its legacy, we should also bear in mind the role of the image
which has arguably become the most dangerous and ubiquitous form of
disciplinary power operating in contemporary society. 3
 Manifeste ronéotypé
 On 8 February 1971, the  Groupe d’Information sur les Prisons was launched
at a meeting held in the Chapelle Saint Bernard, Paris. The group’s
manifesto signed by J.-M. Domenach, M. Foucault and P. Vidal-Naquet
was read out by Foucault before a group of journalists, activists and
academics. The manifesto opened with the following statement:
 No one among us can be sure of avoiding prison. Today less than
ever. The systematic policing of our everyday lives gets tighter: in
the street, on the roads, tightening its grip on foreigners and young
people; thought crimes have returned, anti-drug measures prolif-
erate arbitrarily. We all live under the shadow of police custody. We
are told that the courts are overwhelmed. We can see that much for
ourselves. But what if it is the police that have overwhelmed them?
We are told that the prisons are overpopulated. But what if it is the
population that has been overimprisoned? (Foucault, 2001a: 1042;
my translation).
 If one does a web search of the keywords ‘Foucault’ and ‘GIP’, the image
most likely to appear is one of Foucault reading a statement. Sartre
stands to the left-hand side, lighting a cigarette. Depending on how
the image is cropped, Deleuze sometimes appears in the background,
looking on distracted. Sartre bored; Deleuze tangential. This is not, as
it is often believed, an image of the inaugural meeting of the GIP but a
press conference held by the group on 15 January 1972 in response to
the prison riots taking place at Nancy.


Beyond Slogans and Snapshots 145
 As the most widely circulated documents from the GIP archive, the
manifesto and the photograph of Foucault and Sartre, text and image,
work together, reducing the story of the GIP to slogan and snapshot. In
this context, it is unsurprising, therefore, that discussions of the GIP often
focus on the tensions between academic celebrities and those voices they
were attempting to make heard. Most notably, the work of the GIP tends
to be annexed to that of Foucault, frequently as a biographical footnote
to his theoretical work on institutional power, in particular, his 1975
text  Discipline and Punish . In this respect, it is flagged up as an example
of Foucault’s more successful political activism in contradistinction to
his ‘problematic’ reporting on the Iranian Revolution and elsewhere. 4
Foucault’s work as part of the GIP functions as useful recourse for those
wishing to highlight the ongoing paradoxes of his thought and personal
politics whether as critique  of or rehabilitation  from a sort of apolitical,
neoliberal dandyism. 5
 Consequently, to tell the ‘story’ of the GIP seems to require a certain
contextual framing which reproduces dominant narratives associated
with the intellectual Left in postwar France. 6 How, therefore, do we
conjure the spectral presence of May 1968, the  Gauche Prolétarienne ,
the French Maoists, in order to have done with this part of the story,
to move beyond and outside it ? This is not to deny the importance of
the multiple stakes, struggles, and movements coming together here
but to refuse to allow these moments of resistance to congeal. How do
we push the idea of the  Groupe d’Information sur les Prisons as practice
rather than as a historical artefact so as to rescue its critical force?
 We might go as far as to suggest that the GIP grew out of the desire
to tell a different story to the one we continue to tell and, moreover,
to have someone else tell it. Serious attention was first drawn to the
physical and material conditions of life in French prisons by those
arrested for their political activism. In 1970 this had resulted in the
introduction of the  loi anti-casseurs by a heavy-handed government
anxious to have done with the Gauche Prolétarienne (GP) and related
left-wing groups. While those sentenced as a result of their associa-
tion with the GP, including Le Dantec and Le Bris, editors of  La Cause
du Peuple , the GP’s newspaper, asserted their status as ‘political’ pris-
oners in order to emphasize the oppressive regimes operating against
freedom of speech and the right to protest, they also made it clear
that this did not set them apart from other inmates. Instead, they
called for the acknowledgement of conditions experienced by  all
those serving sentences. A statement to this effect and which claimed


146 Sophie Fuggle
to be ‘written in the prisons of France’ was published in the press in
September 1970:
 We demand full recognition of our status as political prisoners. We
are not, however, demanding privileges denied to so-called ‘common
law’ prisoners; in our view, they are victims of a social system which,
having produced them, refuses to re-educate them and is content
to degrade and reject them. Indeed, we want our struggle, which
denounces the present scandalous regime in the prisons, to help all
prisoners (quoted in Macey, 1993: 259).
 But alongside the word, the image is also key. What is it that we don’t
see here, that we can’t see here, that we are blocked from seeing? How
might the image of Foucault and Sartre point to something more inter-
esting that does not deny the importance of the image but defers this
importance, moving it elsewhere? The work of the GIP was focused
around the idea of the ‘voice’, different voices. But might we articu-
late or frame this better or more usefully for our own moment? Isn’t it
also about ‘visibility’? How do we think about the visible alongside the
audible and the invisible in conjunction with the inaudible?
 In their call for material from inside French prisons, letters, journal
entries, interviews, anything which told a different story to the official
narrative, the GIP also asked for images and flagged up the possibility of
a film. Indeed, in 1972 a documentary film was made, entitled  Les Prisons
Aussi and directed by René Lefort and Hélène Châtelain. However, the
documentary did not reach a mainstream audience. Television in France
was state run, and authorized documentaries continued to present a
limited critique of conditions inside French prisons. 7
 In 2013, a selection of photographs were published under the title  La
Révolte de la prison de Nancy . The collection contains images from the
January 1972 prison riots in Nancy alongside images of meetings, events
and protests organized by the GIP and documents from the group’s
archive. If anything, these images, which include the famous Foucault-
Sartre photograph, attest to an absence. This absence is acknowledged
by the collection’s editor, Philippe Artières. In his introduction he points
to the absence of photography of the inmate in prison, which until very
recently has been largely focused on prison architecture. Where images
of inmates have featured, these have consisted of carefully framed shots,
intended to demonstrate the everyday functioning of the prison as effec-
tive state institution, not its failure. What is missing, Artières suggests, is
the documentation of the events which disrupt prison life, the moments


Beyond Slogans and Snapshots 147
in which the prison loses its grip on those it contains, the moments
where the inmates push back against the architecture, routine and disci-
pline which organizes their existence (Artières, 2013: 8–9). This alterna-
tive reading is most evident in the choice of cover for the collection,
the front and back of which is entirely taken up by an image of inmates
on the roof of the prison. Writing about this symbolism of the roof in a
pamphlet dedicated to the prison riots in France, ‘Cahiers de revendica-
tions sortis lors des récentes révoltes’, the GIP suggested the following:
 The inmates did not get up on the roof in order to escape but so that
their pleas might carry as far as possible. Who is being addressed by
this plea which is currently spreading from prison to prison, gaining
in strength and clarity? (Groupe d’Information sur les Prisons,
2013 [1971–3]: 223, my translation).
 We might question the simplicity of such a reading. Where the image
might be read alongside similar images of inmates on the roofs of Attica
and Strangeways, drawing out the peculiarities as well as the common-
alities with struggles taking place in the US and UK both concurrently
and decades later, there are more questions raised than answered here.
This is not simply a statement about visibility. Certain protestors are
quite clearly attempting to disguise themselves, wearing scarves tied
around their faces. There are also obvious tensions between those up
on the roof. At the same time, might we wonder about all those not up
there? A very specific demographic is visible here in terms of age and
race. The men on the roof are predominantly white, well-dressed young
men between 18 and 35. Who exactly is claiming this visibility? And,
conversely, who remains invisible and why?
 Artière’s collection reminds us of the persistence of these questions
40 years on. But the stakes have changed here and the symbolism of such
self-representation has been co-opted by dominant discourses of power.
Today, the inmate is a firm fixture in public consciousness, as both voice
and image. Yet, if these voices and images have come to dominate the
screen and the press, they have at the same time become increasingly
distorted, fuzzy, pixelated, blurred and over-saturated. Although both
the photograph of Foucault and Sartre and that of the inmates on the
roof of Nancy make a case for the increased visibility of those incarcer-
ated, such visibility has long been co-opted to a politics of fear in which
the inmate is called to ‘perform’ his or her criminality for prime time
television audiences. In this respect, one dimension of the legacy of the
GIP must surely consist of an ongoing critical engagement with forms of


148 Sophie Fuggle
self-representation and the various framings these undergo according to
different political and ideological agendas.
 Furthermore, to remain fixated on the relationship between activism
and that which Foucault once denounced as the ‘scribblings’ [ griffonage ]
of academics serves neither position (Foucault, 2001c: 1169). Both
involve the reification of the academic, the author and the ego above the
cause that they are involved in fighting for and the history they are criti-
quing. The careful and sensitive negotiation between the two enacted by
Foucault, and his awareness that his privileged position was both useful
and distracting when trying to gain support, does not preclude us from
considering the work of the GIP alongside his genealogy of the prison in
 Discipline and Punish . If anything, both work together as a call to be done
with irrelevant binaries which divide up theory and practice, activist
and writer as well as cause and history.
 Foucault’s later, more nuanced notion of power is unquestionably
indebted to his work on prisons which notably took him to Attica in the
wake of the 1971 riots. Foucault described Attica in terms of an ‘immense’
and ‘complex’ machine, the function of which was ultimately social
exclusion. (Foucault, 2001e: 1394–5) To witness the functioning of such
a machine also pushed him to recognize more fully how the space of
the prison is predicated on more than just the negative elements of this
social exclusion.
 Prison is an organization that is too complex to be reduced to the
purely negative function of exclusion: its costs, the care taken in its
administration, the justification given to it, all this seems to indicate
that it has positive functions. The problem lies in discovering the
role that capitalist society has given to its penal system, what end is
sought, what effects have produced all these processes of punishment
and exclusion (2001e: 1396, my translation).
 Yet, if from the mid-1970s onwards Foucault posits an understanding of
disciplinary power as a productive rather than purely oppressive set of
operations on the deviant and docile body alike, then the GIP’s own posi-
tion seems to belong to an earlier understanding of power and authority
far more akin to an Althusserian reading of repressive state apparatuses
(RSAs), which posits prison as a violent and coercive mechanism used by
the state to maintain social order (Althusser, 1971). Consequently, it is
as if to get to the crux of what is at stake in disciplinary forms of power
that Foucault needed to go through an intense process of negotiation.


Beyond Slogans and Snapshots 149
The political and philosophical positions he held framed his work with
the GIP and were instrumental in his articulation of a deep-rooted suspi-
cion of institutional authority and organization. Yet, such positions were
themselves dramatically reshaped, if not dismantled, by the work with the
group itself. Francois Boullant has suggested the following: ‘Far more than
any of his other books,  Discipline and Punish carries the imprint of an inser-
tion into a harsh political context in which, well before its publication,
Foucault had invested completely’ (Boullant, 2003: 10, my translation).
 One of the conclusions we might reach here, perhaps, is that the GIP
exhausted itself in its own moment. Alberto Toscano has suggested that
the work of the GIP offers ‘no practical model’ for those involved in
contesting the existence and functioning of today’s prisons (Toscano,
2013). The organization ceased its activities in 1972, giving way to
inmate-led organizations including the Comité d’Action des Prisonniers
(CAP) and the Association pour la Défense des Droits des Détenus
(ADDD). The point, after all, was not to speak on behalf of those incar-
cerated but to give them the ability to speak for themselves. As we will
see, this point is not as straightforward as it sounds, and the work of the
GIP opened up a series of methodological as well as ethical questions
about the stakes of identifying this previously disparate group of indi-
viduals located in prisons across France as a collective, political voice. 8
 This is not a sociological enquiry
 The Groupe d’Information sur les Prisons has launched its first inquiry.
This is not a sociological inquiry. Instead, it is left up to those with an
experience to speak. Not that they need our help to ‘gain awareness’,
they are already aware of the oppression, this much is perfectly clear;
they are well aware of the enemy. But the current system denies them
the means to articulate and organize themselves. [ ... ] As the first act
of this ‘intolerance-inquiry’, we have distributed a questionnaire at
the prison gates and to all those able and willing to know and act
(Foucault, 2001b: 1043–4, my translation).
 The work of the GIP began through what Foucault referred to as
‘Intolerance Enquiries’. The idea was to collect and distribute as much
information as possible about what was intolerable about French prisons
in order to incite public intolerance and enable the establishment of
widespread social movement against these conditions. However, it is
worth noting here that Foucault and the rest of the group made it clear


150 Sophie Fuggle
that this was not a reformist project. The group considered such projects
to exclude rather than include the voices of those subjected to prison
conditions. Instead, the aim was to provide the information necessary
to push the public to question the very existence of the prison as the
default form of security, punishment and social rehabilitation (Groupe
d’Information sur les Prisons, 2013: 17).
 The first task took the form of a questionnaire smuggled into prisons
via prisoners’ families and those working there: doctors, lawyers, instruc-
tors, all those who were sympathetic to the project and all those who
agreed to take part. At that time access to French prisons was heavily
restricted, as it indeed continues to be, so the intellectuals who formed
the group were unable to enter prisons. Four pamphlets were then
produced under the title  Intolérable :

1. Enquête dans 20 prisons was published in May 1971 and contained
the original questionnaire and several samples of the narratives
received.

2. Le GIP enquête dans une prison modèle: Fleury Mérogis comprised
a study of what was at the time thought to be the most sophisti-
cated French prison in terms of architecture and management. It
included a commentary on the complicity of the mainstream press
with discourses of rehabilitation [ réinsertion ] promoted by prison
authorities.

3. L’Assasinat de George Jackson : Extending the scope of the enquiry
beyond France, the pamphlet was dedicated to Jackson, a member of
the Black Panthers, killed while he was trying to escape San Quentin
in August 1971. The pamphlet featured a preface from Jean Genet,
interviews with Jackson and various texts arguing that Jackson’s
death was, in fact, a ‘political assassination’.

4. Suicides de prison was compiled in 1972 and published in 1973. The
final pamphlet contained a list of 37 suicides committed in French
prisons during 1972, case histories and letters from H. M., an inmate
imprisoned for minor offences, who hanged himself in prison after
being sent to solitary for ‘homosexuality.’
 The concept of the intolerable is of key significance. Evoking the term
had the aim of turning intolerable conditions in the prisons into intol-
erability on the part of the public. But in its striking simplicity, this
gesture also raises the question of the role of the intellectual in defining
the ‘intolerable’ and, moreover, in positing it as something above and
beyond the specific material, physical and even psychological conditions


Beyond Slogans and Snapshots 151
of the prisons which constituted the subject of the inquiry. The term
is echoed in Deleuze and Guattari’s  What is Philosophy? where they
consider how speaking for others is something which cannot but arise
when we are confronted by intolerable suffering (Deleuze and Guattari,
1994: 109–10). If this rehabilitates the intellectual and academic from
privileged elite to engaged social actor, the tension to work through here
is what it means to speak for others and, subsequently, what it means
to give voice to others. How might the concept of the intolerable be
thought of as an ethical obligation whereby those who are able to speak
take up the causes of those who lack the language, organization or plat-
form to do so? How might the concept itself be critiqued as an imposi-
tion or projection legitimizing certain types of intellectual authority and
discourse?
 In her detailed study of the work of the GIP, Cecile Brich provides a
useful critique of the methodology, rhetoric and internal politics of the
group. In particular, she laments the largely hagiographical references to
the movement by David Halperin (1997) and others who often appear
to highlight the movement as little more than an example of Foucault’s
activism (Brich, 2008: 27). Brich’s analysis draws heavily on Foucault’s
own critique of discourse, articulated in  The Order of Discourse and  The
Archaeology of Knowledge . For Brich, the issue of who has the right to say
what, where and under which circumstances bears particular relevance
to the claims made by the group.
 Of specific interest here is her critique of the survey. According to
Brich, the survey or questionnaire as a mode of gathering information is
neither analysed nor problematized by the group. Despite claiming not
to be doing the work of sociologists, the group depends upon a standard
method without a documented debate as to its validity. The second
problem raised by Brich refers to the small number of responses to the
survey. From a potential sample of approximately 20,000 inmates, only
50 completed surveys were gathered . As such, their value was qualitative
rather than quantitative, but given the nature of the survey method,
this raises certain problems (Brich, 2008: 30–1).
 Despite the fact that the group claimed to have produced the question-
naires with the input of former prisoners and modified them following
initial responses (Groupe d’Information sur les Prisons, 2013: 19), Brich
goes on to suggest that a lack of thought went into the reception and
engagement with a survey by inmates both in terms of literacy and the
fear of repercussions if caught with illicit material. For Brich, this attests
to a level of presumption made by a group of privileged male academics
who assumed those whom they were calling on possessed the same


152 Sophie Fuggle
linguistic capabilities and used the same vernacular to describe their
experiences. There was also a lack of sensitivity towards those who may
have felt too ashamed to express themselves in poor French to complete
the survey (Brich, 2008: 31–3).
 If the survey were a failure in these respects, it is perhaps more useful
to consider this failure as achieving a certain aim nevertheless. The issue
of getting the questionnaires into the space of the prison and the enor-
mous difficulties and risks encountered in doing so is perhaps more
important to the group’s legacy than the number of responses received.
In this respect, the intolerance enquiry was a tactic rather than a strategy.
The information received attested as much to the lack of information,
the control and regulation of information, the preclusion and silencing
of alternative channels and the sources of information about life in
French prisons. Michael Welch has suggested that the work of the GIP
be considered as a form of ‘counterveillance’ in which the optics are
reversed and the institution becomes subject to the gaze of the prisoner
(Welch, 2011: 302ff.).
 The intolerance enquiries were not sociological enquiries since, despite
using an approach favoured by sociologists, the data sought was not
intended for academic publication in relevant social science journals
but rather pushed back against studies commissioned and approved by
government and prison authorities, turning the methods of compliant
sociologists and criminologists against them. Moreover, the presenta-
tion of the findings, as the next section will suggest, compiled through
a series of deliberate framings and juxtapositions, constitutes an affirma-
tion of writing, and moreover academic writing, as activism.
 Rogue archives
 Against the valid criticisms of the survey, such as its relying on a
conventional research method, and the ever-present risk of affirming
the voice of the public intellectual over and above that of the inmate,
it is possible to emphasize both elements of the project – the enquiry
and its presentation – in terms of that which Thomas Docherty has
termed ‘clandestine’ research, contrasting the ‘Official’ university with
a ‘Clandestine’ one. Docherty proposes that the ‘real’ research being
carried out by academics or intellectuals must go on outside and some-
times at odds with the formal research supported by their institutions
and external funding bodies (Docherty, 2014). In the case of the GIP,
this ‘clandestine’ research pertained to methods and styles of writing as
well as conducting research, actively contesting academic authority and


Beyond Slogans and Snapshots 153
authorship and challenging existing hierarchies between researchers
and their subjects.
 Writing while in prison has often been affirmed as a transformative
experience which is then mapped back onto the actual experience of
incarceration. 9 The statements, commentaries and editorial processes of
the GIP involved a different process altogether, one which, to appropriate
Foucault’s famous maxim, involved  writing not to have a face . (Foucault,
2002: 19) The juxtaposition of material from inside and outside the
prison walls and the anonymity of the group’s own writing constitute
the deliberate dismantling of institutional hierarchies, bringing the
voices of the prisoners into the same space as those critiquing the prison
from beyond its confines. The coexistence of these voices, speaking from
different places but brought together within the pages of the pamphlets
calls into question not only who gets to speak about incarceration but
how and why.
 Where the framing of the survey responses along with texts by inmates,
statistics and statements from lawyers and social workers is intended to
allow these voices their own spaces in which to speak and be heard
via a series of disclaimers in the group’s own statements, elsewhere in
the pamphlets there emerges a different tactic aimed at shutting down
or talking over dominant discourses on prisons and those incarcerated.
This mode of circumvention is notable in Genet’s preface to the third
GIP pamphlet dedicated to the assassination of George Jackson (Groupe
d’Information sur les Prisons, 2013: 155–63). Genet does not open with
Jackson’s biography, with the exceptional circumstances of his death
but instead establishes these as the rule: ‘In Europe, it is increasingly
rare that a person is willing to risk death for his ideas. Blacks in America
do this every day’ (Groupe d’Information sur les Prisons, 2013: 155; my
translation). The tone set here refuses a romanticizing or eulogizing
of Jackson and instead sets out to reproduce the bitter sarcasm Genet
locates in Jackson’s own writing which he then allows to take centre
stage in the preface (157).
 Another example of this ‘talking over’ can be found in the second
pamphlet which takes the ‘model’ prison of Fleury-Mérogis as its target.
An article in  France Soir celebrating the prison is reproduced and preceded
by a scathing exposé of the standard prison tour and the complicity of
journalists in affirming prisons as well-managed spaces of education
and rehabilitation (139–41). The careful negotiation between the angry
polemic of the pamphlets with their lists of intolerable institutions in
large font and the care taken to prioritize , as far as possible, the voices
of inmates and their families above the statements and commentaries


154 Sophie Fuggle
of the group continues to bear significance for anyone attempting to
(re)present the intolerable experiences of others. With the GIP, tech-
niques of framing and editing are reimagined as forms of engaged,
intellectual responsibility rather than as assertions of academic
authority.
 Our over-imprisoned population
 For them [the bourgeois government], hate is masked by a lying
humanity that they will cling to no matter what (Sartre, 1971: 14–15,
my translation).
 In  Discipline and Punish , Foucault asks how the prison came to constitute
the mode of punishment  par excellence . Here, he argues that it is not a
result of a more humane society that corporal and capital punishment
died out in Western Europe during the eighteenth and nineteenth centu-
ries. The carceral space in its management, surveillance and control of
the criminal body is part of the emergence of a disciplinary mode of
power aimed at producing a docile and compliant workforce.  Discipline
and Punish takes this docile body as its focus, linking the appearance
of such a body with the effective and efficient production of capital.
Foucault’s 1973 lecture series,  La Société Punitive , delivered at the Collège
de France, identifies the emergence of the prison more closely with the
protection of capital and, in particular, a bourgeois capital, constantly
on the move (Foucault, 2013: 107ff.). Imprisonment, as assuring both
the production and protection of a globalized, deterritorialized capital,
must necessarily constitute the starting point for enquiry into our own
over-imprisoned population.
 It is possible to locate the work of the GIP at a precise moment in
recent history in which the very existence of the prison within Western
society was being called into question. (Alexander, 2012: 8) The task
today includes picking up where Foucault left off. How might we begin
to construct a genealogy of the past 40 years that identifies the various
shifts and turning points which have resulted in the over-imprisoned
population we have today?
 Michelle Alexander picks up one possible thread here in her analysis
of Reagan’s ‘War on Drugs’, which, she argues, led to a sudden upward
surge in prison populations which have continued to rise over the last
30 years (Alexander, 2012). However, if Alexander provides compel-
ling evidence for the systemic racism underpinning today’s mass incar-
ceration in the US, this needs to be coupled with further reflection on


Beyond Slogans and Snapshots 155
conditions within the prison system, not just for those who are the
victims of racist drug laws but for all prisoners, including rapists and
serial killers. Again, this is why the work of the  Groupe d’Information sur
les Prisons must be read alongside Foucault’s theoretical analyses of the
emergence of the prison as definitive penalty for illegal activity. To lever
open the mechanisms which allow the effective functioning of both
prison as nineteenth-century disciplinary institution and contemporary
industrial complex requires tactics operating at once from within and
without the carceral space. 10
 On being asked about a world without prisons, Foucault points to the
historical contingency of this form of punishment:
 The answer is easy. There were, in fact, societies without prisons not
so very long ago. As a mode of punishment, prison is an invention
dating from the beginning of the nineteenth century. [ ... ] You want
me to describe a utopian society where there would be no prison.
The problem is to find out whether a society could be imagined in
which the application of rules would be applied by groups themselves
(Foucault, 2001d: 1300).
 Yet, his deliberately evasive answer puts the onus back onto us. To what
extent do we take the space of the prison for granted and incarcera-
tion as default punishment for all crime as an incontestable given? How
many of us would want to see the abolition of prisons and the release
of the general prison population into society? How far are we willing to
consider the implications of such a proposal? This is one of the blind
spots of much academic writing on incarceration today.
 Moreover, it is also not without an acute irony that the notion of
intolerance has been co-opted by a series of overtly repressive state
apparatuses, to return to Althusser’s terminology, in the form of the
zero tolerance measures introduced against individuals and groups. Our
lack of tolerance has been redirected and, as a consequence, what we
are seeing today are increasingly overpopulated prisons and harsher
sentencing for both first-time and repeat offenders. Grayling’s recent
two strikes rule for violent crime echoes measures imposed by Sarkozy
in France and the Reagan (War on Drugs) and Clinton (Three Strikes)
regimes in the US. Despite the accompanying rhetoric, such measures
rarely bear any relation to actual crime rates but, instead, correspond
to a perceived threat as social inequalities are exposed and demands for
redress become more vocal. The excessively punitive sentences handed
out to those caught in the UK riots of 2011 bear testimony to this. It is


156 Sophie Fuggle
far easier to direct one’s sense of injustice, indignation, inequality and
intolerance against those who are weaker than it is to push back against
the system. 11
 Thus, whether education and training programs are cut from or intro-
duced into the carceral space is of little import to the role of the prison
in the opening decades of the twenty-first century. Banning books or
praising alpha courses and prisonfare, to quote UK examples once more,
are all part of a bad faith exercise which enables us, all of us, to draw a
thin veil over the toxic politics of fear, which has come to define late
capitalist society and which continues to justify locking up large percent-
ages of our populations. 12
 Notes
 1 .  For a detailed account of the work of the GIP and the events surrounding the
group, see the chapter ‘Intolerable’ in Macey, 1993: 257–89.
 2 .  Davis argues that we should be wary of ‘reformist’ discourses which are aimed at a
more efficiently managed prison system and  not a serious questioning of the phys-
ical and psychological suffering endured by those incarcerated (Davis, 2003: 20).
 3 .  For compelling analyses of the use of images in the ‘war on terror’ see, for
example, Butler, 2009 and Mitchell, 2011.
 4 .  A summary of the events of 1978–79 is presented before the first of Foucault’s
reports reprinted in  Dits et Ecrits II (Foucault, 2001f: 663). A useful back-
ground is also provided by Michiel Leezenberg (2004). Offering a different
reading to most commentators, Slavoj Žižek has suggested that Foucault’s
engagement with the Iranian Revolution was an ‘appropriate gesture’ but
performed for the wrong reasons. Foucault was right, according to Žižek, in
recognizing the ‘emancipatory potential’ of the events of 1978 but made a
fundamental mistake in how he set about theorizing this potential (Žižek,
2008: 108–16).
 5 .  Francois Boullant has suggested that the anti-Foucauldian movement led by
Jean Baudrillard in the late 1970s and 1980s with his polemic text,  Oublier
Foucault , which contested the validity of Foucault’s work for radical polit-
ical thought, seriously damaged the perception of the possibilities which
Foucault’s work on prisons opened up for thinking differently about incar-
ceration (Boullant, 2003: 8–9).
 6 .  For a brief but nevertheless useful contextualization of the GIP documents in
the context of French Maoism, see Toscano, 2013.
 7 .  According to Alain Brossat, French media interest in the penal system
continues to function according to a circular logic of ‘attraction and repul-
sion’ which takes the form of scandal and silence. Journalists flock to a prison
on the occasion of a scandal or incident, employ a rhetoric of moral indigna-
tion which assures rather than challenges public complicity with subsequent
penal reforms and government legislation (Brossat, 2001: 22ff).
 8 .  As Macey has pointed out, it was unsurprising that some of the families of
inmates involved in the establishment of these new groups sought to distance
themselves from radical left-wing groups (Macey, 1993: 289).


Beyond Slogans and Snapshots 157
 9 .  For a detailed study of a selection of twentieth century French literary prison
writing see, for example, Sobanet 2008. More recently, the philosopher
Bernard Stiegler has published on the relationship between reading, writing
and his own experience of incarceration (Stiegler, 2009).
 10 .  As Alexander (2012), Davis (2003) and others have pointed out, the prison
industrial complexes of the US not only warehouse an unwanted, unneeded
labour force but, in doing so, assure the entire economic infrastructure of
entire towns and communities. Today the prison industry coexists alongside
the war industry as a dominant and guaranteed economy.
 11 .  In  Punishing the Poor , Loïc Wacquant argues that the increasingly punitive
measures introduced in the US stem from widespread anxiety about social
and economic inequality rather than any evidence of an increase in crime
(Wacquant, 2009).
 12 .  In his Easter speech of 2014, the British Prime Minister spoke of promoting
Britain as ‘a Christian society’ which included a reference to the ‘alpha
courses’ on offer in UK prisons. For an interesting comparison between
this ‘moral’ discourse and the emergence of the prison in the nineteenth
century, see Foucault’s lecture from 31 January 1973 on  La Société Punitive .
Here, Foucault points out that while there are obvious points of comparison
between techniques found in prison and those used in religious orders, the
prison did not emerge directly out of a Christian moral framework. Instead,
morality was called into the service of the penal system and policing as a
means of protecting bourgeois interests from the lower classes (Foucault,
2013: 84–102). For Chris Grayling’s endorsement of prisonfare programs, see
his speech on 9 October 2012.
