sur les Prisons Reversed the Optics’,  Theoretical Criminology , 15(3), pp. 301–13.
 Žižek, S. (2008)  In Defense of Lost Causes . London and New York: Verso.


159
 The migration-government nexus: which critique?
 Migration has today become a self-evident buzzword embedded with
multifarious social and political issues which together form a sort of
‘dodgy continuum’, connecting terrorism, insecurity, poverty, traf-
ficking and a humanitarian-securitarian bond that works also as a
border. The main political and epistemic lynchpin that sustains the
translation of various practices of mobility into migration is the para-
digm of government. Moreover, migration as a social issue is coupled
from its very outset with a governmental rationale: migration is a
phenomenon to manage. For these reasons, as indeed many scholars
have argued, there would not be migration without borders, only
mobility. And, given the regulative function of borders, it follows that
there would not be migration outside of a governmental perspective.
‘Government’ stands also for the epistemic blueprint that shapes our
way of thinking and speaking of people’s movements. Most critical
analyses on migration governmentality assume this of coupling migra-
tion-government as an unquestionable bond, addressing criticisms
towards inhumane treatments against migrants or towards the depri-
vation of migrants’ political and civil rights, insisting on the neces-
sity of granting access to services and protection. If the translation
of practices of mobility into migration in order to govern is not chal-
lenged in itself, the critique of the government of migration becomes
a slippery discourse, unwittingly turning into a critique of the excesses
10
 Troubling Mobilities: Foucault and
the Hold over ‘Unruly’ Movements
and Life-Time 1
 Martina  Tazzioli


160 Martina Tazzioli
and violations of the politics of mobility and into a claim for a ‘fair
government’.
 In this regard, it is worth recalling Foucault’s distinction between
critique as judgement or denunciation and critique as inseparable from
transformation and as its condition of possibility. 2 Therefore, critique
does not consist in saying what is wrong but in refusing and resisting
a certain mechanism of power, envisaging and enacting new relational
forms: ‘there is not a time for criticism and a time for transformation;
there are not those who need to do criticism and those who have to
transform [ ... ]. [T]the work of a deep transformation can be done than
in the opened and always turbulent atmosphere of a continuous criti-
cism’ (Foucault, 2002b: 457). 3 Thus, it is precisely by starting from this
unquestioned coupling – migration and government or, better, migra-
tion as a something to govern – and unpacking this coupling that I
introduce Foucault and his notes on the spatial containment of ‘irreg-
ular’ mobility through the apparatus of sequestration and the grasp over
people’s time. Indeed, as I will show, although Foucault never directly
deals with the issue of migration, his sideways approach to this theme
nonetheless brings to the fore and mobilizes a reversal of the gaze on
mobility, politicizing practices of movements and presenting them as
strategies for dodging disciplinary captures.
 Troubling mobility and apparatus of sequestration:
Reversing the gaze on migration
 In particular, I suggest that Foucault’s reflections on the government
of mobility in his lectures at the Collège de France in 1973,  La Société
Punitive , appear to anticipate and dialogue at a distance with more recent
theories of the autonomy of migration (De Genova, 2010; Mezzadra,
2006; Mitropoulos, 2007; Moulier-Boutang, 2002; Papadopoulos and
Tsianos, 2007; Walters, 2013) arguing that the control over mobility
in the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries functioned through appa-
ratuses of sequestration of people’s labour force and time. The 1973
lectures provide an analytical insight into the politics of mobility that
points towards the government of migration as a set of strategies for
taming and channelling ‘mobility disorders’. In this way, if we read
Foucault’s analyses on government in the light of the thesis advanced in
 La Société Punitive concerning mobility – namely, the idea that control
over mobility worked as a strategy for taming and containing ‘dangerous’
displacements – then governmentality appears essentially as a struggle
field and as a strategic response to those troubling movements.


Troubling Mobilities 161
 The choice of attributing Foucault with disrupting the perspective on
the migration-government nexus might seem a strange move, given the
seminal centrality of the notion of government as an analytical grid for
rethinking of power relations. However, what is in question here is not
the usefulness of governmentality as an analytical grid for grasping the
current functioning of migration and border controls and to frame the
so-called migration regime in terms of a struggle field between migrants’
strategies and techniques of capture. Instead, the gesture to displace the
issue of government pertains to a different analytical level – namely, to
the evident coupling between migration and government that leads us
to immediately subsume under and translate practices of movement into
migration to be managed. A history of the present, hinged on mobility
control as a strategic response to ‘undisciplined conducts’, allows us to
trace the inception of a more or less structured politics of control over
people’s movements across Europe. Indeed, as Foucault shows, since
the rise of capitalist modes of production, the political technology of
government over lives has taken mobility as one of its primary points of
contention. However, in a similar fashion to Foucault’s other genealo-
gies, a history of the present does not mean to transpose the technology
of control over people’s mobility in the nineteenth century onto our
contemporaneity in order to understand the current migration regime.
Rather, it is precisely through a series of dislocations, partial continui-
ties, discordances and ongoing reinvestments that the migration regime
emerges as a conflicting site and as a mechanism of capture against
troubling movements and undisciplined conduct. For instance, and as
I will explain later, the productivism associated with the government
of migrants’ lives as labour force has to be reassessed today in light of
mechanisms of discharge as well as the current economic crisis. But at
the same time, despite the specific historical context in which Foucault
grounds his analysis, what is at stake in his reflections is a substantial
reversal of the gaze on mobility and government that can resonate with
and be reactivated in contemporary spaces. In particular, from a spatial
standpoint, I suggest that Foucault’s sideways gaze on mobility, through
an articulated analysis on labour regimes and disciplinary power, allows
us to see borders and partitions that cannot be spatially located on a
map. In fact, geopolitical frontiers and the borders traced by migration
policies need to be intertwined with multiple partitions and bordering
processes that stem from other mechanisms of control.
 One of the seminal reflections of the lectures on  La Société Punitive for
a critique that tries to decouple and displace the migration-government
nexus is, as I said, Foucault’s sideways approach to the topic of mobility.


162 Martina Tazzioli
Indeed, the main subject of the lectures is neither human mobility nor
the politics of control exercised on it but, rather, the emergence of
what Foucault calls ‘punitive society’, paving the way to an analysis
of the birth of the prison that is subsequently developed in  Discipline
and Punish . At the core of his genealogy there is a new economy of
power – disciplinary power – that emerged in the sixteenth century,
deeply modifying the functioning of social institutions well beyond
the walls of the prison, and in which  life-time and  labour-time conflate. 4
Why and how since the eighteenth century has detention become the
main form of punishment? In order to answer this question, Foucault is
in fact forced to engage in a twofold move of widening and displacing
his analysis. Firstly, imprisonment as a punitive technique should be
located within a technology of government and control that, taking
different forms and degrees, has been employed in different sites of
capitalist society. 5 Secondly, the function of imprisoning and banishing
dangerous subjects in order to break their intolerable illegalisms has
always been coupled with that of fixing people to the dispositives of
production. Thus, the attention shifts from the prison as an institu-
tion to the economy of power relations in which the prison is located.
This analytical move towards the economy of power is actually what
makes Foucault engage with the production and the government of
people’s irregular mobility. In fact, to historically retrace the function of
mobility control in emerging capitalist society, stressing the transforma-
tions in its mechanisms and aims over the years does not entail making
a history of marginal subjects but rather points to the centrality of that
object of government (mobility). In this way, more than a history that
accounts for the ‘silences’ in the main narrative of capitalism, a gene-
alogy of the government of ‘turbulent’ mobility, works as an analytical
lens for grasping the economy of power and its contested nature: ‘disor-
dered’ practices of mobility destabilize both the ‘citizenship episteme’
and mechanisms of production and control that react through a reas-
semblage of disciplining captures.
 This methodological approach appears to resonate with what Silvia
Federici has argued about the relevance of a history from the viewpoint
of women, claiming that the word ‘women’ signifies not just a hidden
history that needs to be made visible but a particular form of exploi-
tation, and therefore a unique perspective from which to reconsider
the history of capitalist relations’ (Federici, 2004: 13). In particular, by
taking on mobility as a main stake of the government of conducts since
the modern age, it becomes possible – in the case of Foucault via the


Troubling Mobilities 163
analysis of the penal system – to see ‘the nature of struggles that, in
a society, take place around power’ (Foucault, 2013: 14). Therefore, to
analyse the techniques of containment and punishment through and
within the economy of power that sustains them does not lead to the
neutralization of troubling practices and disruptive movements within a
narrow economic explanation. On the contrary, by looking at the regu-
lation of mobility, Foucault brings to the surface the responsive nature
of disciplining technologies. In the face of the emergence of new forms
mobility and illegalisms, the multiple  foyers of power have to rearrange
themselves into new assemblages. Indeed, they are essentially appara-
tuses of capture that are aimed at taming and extracting value from
those movements. In this regard, it could be argued that the control of
mobility is also an attempt to retake control on the excesses of illegal-
isms and irregularities. 6 Before coming back to the gaze’s reversal on
migration mobilized by Foucault, I will dwell upon the targets and the
functions of this ‘modern obsession’ that mobility is a phenomenon to
govern.
 The containment of freedom and the government of
irregular conducts through the control of mobility
 Why did human mobility start to be framed in the seventeenth century
as a dangerous and troubling phenomenon in need of control? Is it
movement in itself that is source of risk, and what is the ‘scandal’ of
free movement? It is noteworthy that the explanation given by Foucault
correlates with his analysis of disciplinary power. Indeed, the ques-
tion on the function of incarceration as a mechanism of punishment
is displaced by Foucault towards the transformations to the economy
of power relations during the rise of capitalism: the institution of
the prison, as part of a broader political technology, aims at having
a hold on people’s life-time. In the eighteenth century the refusal to
work became an object of moral condemnation and, simultaneously, of
juridical sanction: ‘there is a fundamental identity between the fact of
moving and the refusal of work’ (Foucault 2013: 49). 7 It is not difficult
to understand why Foucault identifies in the vagabond the paradig-
matic character of the deviant subject, the matrix of irregular conducts;
or better, the vagabond becomes the criminal  par excellence , the indi-
vidual in which moral deprivation, resistance to production and the
social instability collapse and coincide (Chamayou, 2012). At that stage
in the evolution of capitalist society the dislocation from the territory


164 Martina Tazzioli
and an uncontrolled or uneven mobility was in itself considered as a
source of instability and trouble since it meant a substantial unreli-
ability of the worker’s commitment.
 Then, following the historical steps marked in Foucault’s analysis, we
are confronted with a slightly different scene: in the nineteenth century
states and employers try to bridle the labour force of the working class
through the regime of contract. Therefore, it is less the marginal subject
or the vagabond whose movements are controlled and hampered than
the industrial worker: ‘the point of application is no longer wealth as an
object of possible appropriation but the body of the worker as produc-
tive force’ (Foucault, 2013: 192). The refusal to put one’s own body
to work now represents the major source of risk for capitalism since
it prevents that life is synthetized into labour force (Foucault, 2013:
236). It is precisely at this stage that the norm intervenes to guarantee
the transformation of life into a docile productive body through the
acquisition of labour-discipline as a habit. The norm works as a multiple
fixing mechanism, which binds the individual physically and morally
to different forms of capture: the social space, the discipline of labour
and the apparatus of production. As Foucault suggests in  Abnormal , ‘the
norm is not simply and not even a principle of intelligibility; it is an
element on the basis of which a certain exercise of power is founded and
legitimized’ (Foucault, 2003: 50). By closely linking the emergence of
disciplinary modes of power with the rise of capitalism, both Marx and
Foucault unfold the unnaturalness of labour, highlighting the necessary
surplus generated by the norm – and formed by coercive measures and
moralizing technologies and discourses – to put subjectivities to work
(Macherey, 2013; Mezzadra, 2014).
 Labour is absolutely not man’s concrete essence or man’s existence
in its concrete form. In order for men to be brought into labour, to
be tied to labour, an operation is necessary, or a complex series of
operations by which men are effectively – not analytically or synthet-
ically – bound to the production apparatus for which they labour
(Foucault, 2002a: 86).
 The norm operates less as an exclusionary measure – dividing up
normal and abnormal – than by tracing a social space in which bodies
and subjectivities are translated into conducts whose social bond is not
given by property but by the fixation to the productive apparatuses.
Hence, the incessant striving to produce labour force and make of it a
natural quality of subjects is primarily a mechanism for destituting and


Troubling Mobilities 165
hindering autonomous forms of conduct that, depicted as depraved or
lazy behaviours, refuse to become labour force.
 Labour force indicates not only individual bodies but also collective
forces. Indeed, the dangerousness of uncontrolled mobility and the
 dodgy continuum with other forms of  illegalism that it establishes depend
on the collective forms it could take: ‘modes of existence’, as Foucault
defines them, try to resist, circumvent and subtract from the capitaliza-
tion of life into productive force, generating ‘a counter-collective that
could menace the institution itself’ (Foucault, 2013: 219). Ultimately,
the menace depends on the fact that ‘labour within capitalist social rela-
tions is, in this sense, always simultaneously labour for capital and also
against capital’ (De Genova 2013). In this sense, irregular mobility is
prominently a practice of spatial flight from a control over life’s time
that tries to bind subjects to the mechanisms of production. A very
similar thesis has been advanced by Yann Moulier-Boutang, whose
seminal book  De l’esclavage au salariat explains the rationale at the basis
of mobility controls and migration policies, both in Europe and in the
colonies: ‘The control of the flight of waged workers represents the most
important element that presided over the birth, the attrition and the
substitution of different forms of non-free labour, and to the origin of
social protection as well as to the status of free wage labour’ (Moulier-
Boutang, 2002: 17).
 The government through non-government and the
fragmentation of migrants’ time
 Taking  Discipline and Punish and  La Société Punitive together allows us
see the twofold effect of incarceration and disciplining power at large in
terms of the production of illegalism and the transformation of life into
docile labour force. In this way, the thesis of  Discipline and Punish cannot
be read in relation to  the government of the mob without considering the
apparatuses of sequestration acting on marginal subjects and later on
the working class. 8 However, as I stated initially, Foucault’s history of the
present is characterized by an attempt to find turning points and pivotal
moments, which indicate how certain mechanisms of capture become
reinscribed into different political technologies in order to respond
to these refusals to work. This is why the picture drawn by Foucault
cannot be applied as it is to the current political framework, since, for
instance, the heterogeneity of the present regimes of labour corresponds
to likewise manifold labour conditions, constrictions and strategies of
resistance.


166 Martina Tazzioli
 Moving beyond this, here, I focus on two limit-cases in which the
economy of power shifts in part from its ordinary functioning: the
economic transformations during a period of crisis and the government
of refugees. Indeed, the functioning of apparatuses of sequestration
described by Foucault in relation to the eighteenth and the nineteenth
centuries cannot merely be extended to any form of the government of
mobility. And in this regard, the government of refugees emerges as one
of the most longstanding mobility regimes. The creation in the twentieth
century of the system of asylum has traced a series of partitions between
different kinds of migration based on the main sustaining watershed
between economic migrants and refugees. And on close analysis, in the
case of the politics of asylum it becomes hard to fully adopt and apply
the analytical grid of the valorization and fixation of life’s time in order
to transform it into productive labour force.
 Indeed, as far as the government of asylum seekers is concerned, the
blueprint of the productive subject to be disciplined and exploited turns
out to be only partly tenable: distinct from the detention of irregular
‘economic’ migrants, which as many scholars have pointed out should
be seen as apparatuses for filtering and decelerating the incoming of
the migrant labour force (Mezzadra, 2006; Papadopoulos, Stephenson
and Tsianos, 2008), the functioning of refugee camps and the regime
of international protection  de facto works by stranding, breaking and
fragmenting people’s lives. These produce an ongoing interruption
and detour in regard to their fixation within a linear, constant and
productive function. While the mechanisms of capture over vaga-
bonds or workers, as described by Foucault, aim at grabbing people’s
time and spatially fixing them to a certain location or function, in this
case the main effect is a substantial fragmentation of temporal conti-
nuity, actions/projects and patterns. Thus, against this background we
should interrogate the kind of governmental rationality upon which
these fragmenting captures are exercised. It could be suggested that
the sorting machine that partitions between refugees and economic
migrants pertains rather to a logic of a government of (migration) popu-
lation that, by tracing a moral geography of protection, reallocates and
distributes people through selective criteria, rejecting the majority of
them from refugee status.
 A similar caution should be taken in relation to the current political
and economic context. Indeed, especially in a period of economic crisis,
the strategy of full employment is by a great deal far from the actual
economic rationale: in fact, a generalized production of precariousness,
based on differential degrees of partial and temporary employment,


Troubling Mobilities 167
seems to better qualify the economic grasp on migrants’ and non-
migrants’ lives. In this regard, it should be questioned whether the
general figure of illegalism is still nowadays that of dissipation: rather,
the moral partition between the active and disciplined working citizen
and the lazy ‘irregular’ subject, which Foucault foregrounds in his
analysis of the nineteenth century capitalist society, does not work
so smoothly anymore, due to the substantial inactivity in which both
migrants and non-migrants are forced to live. This complicates and
blurs the boundaries of the so-called migrant condition, and at the
same time, it partially cracks the rationale of fixing people and putting
them to work. However, this does not mean that in a time of economic
crisis the logic of work, as a mechanism of government and capture, is
no longer applicable in describing the connection between the govern-
ment of mobility and economy of conducts. On the contrary, the
point is the way in which work has been recast in the government of
mobility through mechanisms of generalized precarization that render
the migrant and non-migrant labour force constantly at disposal and,
at the same time, stranded and unemployed for long periods. To put it
in a nutshell, the ‘migrantization’ of life-time and labour-time increas-
ingly affects also the subjects who legally are non-migrants (Tazzioli
2014).
 Beyond exclusion and smooth governmentality: the uneven
machines of capture of migrants’ lives
 Despite these huge transformations and increasing complexities that are
well encapsulated in the multifarious migration taxonomy, Foucault’s
focus on the economy of power relations provides nonetheless a distinc-
tive analytical lens. In fact, Foucault takes his cues from a radical critique
of the notion of exclusion as a fruitful paradigm for understanding
power relations and the complex games between powers and resist-
ances. Through this move Foucault does not want to deny the existence
of mechanisms of exclusion; rather, he points out that the notion of
exclusion is fundamentally grounded in the field of ‘juridical, political
and moral representation’ (Foucault 2013: 7). Indeed, the category of
exclusion induces a sort of double overshadowing. On the one hand,
it conflates all marginal individuals into a negative and lacking subjec-
tivity – the repressed, the excluded – in contraposition to the full citizen;
on the other hand, it does not look at the effective functioning of power
relations, instead bringing attention to the forms in which power is
supposed to work or appears. In the same vein, Foucault explains in  The


168 Martina Tazzioli
Will to Knowledge that his critique of the repressive hypothesis aims ‘less
at showing it to be mistaken than at putting it back within a general
economy of discourses’ (Foucault, 1998: 11). Such a perspective, I argue,
becomes particularly useful in the context of the government of migra-
tions. Or better still, it helps us to avoid any possible binary opposition
between integration/exploitation of migrants in the productive system
and rejection/exclusion to produce bare life. Starting from this set of
considerations, challenging the inclusion/exclusion paradigm means
shifting attention from the productivity/unproductivity of the subjects
to the struggle field battlefield of governmentality, namely to the
economy of powers as confronted with practices of flight and resistance
that force the strategies of capture to reassess their mode of operation.
 Following this, I suggest that we should take into account the diverse
political techniques that underpin the government of different forms of
mobility, not through the double code of inclusion/exclusion but through
detecting the points and the mechanisms upon which such government
applies – for instance, the government of migration population, the
selection of labour force or finally what I would call  government through
nongovernment . This expression designates a fairly common way of oper-
ating within the field of migration management that forces us in part to
reformulate the Foucaultian matrix of government as framed by govern-
mentality studies. Indeed, as far as migration as an object of government
is concerned, an exclusive focus on governmental mentalities tends to
overlook the always existing gaps between governmental texts and the
effective drive of strategies of containment and capture. Rather, the
image of a capillary and at the same time homogeneous machine of
governance crumbles as soon as we are confronted with the infraliminar
level of administrative techniques, bureaucratic conundrums, practices
of detention, management of ‘humanitarian emergencies’ and migrants’
flights. The ‘text’ of migration governance staged in EU documents and
in the discourses of migration agencies is undercut by the plethora of
regulative practices that sidestep the supposed standardized governmen-
tality and in part effectively work through a substantial nongovernment.
Governing through nongovernment does not signify the loss of control
or a loosened grip on migrants’ lives. On the contrary, it indicates that
the alleged exhaustive  prise en charge of migrants’ lives – in the forms of
an indefinite monitoring and surveillance as well as through standard-
ized procedures – is actually confronted and combined with practices
of non-intervention and discharge, leaving some migrants stranded in
their legal, spatial or existential impasses and forms of immobility. The


Troubling Mobilities 169
fragmentation of migrants’ patterns and  life-time is eminently produced
through the disruption of mechanisms that actually aim at hampering
people’s movements. At the same time, it is presented as a source of a
smooth governmentality that selects, allocates and distributes people in
space . Ultimately, the indefinite stalemate of migrants’ lives is a tangible
effect of a way of governing through nongovernment. In this way, in
the place of a substantive definition of governmentality involving a
strong commitment to rationalities of government, Foucault’s defini-
tion of government – ‘an action upon an action, on existing actions or
on those which may arise in the present or the future actions’ (Foucault,
1982: 789) – makes possible not to force it in the direction of a model
of an overwhelming and smooth migration management. Also, it allows
us to take into account modalities of regulation that work by dumping
and stranding people but that, at the same time, have concrete effects
on the possibility of others’ actions in the fragmentation of life’s time
they produce.
 Returning to Foucault’s displacement of the binary inclusion/exclu-
sion, it could be suggested that, along with the implications illustrated
above, this displacement activates a fundamental reversal of the gaze
that, as I will show, leads us to the theories of the autonomy of migra-
tion. 9 In fact, a disengagement from the code of exclusion primarily
engenders a radical turn of the analytical standpoint, since reading the
government of mobility through the lens of exclusion/inclusion means
assuming the order of citizenship as the given space against which any
form of ‘irregular’ mobility is confronted. The refusal to adopt such a citi-
zen’s gaze allows us to look at practices of mobility as movements that
induce what Nicholas De Genova calls ‘reaction formations’ – namely,
strategies and techniques of capture that try to contain, filter and tame
those troubling mobilities (De Genova, 2013). Thus, to speak of practices
of movement in terms of migration and government is already a form of
translation and a way of encoding ‘irregular’ mobility into the order of
citizenship, as an abnormal and disturbing factor, a deviation from it.
In other words, the  unbearableness of irregular mobility relies precisely
on the adoption of the standpoint of the state and citizen, that makes
us see practices of movement as instabilities to be channelled or capi-
talized upon as labour force. Instead, as Dimitris Papadopoulos points
out, reflecting on the practices of flight that characterized the epoch
of the rise of capitalism, ‘the singularities that composed the escaping,
wandering mob were very far from the humanist individual emerging
at the same moment across Europe [ ... ] collectivities that defined the


170 Martina Tazzioli
core of radical struggles for emancipation’ (Papadopoulos, 2010). And,
recalling closely the analysis of Moulier-Boutang on the emergence of
the regime of wage labour, he continues by saying that ‘many of the scat-
tered, disorganised, ephemeral, insurgent movements of people exiting
feudal labour in so many different locales and geographies, continents
and seas were not to enter into the capitalist humanist regime of the
labour market but to escape into a journey that allowed them to create
common worlds’ (Papadopoulos, 2010).
 Therefore, such a reversal of the gaze on migration refracts on a
similar discordant gaze on the history of capital-labour relations, where
mobility turns out to be at the very junction of the struggle field around
freedom and property. If the government of mobility should be read as
a means of controlling productivity, this latter must in turn be under-
stood as the containment of freedom. In fact, as Foucault also indicated
presenting control over mobility as a way for governing conducts and
bodies against practices of freedom and flight, Moulier-Boutang empha-
sizes that ‘freedom is essentially the freedom to escape the land of the
landlord and, at the same time and immediately, the wage labour’
(Mouliar-Boutang, 2002: 272). In this way, we see that Foucault’s insight
on the increasing controls over mobility in capitalist society and the
recent theories on the autonomy of migration enable us to also challenge
the current securitarian rationality that sustains the legitimacy of the
government of migration. Moreover, if migration controls are conceived
as a strategy for containing freedom, it follows that the question on
the failure of migration policies in reducing the number of migrants
is wrongly framed. The illegalization of certain forms of mobility and
techniques of surveillance ‘engender an acceleration of the processes of
mobility’ and ‘in order to escape sanctions [populations] tend to conduct
an irregular life and fall into nomadism’ (Moulier-Boutang, 2002: 195).
Therefore, beyond any government of borders and numbers, we see
that the government of mobility is first and foremost a technology for
managing populations and conducts itself through the containment of
freedom.
 From this perspective, it becomes clear why, as Foucault stresses, the
criminal and the vagabond collapse into the figure of the dangerous
individual: the uncontrolled mobile subject who tries to escape the
norms of society and the regime of productivity and who forces power
to negotiate and reassess the mechanisms of exploitation and to invent
new strategies of control. But beyond that, ‘irregular’ mobility has been
seen by governments and states since the late eighteenth century as the
troubling element of the existing political ontology. Indeed, although


Troubling Mobilities 171
governmental actors frame migration in terms of mere geographic dislo-
cation, practices of movement as resistances to societal normativity are
also transformative forces producing and altering spaces. ‘Migration is
not the evacuation of a place and the occupation of a different one, it is
the making and remaking of one’s own life on the scenery of the world
[ ... ] its target is not relocation but the active transformation of social
space’ (Papadopoulos and Tsianos, 2007).
 Repositioning the autonomy in migration in the light of
migrants’ practices of freedom
 Yet, what cannot pass unnoticed is that all these irregular mobilities
are defined by Foucault as practices of flight, resistance and refusal.
This definition is important in order not to overrate or misunderstand
the notion of autonomy of migration as conceived by the abovemen-
tioned authors and which emerges indirectly also in Foucault’s analyses
on the punitive society. Indeed, the term ‘autonomy’ does not refer, I
would suggest, to a supposed autonomous subject able to sidestep and
exceed the mazes of power, norms and economic exploitation through
a free and insurgent act of migration. On the one hand, such a descrip-
tion would contribute to fostering an image of migrants as heroic or
revolutionary subjects. On the other hand, it would overlook both the
impact of geopolitical and non-geopolitical borders on migrants’ lives
and the anticipatory moves and strategies of capture that today char-
acterize migration governmentality. Moreover, since regulated human
mobility is one of the main conditions for the functioning of capitalism,
migration as such is far from being a revolutionary practice in itself. As
Mezzadra aptly puts it, ‘there is no capitalism without migration, one
could say, with the regime that attempts to control or tame the mobility
of labour playing a strategic role in the constitution of capitalism and
class relations’ (Mezzadra, 2011). From a Foucaultian point of view, it
is important to find the specific moments and contexts in which some
practices of migration have effectively troubled the order of the politics
of mobility. Therefore, the vantage point of the autonomy of migration,
as introduced by Foucault in his account of the disciplining of mobility,
exists on the cusp of a historical and punctual analysis and a general
statement. From an empirical point of view, only sometimes does migra-
tion really interrupt or disrupt the order of mobility; but it remains that
the very existence of a migration regime is itself a set of responsive strat-
egies to practices of freedom that try escape economic or social captures
(Revel, 2008). 10


172 Martina Tazzioli
 Instead, resistances and acts of refusal as migration movements are
always practices of freedom to escape and struggle with existing economic
and social dispositives. This is the reason why more than reading it in
temporal terms – practices of mobility anticipate power’s mechanisms –
the perspective of the autonomy of migration should be better posited
as a vantage point through which to look at the functioning and at the
transformations of the migration regime. Migration policies, techniques
of surveillance and strategies of patrolling represent the most visible and
tangible elements of the multifaceted, frantic attempt to bridle mobility
and turn it into a value. And the excess that some migrations enact,
in relation to the given configuration of power, should not be seen as
an ontological primacy but, rather, as a practice of freedom against the
existing machines of capture and the moral geographies governing
migration population. Ultimately, the political and troubling character
of (some) practices of migration emerges just as far as migration is taken
as a specific kind of social relation and in its imbrication in a certain
struggle field. Thus, it is less migrant labour force as such that is high-
lighted in Foucault’s analysis as a troubling factor than the strategies of
resistance and the practices of freedom enacted by people refusing the
government over conducts and bodies. It follows that by reading prac-
tices of movement not exclusively as a subtraction from control but, in
a broader way, as dissidences and resistances to the moral, economic
and political government over lives allows going beyond the juridical
paradigm and finally ‘cutting off the king’s head’ (Foucault, 1998). The
critique of the binary exclusion/inclusion code goes in hand with the
displacement of the centrality of law with its sorting function – tracing
the boundaries of irregularity: ‘the struggle against coercion is not the
same than overcoming the prohibition [ ... ]. To enact a transgression
means making the law unreal and powerless, for a moment, in a certain
place and for one person; becoming dissident is to attack this coercion’
(Foucault, 2013: 116) and, we can add, to enact a practice of freedom
that is discordant, non-representable into the existing cartography of
the political.
 Recalling Foucault’s definition of practices of simulation in the field
of psychiatry and medicalization as the very ‘underside of psychiatric
power rather than epistemological problem’ (Foucault, 2006: 268), a
similar dislocating move could be suggested in relation to movements.
Indeed, (some) practices of unauthorized mobility are nothing but the
troubling underside of mechanisms of capture over people’s life and time.
Migration categories ultimately aim to discipline bodies and conducts in
spaces, more and beyond them into juridical profiles.


Troubling Mobilities 173
 Notes
 1 .  I want to thank Nicholas De Genova, Sandro Mezzadra and William Walters for
their comments on this chapter and for our fruitful discussions on these themes.
 2 .  Indeed, according to Foucault, critique is an attitude that ‘does not consist in
saying that things aren’t good the way they are. It consists in seeing on what
types of assumptions, of familiar notions, of established, unexamined ways of
thinking the accepted practices are based [ ... ]. Understood in this sense, criticism
is utterly indispensable for any transformation’ (Foucault, 2002b: 456–7).
 3 .  In fact, according to Foucault, the problem is how to articulate the critique of
the technologies of profiling with a possible struggle against them.
 4 .  The functional continuum between the prison and other social institutions of
‘sequestration’, as Foucault puts it, is given also by the techniques of control
on and through the time. In fact, the prison-form and the salary-form share
the sequestration of people’s time. ‘As one gives a salary which corresponds
to the time of labour, conversely one takes the time of freedom as the price to
pay for an infraction [ ... ] what emerges with through these two forms is the
introduction of time into the system of capitalist power and into the penal
system’ (Foucault, 2013: 72–3).
 5 .  ‘The penitentiary is actually a phenomenon that is much larger than the impris-
onment and it is a general dimension of all social controls’ (Foucault, 2013: 104).
 6 .  The excess is determined mainly in relation to the economic necessity (of
labour force) and to the political function of illegalism: when, for economic
or political reasons, certain forms of illegalism become counter-productive,
they start to be sanctioned and banished, and they are framed as immoral
conducts. While tolerated (and necessary), illegalisms are only object of a
juridical sanction that is then not enforced – as they are tolerated – ‘exces-
sive’ illegalisms are instead entrapped in a moral-juridical double that present
them as dangerous and squanderer conducts. But in both cases, as Foucault
stresses, what is at stake is a quite anomalous juridical sanction: actually, these
irregularities are not infractions, since ‘given the necessary freedom of labour
market, it’s impossible to organize the juridical system in a way that [all these
irregularities] can become infractions. Therefore this illegalism spreads at an
infra-legal level’ (Foucault, 2013: 196).
 7 .  Henceforth, all translations from  La Société Punitive are mine.
 8 .  Framing mobility in terms of ‘government of the mob’ allows, I contend, a
re-politicization of that term. In fact, ‘mobility’ has become a buzzword and a
paradigm that nowadays is more and more used to indicate free movements,
and it is often employed in opposition to ‘migration’. On the contrary, the
expression ‘government of the mob’ stresses the disciplinary function of the
politics of mobility and that also alleged free movements are actually chan-
nelled and bridled on the basis of labour regimes and on the basis of the
mechanisms of production of social differences.
 9 .  As Sandro Mezzadra points out, ‘to engage with the autonomy of migration
thus requires a ‘different sensibility’, a different gaze [ ... ]. The autonomy of
migration approach in this regard needs to be understood as a distinct perspec-
tive from which to view the ‘politics of mobility’ – one that emphasizes the
subjective stakes within the struggles and clashes that materially constitute
the field of such a politics (Mezzadra, 2011).


174 Martina Tazzioli
 10 .  As Judith Revel explains, such a conception of power relations entails also a
reformulation of the notion of resistance in terms of ‘productive asymmetry’:
‘Power is at the same time always genitive, managing, parasitical, and always
a reaction, whereas on the other hand the free action of men is primary,
inventive, and given as an action. It is in so far as men invent and open possi-
bilities that power relations can proceed with the capture of this opening.
Or, rather, it is precisely in this differentiation – between a production and a
reproduction, an action and a reaction, a creation and a management, that I
see the dissymmetry’ (Revel, 2008).

Part IV
Politics of Truth


179
 Development, Common-Pool Resources, Environmentality
 One of the most interesting uses of the Foucauldian toolbox in the
last two decades is the famous genealogical inquiry of Arturo Escobar
concerning the discursive formation as well as the political technologies
tied to the paradigm of development (Escobar, 1995; Mezzadra et al.,
2013). Drawing attention to the historical conditions under which the
discourse of development arose after the Second World War, Escobar
stresses the importance of the particular regime of economical truth
that makes many countries appear as ‘underdeveloped areas’ or ‘Third
World’– that is, a large and homogenized range of  abnormalities which,
henceforth, would become a sensible field of intervention through a
multiplicity of governmental technologies. As he puts it,
 Without examining development as discourse we cannot understand
the systematic ways in which the Western developed countries have
been able to manage and control and, in many ways, even create
the Third World politically, economically, sociologically and cultur-
ally; and that although underdevelopment is a very real historical
formation, it has given rise to a series of practices (promoted by the
discourses of the West) which constitute one of the most powerful
mechanisms for insuring domination over the Third World today.
(Escobar, 1984: 384)
11
 Environmentality and Colonial
Biopolitics: Towards a Postcolonial
Genealogy of Environmental
Subjectivities
 Orazio  Irrera


180 Orazio Irrera
 However, Escobar points out that it is not possible to understand the
nexus between knowledge and governmental technologies without
taking into account a wide spectrum of resistances that have enabled
the formation of counter-discourses as well as the emergence of new
forms of political subjectivities. Thus, in order to undertake a genealogy
of development, we have to articulate three levels: (1) the internal struc-
ture of a scientific domain of knowledge with all its related mechanisms
of  véridiction ; (2) the set of procedures and technologies of government
as well as its objects, its aims and the institutions and actors who enact
them; (3) the strategies of resistance as well as the political counter-sub-
jectivations insofar as ruled or dominated subjects are able to both break
the order of power and knowledge and shape relatively autonomous
forms of political and social auto-organization based on a more effective
participation of disadvantaged people.
 In the face of the ongoing obsolescence of the developmental para-
digm implemented by states and certain international organizations
(such as the World Bank or the International Monetary Fund) and
sustained by market-orientated policies, a more participatory and decen-
tralized form of political technology – going also beyond the dichotomy
between public and private system of propriety and management – stems
from the analysis of Common-Pool Resources and Common propriety.
‘Commons’ is a general term used to denote shared resources in which
each stakeholder has an equal interest. Studies on the commons include
the information commons focusing on issues surrounding public
knowledge, the public domain, open science and the free exchange of
ideas – all issues that are at the core of a direct democracy. Common-
pool resources (CPRs) are natural or human-made resources where one
person’s use subtracts from another’s use and where it is often necessary,
but difficult and costly, to exclude other users outside the group from
using the resources (Ostrom, 1990). To date, the majority of research on
CPRs has been in the areas of fisheries, forests, grazing systems, wild-
life, water resources, irrigation systems, agriculture, land tenure and use,
social organization and global commons (climate change, air pollution,
transboundary disputes, etc.). There is a growing corpus of work on ‘new’
or ‘nontraditional’ commons, which focuses on urban commons (apart-
ment buildings, parking spaces, playgrounds, and so on), the Internet,
the electro-magnetic spectrum, genetic data, budgets, etc.
 These resources have long been subjected to overexploitation and
misuse by individuals acting in their own best interest. Conventional
solutions typically involve either centralized governmental regulation


Environmentality and Colonial Biopolitics 181
or the privatization of resources. But, according to Elinor Ostrom, there
is a third approach to resolving the problem of the commons: the design
of durable cooperative institutions, organized and governed by the
resource users themselves. The main focus of problematization of this
institutional perspective concerns ‘how a group of principals who are
in an interdependent situation can organize and govern themselves to
obtain continuing joint benefits when all face temptations to free-ride,
shirk, or otherwise act opportunistically’ (Ostrom, 1990: 29).
 Having provided a brief outline above to the issues of development,
the commons and global resources, I would like to explore two relevant
questions: is it possible to use a Foucauldian grid in order to provide
a deeper historical understanding of the emergence of these forms of
governmental technology concerning CPRs as well as to the related
political subjectivities? Secondly, is it possible to explain how institu-
tional arrangements based on common ownership and management
stem historically from a series of struggles and negotiations between
government and resistances?
 To carry out this form of analysis, I will focus on the question of
the management of an important environmental resource that has
provoked a series of conflicts concerning its use and access. Here, I am
referring to forests, and I will consider in particular the case of the sub-
Himalayan forests in India, specifically those of the Uttarkhand State
with its two divisions, Kumaon and Garhwal. However, in contradis-
tinction to a developmental paradigm, questioning the intricate rela-
tionship between government and resistances entails examining the
shift from intensive exploitation (characteristic of the colonial period)
to a series of political technologies which, in a postcolonial age, aims to
manage this kind of resource through a decentralization of decisional
processes. These processes concern the sets of rules that define the
access, use, exclusion, management, monitoring, sanctioning and arbi-
tration behaviour of users with respect to the forests. Over the last two
decades, an increasing number of studies have been carried out which,
through the analysis of monitoring and enforcing costs, demonstrate
how decentralization towards collective community-based manage-
ment turns out to be more efficient than private forms of ownership
and management or centralized government management (Agrawal,
1996).
 This process of devolution towards communities has already been
considered from a Foucauldian perspective in order to explain the rise of
new forms of political  environmental subjectivities . For this very purpose,


182 Orazio Irrera
Arun Agrawal has developed the notion of  environmentality , defining it
as:
 a framework of understanding in which technologies of self and
power are involved in the creation of new subjectivities concerned
about environment. There is always a gap between effort by subjects
to fashion themselves anew and technologies of power that institu-
tional design seek to consolidate. The realization of particular envi-
ronmental subjectivities that take place within this gap is  as contingent
as it is political . Indeed, it is the recognition of  contingency that makes
it possible to introduce the register of the political in thinking about
the creation of the subject. (Agrawal, 2005b: 166, emphasis mine;
Agrawal, 2005a: 7)
 I believe that in order to radically grasp this historical contingency we
have to go back to the conditions of emergence of this kind of manage-
ment as an event related, on the one hand, to the notion of  milieu
analysed by Foucault with regard to the eighteenth-century European
governmentality:
 [The milieu] is what is needed to account for action at a distance of
one body on another. It is therefore the medium of an action and the
element in which it circulates. It is therefore the problem of circula-
tion and causality that is at stake in this notion of milieu [ ... ]. The
milieu is a set of natural givens – rivers, marshes, hills – and a set of
artificial givens – an agglomeration of individuals, of houses, etcetera.
The milieu is a certain number of combined, overall effects bearing on
all who live in it. It is an element in which a circular link is produced
between effects and causes, since an effect from one point of view will
be a cause from another [ ... ]. What one tries to reach through this
milieu, is precisely the conjunction of a series of events produced by
these individuals, populations, and groups, and quasi natural events
which occur around them. (Foucault, 2007: 36–7)
 On the other hand, the historical contingence of the rise of environ-
mentality also refers to the emergence of a worldwide environmental
stage at the time of the great expansion of colonial empires in the
nineteenth century. Therefore, this historical contingence needs to be
linked with colonial genealogy in which scientific discourses, govern-
mental technologies and political resistances about environment
finally come to define the field of problematization and governmental


Environmentality and Colonial Biopolitics 183
intervention named ‘environmentality’. This is why, following
Agrawal’s ethnographic inquiry, I would like to concentrate on colo-
nial governmentality by sketching out an environmental genealogy
focused on the intertwining between colonial environmental tech-
nologies and the struggles to resist them put into place by peasants.
Indeed, it is only in the light of this colonial genealogy of ecological
struggles that it becomes possible to understand the rise of new envi-
ronmental subjectivities and the CPR’s management in our postcolo-
nial present.
 Indian environmental genealogy: colonial governmentality
and scientific forestry
 Thus, it is important not simply to provide an outline of the history of
political technologies concerning the access and the use of the forests
in sub-Himalayan regions, but also to work through this history in
order to set out the Foucauldian challenges. Nonetheless, in order to
accomplish this goal, it is worth recalling that a genealogical perspec-
tive must show a relative analytical autonomy of the relationships
between knowledge, power and subjectivities from the master Marxist
narration of capitalistic accumulation – especially since many historical
and sociological studies on social protests about sub-Himalayan forests
are often framed through a Marxist analytical framework. This is why,
although the former (for example, the relationships among knowledge,
power and subjectivities) have to be concretely studied in relation to
the mechanisms of economic exploitation, these are not merely the
‘terminal’ manifestation of the latter (for example, the Marxist narra-
tion of capitalistic accumulation).
 In other words, power relations turn out to be immanent to other
types of relations, such as economic ones, since they are the result of
the imbalance of the latter – and, at the same time, constitute the basis
of their transformation. In any case, they cannot be defined as purely
superstructural, but they have a directly productive role of their own
(Foucault, 2003; Balibar, 1989). From this point of view, a Foucauldian
approach meets  political ecology insofar as, according to Joan Martinez
Alier, it can be defined as the study of the ecological distribution of
conflicts (Martinez Alier, 2002), around which – as Foucault puts it –
genealogy constitutes ‘a meticulous rediscovery of struggles and the raw
memory of fights’ (Foucault, 2003: 8).
 I would like to start this environmental genealogy from 1864 when
Britain, after emerging as the world leader in deforestation, as a result


184 Orazio Irrera
of the devastation of its own woods and the forests of its colonies, set
up the Imperial Forest Department with the explicit aim of assessing
the intensive deforestation of the past decades. This purpose required
the introduction of legal mechanisms to assert and safeguard state
control – or, rather, its monopoly – over forests, curtailing the previously
unregulated access enjoyed by rural communities. Madhav Gadgil and
Ramachandra Guha argued that the creation of an appropriate depart-
ment marked a qualitative shift in colonial perceptions of the strategic
value of forests due to its initial attempts at providing a comprehensive
piece of legislation – that is, the Indian Forest Acts (respectively in 1865
and 1878) that would in time serve as a model for forest legislation in
other British colonies (Gadgil and Guha, 1992).
 This discontinuity in colonial governmentality also presupposed the
development of a particular domain of scientific disciplines focused not
on the life of the human species but on the life of the kingdom of  Plantae
(with a wide range of sub-disciplines as forestry, botany, phytopathology,
and so on). Associated with the discipline was a new set of professional-
ized figures, beginning with a set of experts brought in from Germany
for this purpose. By way of example, we might allude here briefly to the
role of Dietrich Brandis and his students. 1
 This scientific domain of knowledge pertaining to forestry allowed the
Forest Department to introduce a series of brutal environmental manip-
ulations which favoured the production and regeneration of strong and
durable timber species such as salt, teak, deodar and chir pine which
could be used for railways sleepers (at that time, the Indian railway
network was rapidly expanding) and for oleo-resin production (Guha,
1989: 43–7). All this was to the detriment of the oaks and the other
broad-leaved species that were more valuable for hill agriculture and
constituted part of the ecological equilibrium implied in the customary
ways in the lives of local populations.
 These interventions that featured what Alfred Crosby named ‘ecolog-
ical imperialism’ (Crosby, 1986) caused an alteration of ecological
succession. This alteration refers to the long-term process of change to
natural vegetation communities. The community of plants in an area
largely determines, for example, which kind of animal life will be part
of that community. In other words, these manipulations caused a real
disjunction between the natural trend of ecological succession, whereby
the oak constituted the so-called climatic stage (the farthest step towards
a type of vegetation adapted to plentiful water supply which the locality
is capable to support) and the imperatives of commercial forestry which
fostered the extension of conifers.


Environmentality and Colonial Biopolitics 185
 This type of scientific forest management based on state monopoly
was brought into effect due to the Indian Forest Acts of 1865 and 1878,
which distributed the responsibility of forest management among
different governmental agencies (mainly the Forest Department and
the Revenue Department), classified the forests and reserved large
areas to commercially valuable species, and which defined (1) a set of
forest offences, (2) a range of prohibited acts (especially in the Reserved
Forests) and (3) a system of fines to apply for each type of violation of
the established act.
 As Agrawal points out, for the hill residents of Kumaon and Garhwal,
who relied on a mixture of livestock rearing and agriculture, these new
rules (1) entailed a set of strong restrictions on customary rights of
lopping trees for fodder or fuel wood, (2) prevented villagers from exer-
cising grazing rights, (3) restricted the use of non-timber forest products,
(4) prohibited the extension of cultivation, (5) enhanced the labour
extracted from the villagers, (6) increased the number of forest guards,
(7) banned the annual practice of burning the forest floor for a fresh
crop of grass and (8) obligated the villagers both to warn authorities of
illegal fires and to collaborate with forest officials in order to extinguish
them (Agrawal, 1996).
 The emergence of political subjectivities: towards an
ethnography of resistance from  dhandak to ‘planned
incendiarism’
 From a Foucauldian perspective, we should draw attention to the materi-
ality of the practices of these villagers that these specific intersections of
power and knowledge, colonial governmentality and scientific forestry
are attempting to shape in order to maximize the process of capital
accumulation. Thus, colonial environmental governmentality emerges
in this way as a ‘conduct of conducts’, setting up processes of normali-
zation in order to determine which conducts are permitted and which
ones are not (Foucault, 2000). These conducts concerned the customary
ways of life, regulating the ecological exchanges between a local rural
population and natural resources. This is the focus of an analysis of
power relations aimed at showing how scientific discourse and various
governmental technologies clash with a series of local knowledges and
social practices comprising what Foucault has referred to as ‘subjected
knowledges’ (Foucault, 2003). In the wake of the seminal research of
Philippe Descola (Descola and Palsson, 1996), Arturo Escobar again
reminds us that


186 Orazio Irrera
 nature exists only in a dense universe of collective representations that
at once grounds different ways of doing things with/around nature.
Succinctly put many communities in the world signify their natural
environment, and then use it, in ways that markedly contrast with
the more commonly accepting ways of seeing nature as a resource
external to humans and which humans can appropriate in any way
they see fit. Not only economic factors and ecological conditions, but
cultural meanings, define the practices that determine how nature is
appropriated and utilized. (Escobar, 2006: 9)
 Consequently, it becomes impossible to speak about nature without
considering the historical and cultural criteria separating what is true,
good and right about it and what is false, wrong and unfair. These
criteria define different Foucauldian ‘regimes of truth’ (Foucault, 2012:
91–9) with regard to nature. The Western colonial regime of truth is
one based on scientific criteria aimed at manipulating nature in order
to assure the exploitation of ecological resources for commercial goals.
This form of technical mastery of nature with its specific regime of truth
produces a normalizing effect that regulates the relationship between
the ecological environment and the material and immaterial existence
of individuals. Since this kind of normalization was translated into a
colonial biopolitics, it came to clash with indigenous regimes of truth
about nature. The latter hinges on other forms of living relationships
with nature and environment with its ethical and political forms of
existence in which knowledge of local environment and cultural mean-
ings (religious, political, and so on) are strictly embedded. Indeed, under
colonization these different ways of understanding, appropriating and
utilizing nature provide specific strategic resources in order to resist the
colonial normative order.
 It is by framing this conceptual and methodological scene, it seems
to me, that we are able to get a better understanding of what Foucault
called ‘the insurrection of subjugated knowledges’ and can subsequently
apply this analytical tool to the forms of resistance that the hill residents
of Kumaon and Garhwal bring into existence. After all, without consid-
ering these sorts of resistances, the internal transformations of both
scientific forestry and political environmental technologies also turn
out to be less perspicuous. However, we should avoid reading this reac-
tivation of ‘local knowledges’ as something homogeneous and always
unchanging; on the contrary, we should focus on the strategic disconti-
nuities in the trajectory of social protests of the Himalayan peasants in
relation to the progressive and increasingly invasive enforcement of the


Environmentality and Colonial Biopolitics 187
colonial order. Thus, the reactivation of popular knowledges comes to
appear as both strategic and modular.
 In Ramachandra Guha’s very relevant study on social protest in the
Himalayas, he stresses sharply the importance of a shift concerning
the forms and strategies of resistance (1989). During an initial period,
starting from the 1878 Forest Act, villagers were limited to the expres-
sion of non-collaborative attitudes against the attempts of colonial
officials to enforce forest rules. For this reason, Guha uses the notions
of ‘everyday forms of peasant resistance’ and of ‘weapons of the weak’
employed by James Scott (1986) and that of ‘avoidance protest’ used
by Michael Adas (1981) in order to envisage a series of non-confronta-
tional strategies of resistance ‘including foot-dragging, false compliance,
feigned ignorance, migration, breaches of the law, and social appeals to
“ customs ”’ (Guha, 1989: 126).
 These practices were codified by an ancient customary idiom of social
protest called  dhandak , which draws its legitimacy from tradition and
does not seek to overthrow the existing social order. Apart from the
aforementioned everyday practices of resistance, even when the rebel-
lion was manifest, it was usually addressed to corrupt or incompetent
officials who had failed to observe the traditional limits of their power.
These mechanisms of protest worked as a safety valve that allowed the
periodic and constructive release of discontent. Guha also argues that
the source of authority of this covenant between ruler and ruled hinged
on the divine kinship of the rulers, a quality peculiar to many tradi-
tional chiefdoms and monarchies. This relationship between ruler and
ruled subjects was marked by a patriarchal style of domination orien-
tated to justice and the protection of subjects (Guha, 1989: 89–90). An
important and distinctive characteristic of  dhandak was the absence
of physical violence: the peasants preferred the temporary abandon
of work in order to claim their rights through demonstrative actions,
such as marches to the capital where masses gathered around a shrine or
demanded audience with the king (Guha, 1989: 67–9).
 Guha notes that around 1910–1911, since the draconian rules enacted
by the Forest Act were strictly enforced, villagers tended to discard the
aforementioned weapons of the weak ‘in favour of more open and
confrontational forms of protest: attacks on channels of communica-
tion, on state buildings, and most frequently on commercial forests
and resin depots’ (Guha, 1989: 126). One of these practices gained great
importance for the colonial authorities: the practice of setting fire to
commercial forests. This strategic reinscription of a traditional practice
(the annual practice of burning the forest floor for a fresh crop of grass)


188 Orazio Irrera
in a new idiom of protest was based on the large and detailed environ-
mental knowledge of local mountains and valleys.
 In the dry years of 1916 and 1921, the colonial surveillance system was
thrown seriously into crisis due to a huge number of widespread fires, a
phenomenon that the authorities called ‘planned incendiarism’ and that
massively damaged commercial timber production and resin extraction,
irremediably compromising the regeneration planning adopted by the
Forest Department. Unable to respond with force, the colonial govern-
ment of Kumaon was compelled to set up the Kumaon Forest Grievances
Committee in 1921 to examine the continuing protests of villagers. After
having examined more than five thousand witnesses, the committee
gave the government a list of recommendations that the latter enacted
especially in relation to two points: (1) de-reserving the larger part of
the newly created Reserved Forests between 1911 and 1917; (2) creating
community forests to be managed under a broad set of rules framed by
government, but for which villagers themselves could craft specific rules
for everyday use according to local conditions.
 Indeed, the 1931 Forest  Panchayat Rules sanctioned the emergence
of rural communities as political subjectivities able to provide forms of
self-government in relation to environmental management. This bundle
of rules was then modified only twice, in 1971 and 1976, constituting
the ground for the majority of the current systems of common-pool
resource management. By sketching out a Foucauldian perspective on
the ethnography of resistance, it is possible to outline how the shifts
within the idiom of protest affect not only the colonial political tech-
nologies by introducing a Forest Grievance Committee and the Forest
Councils Act, but also the scientific domain of forestry by stimulating
important new measures for fire protection which are operative even
today (Guha, 1989: 53).
 Foucauldian perspectives on ethical and political
dimensions of resistance
 In this final section, I would like to revisit a very important question
raised by Guha concerning how to ‘explain the transition from custom
to confrontation in the social history of protest’ (Guha, 1989: 127). It
seems to me that he identifies two important points: (1) The incapacity
of colonial officials to fully decode the idiom of the peasant protest –
that is, that  dhandak idiom that did not aim to overthrow the social
and political order: this incapacity urged colonial authorities to answer
through the use of force and intransigence against the claims of protest.


Environmentality and Colonial Biopolitics 189
The result of this attitude has been a radical change in the peasant percep-
tion both of the overlord and of the paternalistic subjection/protection
covenant between ruled and ruler that was a defining characteristic of
the early native kings. (2) The need felt by the peasants for ‘a moral/
religious sanction for their acts’ (Guha, 1989: 117) affirmed by the active
role of religious figures in the protest and by the fact that villagers often
gathered to demonstrate around a shrine or a holy place.
 Ramachandra Guha does not completely relate these points to each
other, and thus, in order to provide some answers to his above-men-
tioned question, his theoretical and methodological framework of anal-
ysis drives him to refer mainly to the important but problematic notion
for the Subaltern Studies Collective of ‘peasant consciousness’ (Guha,
1983; Spivak, 1986). He frames this notion through a composite interpre-
tative grid hinging, on the one hand on the concept of ‘moral economy’
of the peasant (Thompson ,1971; Scott, 1976; Fassin, 2009) and, on the
other hand, on a Weberian approach to the sociology of domination
(Guha, 1989: 1–8).
 I believe that this framework, although it constitutes a remarkable
perspective for studies on resistance, might be better implemented with
recourse to a Foucauldian mode of analysis, placing the two aforemen-
tioned points into closer proximity in order to provide a more articu-
lated answer to Guha’s question. Starting from these points, we can see
how – in the transition from customary to confrontational strategies of
resistance – the source of power appeared delegitimized, and thereby,
the villagers faced the problem of creating other forms of power rela-
tions and collective subjectivation in order to codify their discontent
and their claims. In other words, this situation entailed the need of
what Ernesto Laclau has called ‘chain of equivalence’ that results by
shaping contingent political forms of subjectivity in function of a wide
and differentiated set of protest claims (Laclau, 2000 and 2005). This
notion, it seems to me, might be very useful to approach the question of
the emergence of a  new idiom of resistance.
 Nonetheless, faced with this kind of problem, on the one hand,
the notion of ‘moral economy’ seems to provide only the possibility
of translating the need of new power configurations, as well as related
forms of resistance, into an archaic idiom of tradition. Now, it is exactly
this possibility that is denied here, and consequently, although we are
in a moment of political contingency, it appears to be quite problem-
atic to explain the emergence of  new forms of political subjectivation in
this way. In other words, if we accept that the villagers’ aspirations to
reacquire their customary rights to the access and use of forests could


190 Orazio Irrera
be framed through the notion of moral economy, then, moral economy
seems unable to explain (1) the strategic trajectory of the practices of
resistance within different configurations of power stemming from
the struggles with colonial governmental authorities and (2) the loss
of the customary perception of the divine overlord to the extent that
customary ways of  making manifest one’s discontent are no longer feasible
in this context.
 On the other hand, by defining the notion of ‘legitimation’ ( Geltung )
based on the probability that rules turn out be concretely observed, the
Weberian approach to the sociology of domination and resistance brings
out the thorny problem of a sociology of action that is entirely prag-
matic and, by definition, excludes from its field of problematization the
question of subjectivation (Weber, 1968: 8–12 ). To provide an example
taken from a very relevant study of James Scott, this approach does not
seem fully able to explain how it is possible to transform the ‘hidden
transcripts’ (a non-confrontational kind of resistance) into the ‘public
transcript’ (a manifest discourse of protest), and it doesn’t seem fully
able to explain how a new form of political subjectivity could emerge
from this passage as a result of a series of political struggles led through
new strategies of resistance (Scott, 1990).
 Thus, framing this scene, I will proceed to explain how Foucault’s
reflections in the two last lectures at the Collège de France might
contribute to our interpretative grid in order to provide more complex
genealogical depth to Guha’s question. In these lectures Foucault focuses
on Greek  parrhesia – the courage of truth-telling – and then he examines
the historical configuration of an important isomorphism between the
government of the self and the government of others (Foucault, 2010).
In this isomorphism, the forms of ethical elaboration of the self make
the individual  bios a surface of emergence of a regime of truth that ties
ethics and politics together through the ability to courageously put his
own life into play and to show other forms of individual and political
existence. Stressing the importance of the ancient Cynics, Foucault
goes further: he indicates how the nexus between the courageous and
dangerous manifestation of truth, on the one hand, and the form one
gives to one’s own existence, on the other, hinges on the capacity to
break out of commonly accepted power relations and the regimes of
truth in order to display the possibility of a political transformation of
the given order (Foucault, 2011).
 I believe that the contingency into which the discontinuity between
custom and confrontation indicated by Guha is folded produces in a


Environmentality and Colonial Biopolitics 191
sense something akin to the chain of equivalence. Nonetheless, unlike
Laclau’s and Mouffe’s notion, the collective subjectivation here does not
seem to presuppose a moral weakness, but on the contrary, it entails a
different political framework. Within this field of historical contingence,
different ways of conceiving of political subjectivation (as particular rela-
tionship between manifestation of truth and forms to give to the ethical
and political existence) competing or partially overlap. Therefore, if colo-
nization could be considered as a movement through which individuals
are subjugated through colonial mechanisms of power that adhere to a
 truth , then we might name  decolonizing alethurgy 2 the movement through
which an individual gives himself or herself the right to challenge truth
(a colonial regime of truth) on its effects of power as well as to question
colonial power on its discourses of truth (Irrera, 2013; Renault, 2012).
Therefore, in order to understand the decolonizing forms of political
transformation able to affect relations of colonial power as well as the
colonial regimes of truth based on a classical Western understanding of
the human – and in our case also environmental – sciences, we can use
the notion of ‘decolonizing alethurgy’ in order to designate forms of
subjectivation that, through a decolonizing regime of truth able to place
the ethics into the politics, oppose the colonial link between power and
truth.
 Thus, a Foucauldian analysis of complex and diversified leadership
formations within the peasant social protest might shed light on how the
constitution of a new form of political subjectivity (with its own strate-
gies of resistance and its own environmental regime of truth) was related
to the ability  of the ethical and political figuration showed and offered by
its leaders. Therefore, the decolonizing alethurgy enacted here by the
leaders of peasant protests seems very different to the Weberian notion
of ‘charismatic authority’ since decolonizing alethurgy hinges more on
a continuous work of ethico-political elaboration of oneself rather than
on personal or temperamental qualities).
 In this way, we need to return to the discontinuities at stake in the
idiom of peasant social protest, reconsidering the role played by reli-
gious leaders, by ex-soldiers – Guha outlined the importance of the
involvement of ex-soldiers that were fierily distinguished in the First
World War – or by the  Kumaon Parishad , a nationalist association mainly
composed of lawyers, journalists and activists. Thanks to these same
interpretative insights, we might also reread the history of the succes-
sive  Chipko Movement as framing the competing ‘discursive strategies’
of the  Chipko leaders, studied extensively by Haripriya Rangan, within


192 Orazio Irrera
the wider context of a genealogy of the relationships between (environ-
mental) truth manifestation and forms of existence (Rangan, 2000).
 I would like to conclude by noting how in bringing decolonizing
alethurgy into play, we might also go further in relation to the current
paradigm of governmentality studies interested more in technolo-
gies of power and less in resistance and ethic-political subjectivation.
Using these last Foucauldian reflections, we might return to the initial
assumptions of Arun Agrawal concerning the gap between technologies
of power enacted and consolidated by governmental interventions and
the work of the self upon oneself in order to elaborate new forms of
subjectivity (environmental subjectivity). Within this gap, as contin-
gent as it is political, the act of recognizing the historical contingency
means introducing the ethical constitution of the self into the register of
the political in order to think about the creation of the environmental
subjectivities.
 By promoting the study of the genealogy of environmentality in its
historical contingence as colonial event, we intend to open further
possibilities with Foucault but also beyond Foucault, to analyse and crit-
icize the current issues concerning environmental history. More gener-
ally, dealing with environmentality in this way proposes that discursive
proliferation about the commons and the resource management (as
CPRs) could be better understood if we are able to recognize the emer-
gence of these discourses and governmental technologies in terms of
their historical contingence. Finally, questioning this contingence from
our postcolonial present means to attempt to understand genealogically
the multiplicity of struggles and resistances, from which these discourses
and practices have stemmed as global events appearing for the first time
at nineteenth century on the world stage of colonial empires. This is
why, today, one of the most interesting challenges concerning the use
of Foucauldian toolbox consists in the arduous task of expanding its
genealogical analysis of European biopolitics to a wider field, that of a
colonial biopolitics.
 Notes
 1 .  Sir Dietrich Brandis, (1824–1907) was the most important German forester
who worked with the British Imperial Forestry Service in colonial India. He
is considered the father of tropical forestry. He influenced the forestry move-
ment in the United States by mentoring Gifford Pinchot, Henry Graves, others
who came to study with him in Germany, and others through his voluminous
correspondence with many other men such as Charles Sprague Sargent and
Franklin Hough involved in establishing the US national forest system.


Environmentality and Colonial Biopolitics 193
 2 .  This notion has been coined with Matthieu Reanult in the frame of the seminar
we have been holding since 2012 at the Collège International de Philosophie
and at the Université Paris-Est Créteil, see. http://decolonisationsavoirs.word-
press.com/.
