195
 In the last few years, the concept of ‘spiritual exercises’ has been placed
at the core of an interesting philosophical debate, developed especially
in contemporary French philosophy. This debate pivots on a possible
refashioning of our moral thought (Davidson, 1995, 1997; Laugier,
2010). The specificity of this reflection consists in the way in which it
connects an ancient practice to our present and effects a reconceptuali-
zation of spiritual exercises in order to elaborate a contemporary ethics.
This renewal of moral philosophy strongly diverges from the tradi-
tional French reflection on ethics. Indeed, French philosophers such
as Emmauel Lévinas, Jacques Derrida and Paul Ricœur (Lévinas 1968;
Derrida and Dufourmantelle, 2000; Ricœur, 1992) developed an ethics
rooted in the concept of the ‘priority of the other’. Instead, this new
moral reflection revolves around the concept of the ‘self’ and its starting
point is a work of self-transformation, in which the concept of ‘other’
plays a very limited role.
 This chapter aims to analyse two different ways of reconceptualizing
spiritual exercises for rethinking the horizon of French moral philos-
ophy: these are reflections made by Pierre Hadot and Michel Foucault.
Firstly, I will consider Hadot, who coined the concept of spiritual
exercises with the primary purpose of gaining a better understanding
of the ancient world; then I will focus on Foucault, who started with
this concept and transformed it in terms of the contiguous notion of
‘techniques of the self’. The chapter will take into account Hadot’s and
Foucault’s different political and philosophical paths, which led them
12
 Pierre Hadot and Michel Foucault
on Spiritual Exercises: Transforming
the Self, Transforming the Present
 Laura  Cremonesi


196 Laura Cremonesi
to study and reactualize spiritual exercises. In fact, some divergences
in their theoretical and political backgrounds are crucial in explaining
significant differences in their reinterpretations of spiritual exercises and
in the moral philosophy they elaborate. One of the main arguments
of this chapter is that Hadot and Foucault did not intend to translate
directly and uncritically the ancient practice of spiritual exercises into
our present; rather, they both suggested a useful redefinition, allowing
us to enact today a renewed practice of the self. In conclusion, this
chapter will underline some relevant differences between Hadot’s and
Foucault’s thoughts concerning the relation between spiritual exercises,
power relations and subjectivation. Hadot and Foucault were led to
study the ancient world and its practices via two different paths. Pierre
Hadot had a solid theological background, as he studied at the Grand
Séminaire de Reims, pursued philosophical studies partially under the
tutelage of Jean Wahl and developed a particular interest in existen-
tialism, thanks to his acquaintance with Gabriel Marcel. Afterwards he
switched to a more historical-philological work under the guidance of
Paul Henry, beginning his research with a critical edition of the work
of Marius Victorinus. 1 Foucault’s philosophical background is certainly
better known: sympathetic to Marxism and structuralism at first, he
then rapidly abandoned both and pursued his own original thought
as can already be seen in his early works, which cannot be categorized
according to any philosophical school. 2
 The similar historical and philosophical context has certainly made
the account of spiritual exercises proposed by Foucault and Hadot very
similar in many aspects. It is worth noting that the reading of Foucault
owes much to Hadot. Foucault explicitly states that he began to spot the
presence of spiritual exercises in the ancient world thanks to Hadot’s
works. However, the difference in the philosophical routes followed
by the two authors resulted in two markedly different interpretations
of the spiritual exercises due to their dissimilar philosophical orienta-
tion and also to their different theoretical aims. This chapter takes as
its focus both the similarities and differences present in Hadot’s and
Foucault’s interpretations of spiritual exercises. The goal here is to inves-
tigate the significance, if any, that the accounts of spiritual exercises
provided by Hadot and Foucault might have for the ways of dealing
with philosophy.
 Pierre Hadot
 The concept of spiritual exercises was introduced by Hadot in 1977, in
an article entitled ‘Spiritual Exercises’ (Hadot, 1995a). 3 In conversations


Pierre Hadot and Michel Foucault on Spiritual Exercises 197
Hadot clearly explained how this work had a twofold starting point,
philosophical and a historical-philological. This twofold starting point
reflects Hadot’s joint background, which was both historical-philolog-
ical and philosophical. 4
 From a more philological point of view, the main concern was related
to the very nature of the ancient philosophical texts. Hadot stated
that his studies were led by the need to reach a better understanding
of the ancient texts, which would enable him to better comprehend
their nature. If ancient texts seem to present several inconsistencies and
contradictions, that is because they’re read through a modern philo-
sophical lens, and thus they’re conceived of in terms of a complete expo-
sition of systematic theories. But, as Hadot suggests, in order to restore
their original signification and their coherence, ancient texts should be
located in their historical and social context, where they played a specific
role. Rather than forming a systematic construction, they formed part of
a pedagogic practice, aiming at gradually leading the interlocutor down
a certain path, modifying not only his way of thinking but also his way
of living and of being (Hadot, 1995a). Indeed, in Hadot’s opinion, when
philosophy emerged in the Western world with the figure of Socrates, it
was originally and radically connected with life, and it was meant as a
practice to help men orientate their way of conducting themselves and
modify their vision of things. In a way, we can say that for Hadot philos-
ophy, in its original vocation, was a way of life that one could freely
decide to follow. 5 The idea that philosophical texts were connected to
a way of life takes us to the second reason that led Hadot to formulate
the notion of ‘spiritual exercise’, which is grounded on his philosophical
orientation.
 Since the beginning of his philosophical career, Hadot had been
attracted by a kind of philosophy which was directly connected with life
itself and the way of conducting it, instead of constituting an abstract
construction of theories. Before shifting his attention to classical
studies, he located traces of this kind of philosophy in twentieth-cen-
tury thought – partially in existentialism and particularly in the work of
Henry Bergson. According to Hadot, in Bergson’s thought it is possible
to identify a clear way of conceiving of philosophy as a choice and as
a transformation in one’s perception of reality (Hadot, 2009: 121–44).
Nevertheless, it is in Ancient Greek and Roman thought that Hadot
finds the roots of this conception of philosophy as a path leading to a
specific way of life. In the ancient world, this philosophical route was
gradual and strewn with ‘exercises’ whose traces are identifiable in most
of the ancient philosophical texts and which find their best definition
in the expression ‘spiritual exercises’.


198 Laura Cremonesi
 Despite their great doctrinal differences, the Hellenistic and Roman
philosophical schools agreed on the same vision of the aim and role of
philosophy. Philosophical teaching was meant to help the individual
to turn from a way of life dominated by passions, fears and absence of
freedom and serenity to a more authentic one. This change had the char-
acteristics of a true and proper conversion, which implied a deep trans-
formation of perspective and a way of being for the individual. It was
a passage from a partial vision of the world dominated by passions to a
more universal one where the individual raised himself to a rational and
objective point of view (Hadot 1995a). This change could be obtained
and maintained due to the constant practice of certain exercises such as,
for instance, the deep assimilation of a rule of life [ kanon ] which could
help the individual in reaching and maintaining a universal perspective.
For the Stoics, for instance, the rule of life consisted in the distinction
between what depended on the will and freedom of man – in respect
to his moral actions – and what did not depend on it. Anything not
dependent upon it is without value and should thus be accepted in its
entirety as part of the rational universe.
 In order to keep this vision alive, it is necessary to maintain full and
constant attention on the present moment, in order to evaluate at every
single instant what depends on oneself and what, instead, should be
accepted with serenity. This inner attitude, according to which the indi-
vidual makes an effort to keep this point of view, causes a rising to a
different perspective that leads to a vision of the world under the light
of the universal reason which, according to the Stoics, rules the cosmos.
Through such effort, men can partially take part in it. The acceptance of
every single instant as part of the universe animated by reason leads to
the comprehension of the inestimable value of the present and, conse-
quently, to the appraisal of only those joys and inalterable serenity
which human beings can experience.
 Although the conceptual basis might differ, the change of perspec-
tive which causes a transformation in the way of being and living is the
aim of every philosophical school. Hadot locates a change of perspec-
tive in Socrates, where the Greek philosopher invites Athenian citi-
zens to pay attention to themselves, in the Platonic dialogues, in the
Epicurean schools as well as in Plotinus. The work of Marcus Aurelius’
Meditations acquires coherence when it is interpreted as a collection of
exercises aimed at helping the author to gain and maintain the correct
vision of the world in the most difficult of situations. 6 Rereading ancient
theoretical systems within the framework of ancient thought enables


Pierre Hadot and Michel Foucault on Spiritual Exercises 199
us to re-evaluate them in terms, according to Hadot, of an element able
to guide the philosopher’s way of being and to shape his vision of the
world. Physics, for instance, could be practised as the most important
spiritual exercise in terms of achieving a change of perspective and
raising the soul towards a universal vision of things (Hadot 1995b).
 It is now possible then to understand the importance of the concept
of spiritual exercises in order to attain an accurate and philological
reading of the classical thought. Thanks to this concept, Hadot renews
the interpretation of the philosophical texts which can now be read
under a new light, identifying in them the elements of the practice of
the spiritual exercises. From this perspective, they acquire a new inner
coherence as they are understood in the light of their role and of their
function as elements of a philosophical practice connected with the way
of life. Hadot also renews our perception of ancient philosophy which
now appears to us in its original and true vocation as an instrument
for orientating people in their way of life and guiding them towards a
conversion of their way of being and perceiving.
 Michel Foucault
 This interpretation of the ancient philosophy and texts developed by
Hadot deeply influenced the work of Michel Foucault, who, as is well
known, developed an interest in the ancient world towards the end of
the 1970s, in almost the same period when Hadot published  Spiritual
Exercises . Foucault’s starting point was not the same as Hadot’s, here. He
wasn’t directed by historical-philological concerns but by genealogy. 7 As
is well known, Foucault’s historical method is the result of his concern
for the present. Genealogy aims at tracing the emergence of the appa-
ratus of power and knowledge which characterize our present moment.
His interest in the ancient world is no exception here and should be
located within this historical-philosophical perspective.
 While the aim here is not to offer an exhaustive overview of Foucault’s
interpretation of the ancient world, now widely read and discussed
thanks to the gradual publication of his last courses at the Collège de
France, it is useful, nevertheless, to acknowledge two main trajectories
in these readings, corresponding to two interwoven genealogies. The
first trajectory corresponds to the research Foucault dedicated to the
experience of the  aphrodisia in the Ancient Greek and Roman world and
which he dealt with in the second and third volumes of  The History
of Sexuality (Foucault, 1992 and 1990). Such research can be read as a


200 Laura Cremonesi
genealogy of the subject of desire and modern sexuality. In these texts,
Foucault locates the point of emergence of the subject of desire in early
Christianity, not the ancient world. The Ancient Greek and Roman
worlds did not have the same experience of sexuality since pleasure and
desire were positioned within a different configuration of power and
knowledge.
 Alongside his history of sexuality, Foucault also studied the ancient
world from another point of view, closer to Pierre Hadot’s perspective.
In his last courses at the Collège de France (Foucault, 2005, 2010 and
2012) Foucault analyses a number of practices which can be brought
together using the concept of ‘care of the self’ through which indi-
viduals in the Hellenistic, Ancient Greek and Roman worlds strived to
modify their way of being. The similarity to Hadot’s analysis is evident:
spiritual exercises, a main characteristic of ancient philosophy, are read
by Foucault as common practices diffuse within ancient society, found
not only in philosophy but also in the concomitant fields of ethics and
medicine. Foucault shares with Hadot the idea that, through these exer-
cises, an individual could have a proper and true conversion in his way
of being, way of living and vision of the world. According to Foucault,
though, the impact of these practices is further reaching. They are to be
considered as techniques able to not only effect a change in one’s life
and attitude but also act upon the very forms of ancient subjectivity.
What for Hadot were spiritual exercises, for Foucault are ‘techniques
of the self’ as their field of action is directly the self’s relationship to
itself.
 It is here that we find the second genealogy underpinning Foucault’s
interpretation of the ancient works. At stake is the history of the modern
subject and of the modes of constitution of subjectivity. 8 In this way, he
gives a historical and genealogical consistency to his theoretical asser-
tions on the nature of the subject. As is well known, Foucault repeatedly
refused the essential and transcendental nature of the subject, looking
at it in terms of a function, always produced differently according to
the way in which the epistemic configurations change over time.
Looking back at the ancient world, he focuses on the effective history
of the modes of constitution of subjectivity, in its relation to power and
knowledge. Therefore, he clearly and definitely interprets subjectivity
as a form, as the self’s relationship to the self, constantly modified by
historical circumstances. Thus, for Foucault there are techniques dealing
with the self which are historically created and historically modified,
which, while they’re closely related, differ from techniques of power


Pierre Hadot and Michel Foucault on Spiritual Exercises 201
and knowledge. The specificity of the techniques of the self is clearly
described by Foucault in a text called ‘Techniques of the Self’:
We must understand that there are four major types of technologies
[ ... ]: technologies of production, which permit us to produce, trans-
form or manipulate things; technologies of sign systems, which permit
us to use signs, meanings, symbols, or signification; technologies of
power, which determine the conduct of individuals and submit them
to certain ends or domination [ ... ]; technologies of the self, which
permit individuals to effect by their own means or with the help of
others a certain number of operations on their own bodies and souls,
thoughts, conduct, and way of being, so as to transform themselves
in order to attain a certain state of happiness, purity, wisdom, perfec-
tion, or immortality. (Foucault, 1988: 18)
 These techniques are historical and for this reason it is possible to trace
their genealogy. Thus, Foucault uncovers a kind of ‘prehistory’ of current
techniques of subjectivation in early Christianity. He also locates in this
historical period the emergence of the subject of desire and the produc-
tion of various elements which characterize the modern apparatus of
sexuality. So, Foucault was brought back to the ancient world by two
different yet interlinked genealogies. Moreover, by involving new tech-
niques of power and new practices of truth, the emergence of a new
conception of desire produces a new form of subjectivity. Foucault
seems to have been led to interpret the ancient world in terms of spir-
itual exercises by a genealogical concern, in the same way that Hadot
was driven in this direction by a philological starting point. However,
Foucault’s interest in ancient techniques of the self also depends on a
more strictly philosophical and ethical concern. In order to understand
the origin of this philosophical and ethical concern, we should briefly
revisit the features of Foucault’s analytics of power, which he developed
in the 1970s (Foucault 1977, 1998).
 Many commentators have criticized Foucault for his conception of
power, which seems to be unable to make room for any kind of volun-
tary action or reaction. In Foucault’s account, relations of power seem to
permeate the whole field of possibilities of action. In fact, by producing
the current systems of knowledge and truth, they seem to abort every
attempt to oppose power with a free, non-ideological and disinterested
search for the truth. Moreover, since the eighteenth century, the disci-
plinary apparatus of knowledge and power produces what Foucault calls


202 Laura Cremonesi
a function of ‘individualization’; this means that the ‘individual’, which
classical political theory conceives as the elementary and primary entity
of society, is nothing other than a result or an effect of power. And,
since the ‘individual’ is fabricated by an apparatus of power, it becomes
evident that he or she cannot be conceived as an inviolable nucleus
which can be opposed to power. The idea of limiting power by way of
the individual and their natural rights is, in Foucault’s view, a political
illusion that should be abandoned.
 In his book on Foucault, Gilles Deleuze clearly outlines the philosoph-
ical and political effects involved in Foucault’s conception of power.
With reference to the first volume of  The History of Sexuality , Deleuze
writes:
 What happened during the fairly long silence following  The History
of Sexuality ? Perhaps Foucault felt slightly uneasy about the book:
had he not trapped himself within the concept of power relations?
He himself put forward the following objection: ‘That’s is just like
you, always with the same incapacity to cross the line, to pass over
to the other side  ... it is always the same choice, for the side of power,
for what power says or of what it causes to be said.’ [ ... ] He might
equally have added that the diffuse centres of power do not exist
without points of resistance that are in some way primary; and that
power does not take life as its objective without revealing or giving
rise to a life that resists power; and finally that the force of the outside
continues to disrupt the diagrams and turn them upside down. But
what happens, on the other hand, if the transversal relations of
resistance continue to become restratified, and to encounter or even
construct knots of power? (Deleuze, 1988: 94)
 In this striking passage, Deleuze brilliantly captures the moment in
which Foucault realized the need to slightly modify the direction of
his philosophical itinerary, and thus, Deleuze/Foucault opened up a
new realm of analysis. Many commentators read this shift in Foucault’s
thought as a real turn. It cannot be denied that after the publication of
the first volume of  The History of Sexuality in 1976, Foucault definitively
shifts his focus from the domain of modern history to the ancient world.
However, I think that rather than radically changing the philosophical
horizon of his research, Foucault simply widens his understanding of
the concept of resistance. In his text, Deleuze suggests that Foucault’s
research on the ancient world and subjectivity can be understood as an
attempt to ‘cross the line’ (Deleuze, 1988: 95) and to open up another


Pierre Hadot and Michel Foucault on Spiritual Exercises 203
dimension besides the domain of power and knowledge. According to
Deleuze, the reason why Foucault draws his attention to the modes of
subjectivation is that this new dimension has great capacity for setting
up resistance to power. Due to its relational nature, the field of subjec-
tivity is open to a number of transformations and modifications, which
enables it to always start again within the strategic field of power and
knowledge.
 Foucault’s interest in this new dimension must surely therefore be read
in relation to the issue of resistance. However, in my opinion, Deleuze
misunderstands Foucault’s purpose when he suggests that, in Foucault’s
account of the relationship between power and resistance, there’s always
a ‘force of the outside’ that ‘continues to disrupt the diagrams’ (Deleuze,
1988: 94). Foucault never states the existence of an ‘outside’ to power.
The new realm of analysis opened up by Foucault must therefore be read
as located entirely in the strategic field of power relations. Thus, subjec-
tivation is understood by Foucault as a form of resistance to certain
specific and historical power relations. Like all forms of resistance, it
does not find a way out from the domain of power but rather acts by
modifying and inverting the current relations of power. However, in
this passage Deleuze aptly points out that the opening of a new realm
of analysis emerges from the need, for Foucault, to ‘cross the line’ and
to leave behind a too narrow and fixed conception of power. Here, I’d
like to underline two aspects of Foucault’s need to ‘cross the line’ at the
end of the 1970s.
 The first point concerns Foucault’s uneasiness about the outcomes of
his analytics of power. This uneasiness doesn’t come from the fact that
power doesn’t offer ways out; the uneasiness instead emerges from the
acknowledgement that this conception of power lacks the consideration
of a significant dimension, that is, the dimension of subjectivity and tech-
niques of the self. Since modern individualization is a historical effect of
specific disciplinary techniques, it should be possible, as a consequence,
to create different forms of action upon the way of being of individuals
and to create new techniques of the self-acting towards different goals,
in relation to other modes of knowledge and in a different relation to
power. Thus, in Foucault’s view, we could produce, by our own means,
new techniques of subjectivation. But not only does Foucault suggest the
potential constitution of new techniques: he also identifies a historical
and concrete domain, the Ancient Greek and Roman worlds, in which a
set of techniques of the self, dissimilar from disciplinary ones, has actu-
ally been developed.


204 Laura Cremonesi
 The second point concerns the relationship between Foucault’s
analytics of power and the opening of this new space, constituted by
the subjectivity and the techniques of the self. In a way, the possibility
of opening this new realm of analysis is an implicit consequence of
Foucault’s relational conception of power. In 1976, in the first volume
of  The History of Sexuality , Foucault clearly stated that power takes effect
in only a strategic field, that is, it is always permeated by some forms of
resistance. In a well-known passage of  The Will to Knowledge , Foucault
writes:
 Where there is power, there is resistance and yet, or rather conse-
quently, this resistance is never in a position of exteriority in relations
to power. Should it be said that one is always ‘inside’ power, there is
no ‘escaping’ it, there is no absolute outside where it is concerned,
because one is subject to the law in any case? Or that, history being
the ruse of reason, power is the ruse of history, always emerging the
winner? This would be to misunderstand the strictly relational char-
acter of power relationships. Their existence depends on a multi-
plicity of points of resistance: these play the role of adversary, target,
support, or handle in power relations. These points of resistance are
present everywhere in the power network. (Foucault, 1998: 95)
 This passage clearly shows that the space in which Foucault located his
analysis of the techniques of the self was already implicit in its conception
of power. 9 Therefore, in order to shift to the analysis of the techniques
of the self, Foucault does not need to radically change his philosophical
point of view but simply needs to widen his understanding of the way
in which power acts. If power relations take effect on our way of consti-
tuting ourselves, this means that the constitution of the self can become
a starting point for a resistance which inverts or modifies power’s way of
acting. So, it is in this space, implicit in Foucault’s conception of power,
that he locates the opening of this new realm of analysis, which revolves
around a work that we can perform on ourselves, in order to modify our
way of being and conduct. Consequently, this work on the self appears
to Foucault as a response to the relations of power which, over the last
two centuries, have given a specific form to modern subjectivity. From
this point of view, a new economy of the techniques of the self could
produce a new constitution of the self. This new constitution of the self
is not ‘outside’ the field of power but, rather, consists of a response to
the current techniques of power and to the history which led us towards
our current way of being.


Pierre Hadot and Michel Foucault on Spiritual Exercises 205
 Thus, Foucault’s interest in spiritual exercises arises from a twin concern
which is genealogical and philosophical at the same time. He is led to
the Ancient Greek  askesis for two main reasons. His aim is to locate the
moment in which the form of our current subjectivity emerges, but he
also recognizes in the realm of techniques of the self a possible response
to the way in which we are subjugated to a specific apparatus of power
and knowledge. It is an ethical response, which assumes the voluntary
choice of transforming the self as a philosophical and political task in
order to resist current configurations of power.
 Conclusion
 From this overview of Foucault and Hadot’s way of thinking about spir-
itual exercises, we can conclude that their interpretations of ancient
exercises are both strictly linked to our present and, also, that they
suggest a kind of reactivation of ancient experience. I’d like to conclude
by analysing in more detail the way in which Hadot and Foucault have
both produced, each in their own way, a reactivation of spiritual exer-
cises. According to Hadot, there is a common thread running throughout
the history of philosophy, from antiquity to the present day, in which
philosophy is conceived and presented as a way of life. On several occa-
sions, Hadot provides a long list of thinkers who have maintained this
tradition: Montaigne, Goethe, Thoreau, Bergson, Heidegger, Merleau-
Ponty and the existentialists. All of them conceived of philosophy as
an  askesis , as a series of exercises which involve a direct relationship
between one’s thought and one’s own life.
 This conception of philosophy has persisted, carrying with it an
attitude which first emerged with Socrates. In Hadot’s view, the nature
of this attitude can be revealed by an attentive reading of ancient
texts or texts belonging to this tradition. Moreover, the very reading
of ancient texts is, in and by itself, already a spiritual exercise and
a first step towards developing this particular attitude (Hadot 2010:
26–31). I have already described the attitude that we can gain in this
exercise of reading which entails a difficult transformation of our
vision of things, in order to reach a universal perspective. For Hadot,
the present value of this attitude is self-explanatory and enjoys some
kind of immediate relevance to existence. This perspective is clearly
shown by the fact that, according to Hadot, this attitude and the
ethical modification of our perspective is our only means of achieving
scientific objectivity and of conducting an objective interpretation of
the ancient texts. The fact that this objectivity is, indeed, an ethical


206 Laura Cremonesi
virtue is clearly stated by Hadot in a number of texts (Hadot, 2009:
61–74; Hadot, 2010: 28–30).
 But, how can we reach and maintain this attitude and this universal
perspective? What kind of exercises should we practice? Do we need to
choose between Stoic exercises and Epicurean ones? And, if we practice
Stoic and Epicurean exercises, does this mean that we must embrace the
Stoic or Epicurean theoretical framework?
 Hadot’s answer to these questions is quite clear: in order to practice
spiritual exercises, we do not need to adhere to any ancient philo-
sophical school. This would be a dangerous form of anachronism and
a misleading way of interpreting ancient philosophy. This fundamental
spiritual attitude, which consists in attaining the universal perspective
and in modifying our vision of things, is not necessarily linked to the
Stoic philosophy or the Epicurean philosophy; it runs through a great
part of occidental thought and in some literary and aesthetic experi-
ences – in Goethe, Klee or Cézanne, for instance – (Hadot, 1995c). What
Hadot suggests is to practice a kind of ‘eclecticism’ and to consider the
ancient schools as useful laboratories, from which we can draw texts,
practices or exercises (Hadot, 2010: 34; Hadot, 2009: 102–3). Most
important for Hadot is the need to keep this practice alive as an experi-
ence which can teach us how to realize a transformation of our way of
being, conducting and considering things, with the help of some exer-
cises that we could practice autonomously and without any reference
to an authority but in connection with a philosophical tradition. Here,
one could object that, in his definition of this fundamental spiritual
attitude, Hadot refers to Stoic concepts, such as universal reason and a
Stoic way of conceiving of nature and necessity, which have little appli-
cation within our own belief systems. In fact, for various reasons, it is
difficult to extract spiritual exercises and their aim from the theoretical
context in which they emerged and to separate them from the Stoic
concept of universal reason. However, it is perhaps possible to main-
tain, independently from its historical and philosophical context, the
idea of philosophy as a way of life and exercise, capable of modifying
our way of being, conducting and existing. In this sense, philosophical
activity could be understood as a way of elaborating an autonomous and
non-prescriptive ethics. And that is exactly what this long philosoph-
ical tradition transmitted to us and the practical elements that Foucault
found in the ancient world.
 For Foucault, these spiritual exercises can be reactivated in the
present, although it is not so evident how one can achieve this task.
Without any doubt, Foucault is not interested in an attitude related to


Pierre Hadot and Michel Foucault on Spiritual Exercises 207
the universal perspective; nor can he adhere to a conception of the self
rooted in the concepts of authenticity and inauthenticity, which we
can easily find in Hadot. From this point of view, Foucault seems more
inclined to eradicate spiritual exercises from the theoretical context
from which they emerged and yet to maintain the very idea of exercise,
as an ethical practice of self-transformation. However, for Foucault, the
goal of spiritual exercises that we can practice today bears not only an
ethical dimension. This is the most relevant difference with respect to
the way in which Foucault and Hadot re-actualize the practice of spir-
itual exercises.
 From Hadot’s perspective, spiritual exercises act upon the way of being
of individuals and realize a modification of their whole personality. This
constitutes a spiritual and ethical modification in which the political
dimension is not directly involved. One could say that the virtue of
scientific objectivity, that is, one of the goals of spiritual exercises,
certainly has positive effects in the political realm. That there exists a
relationship between the universal perspective and the political domain
is clearly stated by Hadot in a number of passages of the conversations.
But if we look at the examples given by Hadot, we can easily under-
stand that the political benefit is always a secondary effect. In Hadot’s
account, the main goal of spiritual exercises is an ethical transformation
of the self.
 Instead, in Foucault’s view, the political dimension is immediately
linked to the field of the techniques of the self. As mentioned before,
Foucault’s interest in techniques of the self arises from a specific concep-
tion of power, and he forms a genealogy of the way in which we have
been led to constitute ourselves as subjugated subjects, in relation to an
apparatus of power and knowledge. Thus, the theoretical horizon on
which Foucault locates his interpretation of spiritual exercises is above
all and entirely a philosophical and political one. Moreover, for Foucault,
today we can practice techniques of the self only as a voluntary choice
of modifying or inverting some of the effects of power. In fact, the work
upon the self is understood by Foucault as a form of resistance which,
in order to produce a new economy of subjectivation, must involve, at
the same time, an action of desubjectivation. The work upon the self
is always rooted in a critical attitude towards power, which is both the
effect and condition of the practice of self-transformation.
 The goal of spiritual exercises is not only the transformation of the self
by the self, but also entails a more general modification of the current
configuration of power, knowledge and techniques of the self. Unlike
Hadot, Foucault extracts spiritual exercises from their historical context


208 Laura Cremonesi
and maintains only the idea of an  askesis , conceived as a political and
ethical task of critique against power relations.
 Notes
 1 .  On Pierre Hadot’s background, see Hadot, 2009.
 2 .  On Michel Foucault’s background, see Eribon, 1991 and Defert, 2001.
 3 .  On spiritual exercises and, in general, on the philosophical aspects of Hadot’s
works, see Davidson, 1995 and Davidson, 1997.
 4 .  On the relationship between Hadot’s background and the concept of ‘spiritual
exercises’, see Balaudé, 2010.
 5 .  That ancient philosophy was not only animated by the concern for the way of
life is clearly shown by Hadot, who underlines how for many ancient philoso-
phers the concern for the construction of logoi was stronger than the care for
the way of life. See Hadot, 2010.
 6 .  On the correct way of reading  Meditations by Marcus Aurelius, see Hadot,
1998.
 7 .  On genealogy, see Foucault, 2000.
 8 .  These two genealogies are strictly connected: in the two last volumes of  The
History of Sexuality , Foucault clearly shows the role played by techniques of the
self in the constitution of  aphrodisia .
 9 .  On the relationship between these three domains (power, knowledge and
techniques of the self), see Davidson, 1985 and Davidson, 1994.

210
 Introduction: decentring the culture of confession
 In the inaugural speech of Michel Foucault’s 1984 Lectures at the Collège
de France,  The Courage of Truth , he traces the process through which he
came to problematize  parrhesia . He explains why and how he replaced
the question of ‘epistemological structures’ with that of ‘alethurgic
forms’; in other words, how he replaced the problem of truth and ‘true
discourse’ with that of the ‘production of truth’ and ‘the act by which
it is manifested’. Foucault recounts his shift from an exploration of the
practices and discourses of truth about the subject to a study of ‘the
discourse of truth which the subject is likely and able to speak about
himself’ (Foucault, 2011: 3) – hence, the attention paid by Foucault
to a range of practices such as avowal, confession or the examination
of conscience. This shift occurred in the first volume of  The History of
Sexuality ,  The Will to Knowledge : ‘Since the Middle Ages at least, Western
societies have established the confession as one of the main rituals we
rely on for the production of truth’ (Foucault, 1998: 58). Confession,
spontaneous or extorted, ‘became one of the West’s most highly valued
techniques for producing truth’: ‘Western man has become a confessing
animal’ (59). In each of these statements, Foucault precisely locates his
object of inquiry as if, here more than elsewhere, he had to limit the
(geographical) scope of his own discourse; as if the very practice and
concept of ‘confession’ threatened to be confined to the West. The same
applies to Foucault’s 1981 Lectures at the Catholic University of Leuven,
 Wrong-Doing, Truth-Telling ( Mal faire, dire vrai ): ‘If we consider only “our”
societies –  the Christian Western societies – it seems to me that we could,
without too many speculations, speak of a massive growth of confes-
sion’ (Foucault, 2013: 7, emphasis added).
13
 A Decolonizing Alethurgy: Fanon
after Foucault 1
 Matthieu  Renault


A Decolonizing Alethurgy 211
 In this regard, it is important to take into account the ‘history of the
present’ in which Foucault’s reflections on confession are embedded.
This is in particular the history of the aftermath of the Algerian War
which, through the systematic use of torture, had given rise to a literally
monstrous growth of the practice of extorting confessions – which were
not confined to colonialism in Algeria, as evidenced by the so-called Trial
of the parliamentarians that took place in Madagascar after the insurrec-
tion of 29 March 1947 (Vidal-Naquet, 1972). However, below the limit-
experience of torture, one can hypothesize that the daily mechanisms
of colonial power themselves exhibited an authentic will for the colo-
nized’s confession and that confession was thus a crucial instrument of
colonial subjection. The question is: can we speak of a colonial culture
of confession? Answering this question does not involve scrutinizing
confession in ‘other’ non-Western cultures; it rather involves decentring
the Western culture of confession depicted by Foucault by considering it
‘from its outside’, from the perspective of its margins. Exploring colonial
confession means engaging in the writing of a history of truth in the
colonies. In this respect, in addition to the Foucauldian question of the
historical production of truth, we have to raise the issue of its spatial and
geopolitical localization: ‘ Where is the truth?’ Finally, studying colonial
confession also means excavating forgotten practices of the ‘decoloniza-
tion of truth’ or, to say it in Foucault’s terms, exploring the production
of a ‘decolonizing alethurgy’ (Irrera, 2013; Renault, 2012).
 To start with, it is heuristic to compare Foucault with Frantz Fanon
and, more precisely, to put Foucault’s thought to the test of Fanon’s
relatively unknown reflections on confession as a central issue in the
colonial conflict. Conversely, Foucault’s concepts can be used as ‘tools’
for interpreting Fanon’s notion of the ‘production of truth’ within an
(anti)colonial context. In this respect, such a ‘dialogue’ is intended as a
contribution to the already rich field of interpretation, appropriations
and translations of Foucault’s work from a postcolonial perspective.
 The places of confession: an (anti)colonial alethurgy 2
 For Fanon, the history of truth in the colonies is a ‘non-history’ insofar
as the colonial situation constitutes a freezing of the historical-dialec-
tical process, a premature end of history. Truth is locked up in colonial
Manichaeism. As Fanon argues in  Black Skin, White Masks , the colonial
(colonizing and colonized) consciousnesses are racial ‘false conscious-
ness’: the truth that the colonizer ‘offers’ to the ‘savage’ is an ‘all-white
truth’ (Fanon, 2008: 114), and falsehood is necessarily all-black. Subject


212 Matthieu Renault
and predicate are substitutable: ‘falsehood is black’ means ‘the black
man is falsehood’; ‘truth is white’ means ‘the white man is truth’. As in
Sartre’s  The Respectful Prostitute , truth is nothing but a property (attribute
and possession) of the white race (Sartre, 1989). However, opposing the
movement of Negritude, Fanon refuses ‘to pose the problem of black
truth’ (Fanon, 2008: 178). For him, undoing the identification of race
and truth means instead challenging the politics of truth within the
colonial context.
 Fanon begins this task by shifting, in a Foucauldian manner, from the
problem of truth to the problem of what he calls ‘truth conducts’. Before
him, Richard Wright had argued that, in the American racial situation,
the black man’s ‘rule of conduct’ was not to tell the truth to the white
man but rather to play with truth and falsehood/lie in order to escape
from the white man’s anger:
Even when a white man asked us an innocent question, some uncon-
scious part of us would listen closely, not only to the obvious words,
but also to the intonations of voice that indicated what kind of
answer he wanted; and automatically, we would determine whether
an affirmative or negative reply was expected, and we would answer,
not in terms of objective truth, but in terms of what the white man
wanted to hear. (Wright, 2002: 41)
Already in the nineteenth century, the issue of non-confession was at
the heart of American slave narratives, as evidenced by this excerpt from
Solomon Northup’s  Twelve Years a Slave : ‘It is not safe to contradict a
master, even by the assertion of truth’ (Northup, 1855: 229).
 In 1955, at the occasion of the 53 rd French-Language Congress
on Psychiatry and Neurology, Fanon presents a short paper entitled
‘Conducts of Confession in North Africa’. Making a psychiatric determi-
nation, he argues, means ‘discovering the truth of the act, which will be
the basis of the truth of its author’ (Fanon, 1975: 1115); but in colonial
Algeria, the psychiatrist is faced with the accused native’s systematic
denial of the criminal act. Fanon then raises the following provocative
question: like the colonial psychiatrists, should we not say that ‘the
North-African is a liar’, that the ‘race’ he belongs to ‘suffers from an
inclination to lie, to conceal the truth, or is unable to differentiate the
true from the false’ (1116)? In this regard, the native is conceived of
as a ‘stranger to truth’: either he is unable to discern the truth or he
dissimulates it and lies. However, says Fanon, this argument ‘removes
the problem without resolving it’. In opposition to these statements, he


A Decolonizing Alethurgy 213
claims that it is necessary to bring into question the ‘lived experience’
[ le vécu ] of the act, ‘the facts as seen by the accused’. For this reason, one
has to replace the nosological approach by an existential approach – the
very approach that Foucault was to adopt in his first book  Mental Illness
and Personality , before firmly rejecting it in his later research.
 Understanding the denial of the criminal act implies analysing what
Fanon calls the ‘orchestration of the lie’; it involves breaking with the
Manichean division between true and false: ‘In any case, the liar himself
is a being who constantly asks himself the question of truth’ (Fanon,
1975: 1116). For Fanon, the real problem is that, for the colonized,
truth-telling is equivalent to swearing allegiance to the colonizer and
hence showing submission to him; for the colonized, the truth and the
‘objectivity’ of the colonizer are suspect by definition. Fanon writes in
 The Wretched of the Earth : ‘For the colonized subject, objectivity is always
directed against him’ (2004: 50). In this regard, the confusion between
true and false is not the consequence of any biological or constitu-
tional weakness; this is a mechanism of defence – in a sense inspired by
psychoanalysis – against colonial aggressions. Such a defence denial is
not a logical negation: it reveals (unveils) the truth as much as it masks
(veils) it. The colonized tells the truth by telling the false.
 In  A Dying Colonialism , Fanon refers to his seminal paper on confession
and extends its implications beyond the judicial domain. He speaks of
‘the overall attitude of the colonized who is hardly ever truthful before
the colonizer. The colonized does not let on, does not confess himself,
in the presence of the colonizer’ (1994: 127). The colonized learns
from the colonizer to be duplicitous, to hide his thoughts and desires.
He builds ‘psychological walls’ between himself and the occupier. To
put it differently, he develops the technique of lying: ‘To the scandal
of military occupation, he opposes a scandal of contact. Every contact
between the occupied and the occupier is a falsehood’ (43). Fanon also
deals with confession when considering the question of torture: ‘the
most important consequence [of truth serum] has appeared to us to be a
certain inability to distinguish the truth from the false’ (138). He repeats
a similar statement in  The Wretched of the Earth : the use of ‘truth serum’
in order to extort the confessions of the ‘Algerian patriots’ is the cause
of ‘a basic indistinction between true and false. Everything is both true
and false’ (2004: 212). Although torture is a limit-experience, it unveils
the subordination of truth and knowledge – in this case, medical knowl-
edge – to the logic of colonial subjection: ‘Science depoliticized, science
in the service of man, is often inexistent in the colonies’ (1994: 116).
Conversely, Fanon argues that practices of decolonization should ‘ensure


214 Matthieu Renault
that all the untruths planted within [the colonized] by the oppressor are
eliminated’ (2004: 233). The politics of decolonization should be poli-
tics of truth.
 Such a displacement translation, in the colonies, of the problem of
confession is also the source of theoretical translations. Fanon reveals
the existence of a genuine and original colonial quest for confession.
Indeed, not only does the colonizer seek to impose his domination
and to subdue the colonized body, but he also desires to conquer the
colonized’s mind, to penetrate into his consciousness and to obtain his
consent to his own servitude. For the colonized, telling the truth (about
oneself) should be equivalent to recognizing colonial truth, to endorse
the truth of colonialism. Colonial confession should produce the iden-
tity of the colonized’s subjection and subjectification. However, Fanon
is not so much interested in the mechanisms of such a colonial power
of confession as in the colonized’s resistance (denial, dissimulation) to
them. He does not problematize confession as such, but rather its nega-
tion, the refusal of confession, the colonized’s disavowal ( désaveu ). For
Fanon, providing an account of ‘truth conducts’ in the colonial situation
implies analysing ‘falsehood conducts’; the colonial alethurgy is insepa-
rable from what we can call (by deleting the privative ‘a’ of ‘alethurgy’)
an  anticolonial alethurgy . The postcolonial politics of truth first emerges
as a politics of falsehood; but, since this helps to unmask the ‘colonial
lie’, falsehood is already an act of truth: the dissimulation of truth iden-
tifies with its very manifestation. Finally, Fanon never presupposes the
existence of a ‘hidden truth’ retained by the colonized behind the mask
of falsehood. During the stage of counter-confession (disavowal), the
colonizer is entirely false to himself. For this reason, the issue of decolo-
nization will also involve the question of the conditions of emergence of
a discourse of truth concerning the colonized himself, the very question
of his subjectification.
 The dialectics of truth: a decolonizing alethurgy
 Fanon reveals the mutation of the ‘practices of truth’ during the
Algerian struggle for national liberation. Before the revolt, the colo-
nized developed defence mechanisms based on a withdrawal into
native ‘values’. This ‘culture of culture’ reproduced the colonial divi-
sion of true and false by inverting it. Such a ‘counter-assimilation’
offered itself as a global rejection of all the occupier’s truths and values.
Fanon is critical of this ‘mechanical sense of detachment and mistrust’
(Fanon, 1994: 117), which is the source of ‘uncompromising, rigid,


A Decolonizing Alethurgy 215
static counter-proposals’ (14). He argues furthermore that some of the
European occupier’s values would ‘objectively be worth choosing’ (63).
Sometimes, the colonized is ‘pushed in the name of truth and reason,
to say yes to certain forms of presence of the occupier’ (100). The
dilemma lies in the fact that the colonizer’s truth imposes itself as the
truth of French colonialism in Algeria. In other words, the acknowl-
edgement of the colonizer’s truths by the colonized is understood by
the former as a proof of the legitimacy of his domination: ‘The truth
objectively expressed is constantly vitiated by the lie of the colonial
situation’ (128).
 With the beginning of the struggle for national liberation, ‘the truth,
for once, eluded its traditional trustees and placed itself within the reach
of any seeker’ (1994:101–2). In order to explain this mutation, Fanon
relies on a dialectical (anti-Foucauldian) approach – which was inad-
equate for dealing with the colonial situation simply because this situa-
tion was determined by anti-dialectical Manichaeism, by a pure duality
without any underlying unity, and hence without contradiction. The
struggle, Fanon argues, produces a reversal of the colonized’s attitudes
towards the colonizer’s ‘gifts’. The uncompromising ‘no’ is replaced by a
‘yes’ that is no less radical. A ‘dialectical progression’ occurs, as evidenced
by the colonized’s massive appropriation of the radio (its ‘domestica-
tion’ or ‘digestion’ in Fanon’s words), which was formerly rejected as a
tool of the enemy and the medium of the colonial message. A ‘war of
sound waves’ takes place and the colonial forces tirelessly seek to silence
the broadcasts praising the Algerian revolution. But the ‘power of the
enemy sabotage’, his will to muzzle the ‘Voice of Algeria’, revels, as a
photographic negative, the intensity of the struggle. By trying to veil the
colonized’s voice, the colonizer unveils it:
 The ‘truth’ of the oppressor, formerly rejected as an absolute lie, was
now countered by another, an acted truth. The occupier’s lie thereby
acquired greater reality, for it was now a menaced lie, put on the
defensive. It was the defences of the occupier, his reactions, his resist-
ances, that underscored the effectiveness of national action and made
that action participate in a world of truth. The Algerian’s reaction was
no longer one of pained and desperate refusal.  Because it avowed its
own uneasiness, the occupier’s lie became a positive aspect of the nation’s
new truth. (Fanon, 1994: 76)
 This passage shows the complexity of a potential dialogue between Fanon
and Foucault. On the one hand, Fanon argues that truth is acted out


216 Matthieu Renault
and must be conceived in subjective and ethical terms: it is ‘performa-
tive’ and cannot predate the ‘truth conducts’ that produce it. However,
this does not prevent Fanon from considering those conducts as stages
in a dialectical process of truth’s disclosure. However, for Fanon, what
this ‘dialectical progression’ reveals is not the pure naked truth. Radio
jamming has a profound impact on the process of listening and inter-
preting: the ‘ Voice of Fighting Algeria soon became inaudible’ (1994: 85);
the interpretation of the message is, therefore, a ‘task of reconstruction’,
and even an ‘autonomous creation of information’ (86). It is undoubt-
edly a ‘distortion’ of truth; but more importantly, it is ‘the deliberate
choice [ ... ] between the enemy’s congenital lie and the people’s own
lie, which suddenly acquired a dimension of truth’ (87). It is a  true lie
that reveals the truth only by hiding it and hides it only by revealing.
It is a spectral truth that is still embedded in fantasy. For Fanon, the
emergence of a genuine postcolonial truth can be only the conclusion
of a long-term process of decolonization of bodies and minds. However,
how and when will such a postcolonial truth succeed in asserting itself,
in separating itself from falsehood? In  The Wretched of the Earth , Fanon
writes:
 The question of truth must also be taken into consideration. For the
people, only fellow nationals are ever owed the truth. No absolute
truth, no discourse on the transparency of the soul can erode this
position. In answer to the lie of the colonial situation, the colonized
subject responds with a lie. Behavior toward fellow nationalists is
open and honest, but strained and indecipherable toward the colo-
nists. Truth is what hastens the dislocation of the colonial regime,
what fosters the emergence of the nation. Truth is what protects the
‘natives’ and undoes the foreigners. In the colonial context there
is no truthful behavior. And good is quite simply what hurts them
most. (Fanon, 2004: 14)
 Fanon’s will for a radical rupture with Europe in  The Wretched of the
Earth therefore seems to involve an inversion, and hence a repeti-
tion, of colonial Manichaeism. The perfect balance between colonial
violence and anticolonial counter-violence seems to strike at the same
time further balance between the colonial lie and the anticolonial
‘counter-lie’, in such a way that any horizon of truth is foreclosed.
Nonetheless, one must not forget that, for Fanon, counter-violence
is only a stage in the process of ‘true’ decolonization. Although this
process actually starts with a conversion  to violence, the almost


A Decolonizing Alethurgy 217
animal insurrection of the beginnings must transform into a genuine
political struggle for liberation. It must produce a conversion  of
violence into rationality. In the conclusion of the book, Fanon argues
that true decolonization is both a total rupture and a renewal ( recom-
mencement ); it is a radical separation that does not prevent but, on the
contrary, implies the appropriation of the colonizer’s ‘gifts’. In this
regard, this appropriation is not mere repetition; as Fanon puts it in  A
Dying Colonialism , it is a ‘quasi-invention’. The same applies to truth:
truth is not given, and it is not a question of unveiling it; rather, it
needs to be made, to be (re)invented. Fanon gives rise to what can be
called a  decolonial alethurgy .
 However, how do we accommodate the fact that such a history of
truth in the colonies is conceived of by Fanon in dialectical terms. Like
Hegel, Fanon calls for breaking the rigid opposition between true and
false. Like Lukàcs, he conceives of ‘false’ as an ‘aspect of the “true”, as
both “false” and “non-false”’ (Lukàcs, 1979: xlvii); like Merleau-Ponty,
he thinks of the combination of ‘true’ and ‘false’ (Merleau-Ponty, 1974);
like Alexandre Kojève, he shows that truth is realized ‘in time’ and comes
out of action (Kojève, 1980); like Trotsky, he argues that the lie is an
instrument of war which is needed for the advent of a society without
class-race and hence without the lie (Trotsky, 1942). Such a dialectical
grounding locates Fanon’s thought within an episteme from which
Foucault will radically depart. Yet Fanon’s singular combination of a
dialectic of truth on the one hand, and an analysis of alethurgical forms
on the other hand, cannot but remain a source of surprise as it chal-
lenges some epistemological breaks that have become commonplace.
For example, unlike Foucault, Fanon does not care about the distinction
between the ‘political history of veridiction’ and the (dialectical-Marxist)
determination of ‘the mechanisms of illusions or ideologies’ (Foucault,
2013: 9). Similarly, ‘epistemological structures’ and ‘alethurgical forms’
are inextricably related in Fanon inasmuch as it is the analysis of the anti-
colonial conducts of truth-falsehood that leads him to raise the problem
of the conditions of emergence of a genuine postcolonial discourse of
truth. Finally, Fanon’s writings reveal a certain hybridization of practices
of liberation and practices of liberty, whereas Foucault – who precisely
refers to the anticolonial struggles – conceives of liberation as the mere
‘political and historical condition for a practice of liberty’ that bears no
relation with it (Foucault, 1987: 114). Rather than arguing that Fanon’s
dialectical thinking is nothing but a theoretical residue that needs
to be expelled – which is Homi K. Bhabha’s approach here (Bhabha,
2004) – one should consider seriously the dialectical  versus alethurgical


218 Matthieu Renault
tension that inhabits his thought. In other words, rather than seeking to
locate Fanon in a predefined European intellectual history, one should
consider how his ‘gaze from the outside’ (from the colonies) allows us
to reassess this history by questioning, from a decentred perspective,
epistemological breaks that are most often considered as being purely
internal to the Western world.
 The subject of confession: from the social contract to
the race war
 However, we have to acknowledge the fact that Fanon’s early formu-
lation of the question of truth-telling remains largely dependent on a
psychiatric perspective, which, far from being alien to Foucault’s inves-
tigations, is rather one of their most crucial objects. In this regard,
Foucault allows us to understand that, even though Fanon was trained
in ‘institutional psychotherapy’ and introduced revolutionary methods
such as ‘socialtherapy’ in (colonial) Algeria and ‘day hospitalization’
in (independent) Tunisia, his psychotherapeutic practice, as Françoise
Vergès argues, is still contained in the psychiatric institution (Vergès,
1996: 140). In his inaugural speech at the University of Leuven, Foucault
says that he wishes to analyse confession from the perspective of the
relations between ‘truth-telling and judging’ (Foucault, 2013: 11). In
the subsequent lectures, he raises the question of the ‘connection’ or
‘mutual contamination’ throughout history of the subject of law and
the subject of confession. In this respect, Fanon’s identification of the
‘truth of the act’ with the ‘truth of its author’ is nothing but the conclu-
sion of the gradual historical process of juridification of the conducts of
confession. It is ‘a huge mutation that produces a shift from a criminal
judgment concerned with acts to a strange action whose object is a prin-
ciple of rationality and measure, the manifested truth of the whole indi-
vidual’ (Foucault, 2013: 12).
 Focusing on the introduction of psychiatric expertise in criminal
affairs, Foucault argues that in a system governed by an ‘appetite for
confession’, psychiatric expertise serves as a remedy in situations where
confession is missing. It replaces the subject’s ‘self-verediction’ by a
‘hetero-verediction’ that aims at unveiling ‘the truth of the criminal
that the criminal himself is not able to express’. Rather than the ‘fact’
of the crime, psychiatric expertise intends to disclose the ‘meaning’ its
author gives to it: it intends to reveal ‘a subjectivity, which has a signifi-
cant relation to his crime: [ ... ] a criminal subject’ (Foucault, 2013: 211).
Foucault’s object of analysis here is the very theoretical and practical


A Decolonizing Alethurgy 219
standpoint of Fanon along with most of his contemporaries. We have
showed that Fanon’s writings constitute a precious tool for decentring
Foucault’s notion of confession; now we must add that, conversely,
Foucault’s writings reveal Fanon’s intellectual and professional ‘centra-
tion’, his own Eurocentrism.
 Nonetheless, Fanon displaces the question of (individual) confes-
sion towards political struggles for decolonization and conceives of
disavowal as a practice of collective counter-subjectification. He seeks
to liberate confession from its enmeshment in colonial psychiatric and
juridical structures. Fanon’s 1959–60 lectures at the University of Tunis
exemplify such a political translation of confession: ‘To confess means
to confess to being part of one’s own social group’ (Fanon, 1960: 10).
For Fanon, confession is two-fold: it is at the same time telling the truth
about oneself and claiming belonging to a given community. Fanon
had already made it plain in an unpublished version of ‘Conducts of
Confession in North Africa’ that ‘there is a moral dimension in confes-
sion [ ... ], but there is also a civic dimension, and we know that this idea
was dear to Hobbes and the philosophers of the social contract’ (Fanon,
1955). Hence, the following question arises: ‘does the native Muslim
feel bound by a social contract? Does he feel excluded because of his
fault? What will henceforth be, for him, the meaning of his crime, of the
investigation and, finally, of the sanction?’ According to Fanon – who is
inspired by Bergson’s reading of Dostoyevsky’s  Crime and Punishment –
for the criminal, the confession of the crime is the ‘ransom of his reinte-
gration into the group’ (Fanon, 1975: 1116).
 Actually, Foucault also deals with this stage in the history of confes-
sion: in its modern form, ‘confession is a reminder of the social pact; it is
its restoration. [ ... ] This confession is the first step of [the] reintegration
[of the culprit] because, through confession, one recognizes that he broke
the fundamental pact; but, by recognizing it, he makes the first step
[ ... ] towards his reintegration’ (Foucault, 2013: 207). However, Fanon’s
conception remains profoundly original insofar as he considers the rela-
tion between confession and social contract in a context in which the
very condition of possibility of this relation is negated: indeed, in the
colonies, there is no and there never was any social contract between
the colonizer and the colonized. There cannot be ‘reintegration if there
was no integration’ (Fanon, 1975: 1116). In the colonies, confession is
deprived of any function.
 In Fanon’s thought, the relation between confession and social
contract is replaced by a connection between truth and race or, in other
words, by the issue of racial politics of truth. Borrowing from Hobbes’s


220 Matthieu Renault
political anthropology, Fanon argues that the absence of any social
contract between the colonizer and the colonized implies that ‘coloni-
alism is naked violence [ la violence à l’état de nature ] and only gives in
when confronted to a greater violence’ (Fanon, 2004: 23). For Fanon,
colonialism is nothing but the negation of the political community and
the return to the state of nature. The state of nature is no longer the
age-old past of the political community; it is the very effect of colonial
power; it is a ‘becoming’ rather than an origin. This refashioning of the
Hobbesian schema of sovereignty – although Hobbes himself thought of
the English Civil War as a form of re-enactment of the state of nature – is
also a transgression. As Foucault states, it is a mistake to consider Hobbes
as the founding theoretician of permanent war. In his 1975–6 lectures at
the Collège de France,  Society Must be Defended , he says: ‘In Hobbes’s state
of primitive war, there is no blood and there are no corpses. There are
presentations, manifestations, signs, emphatic expressions, wiles, and
deceitful expressions; [ ... ] we are not really involved in a war’ (Foucault,
2003: 92); in contrast, Fanon deals with a very concrete and bloody war:
the Algerian war. Moreover, while for Hobbes, as Foucault argues, war
never plays a determining role in the genesis of the state, Fanon affirms
that decolonization (beyond independence) involves a ‘death-struggle’
which will put an end to violence, by violence.
 Fanon produces a discourse on Manichaeism, on the binary-internal
division of the colonial world and the permanent antagonism between
the colonizer and the colonized. He reproduces, so to speak, a ‘race war’
schema, which, according to Foucault, was opposed to the Hobbesian
juridical schema. Indeed, Fanon’s discourse is structured around the
opposition between the natives and the foreigners: ‘the colonist always
remains a foreigner [ ... ]. [He] is first and foremost the outsider from
elsewhere, different from the indigenous population’ (Fanon, 2004: 5).
To repeat what Foucault says about the historians of the race war, Fanon
attributes great importance to the brute ‘psycho-physiological facts
(physical strength, force, energy, [etc.])’, as well as to the ‘psycholog-
ical and moral elements (courage, fear, scorn, hatred, [etc.])’ (Foucault,
2003: 54–5). Finally, Fanon’s discourse ceaselessly calls for a ‘prophetic
rupture’ (Foucault, 2003: 71).
 Such a ‘return’ to the schema of the race war has an eminently strategic
function. Fanon does not defend a form of counter-racism. He rather
subverts racism by returning to its very origins – that is to say, by refash-
ioning a (racial) discourse that did not assign to race a fixed biological
meaning and was unconcerned by the (racist) idea of the ‘splitting of a


A Decolonizing Alethurgy 221
single race into a superrace and a subrace’ (Foucault, 2003: 61). As Robert
J. C. Young states, Fanon strategically reverses Marx’s own reversal of
racial explanation into class analysis (Young, 2001: 278). The colonizers
do not form a class strictly speaking, but rather a ‘ruling species’: ‘it
becomes clear that what divides this world is first and foremost what
species, what race one belongs to’ (Fanon, 2004: 5). Fanon subverts the
erroneously Darwinian-inspired notion – which was popular among
colonial psychiatrists – of the biological-racial ‘life struggle’. He thus
rejects any conception of the ‘social body’ as a biological entity. To sum
up, Fanon steals and transforms the enemy’s (intellectual) weapons in
order to turn racism against itself.
 In conclusion, by recasting the binary opposition between antagonist
races, Fanon appropriates a discourse in which the author necessarily
belongs to one of the struggling groups, a discourse which, in Foucault’s
words, ‘was essentially an instrument used in the struggles waged by
decentered camps’ – and will later ‘be recentered and will become the
discourse of power itself’ (Foucault, 2003: 61), namely, the discourse of
biological state racism. Problematizing the conflict of the colonial ‘social
races’ thus means challenging the discourse of racism in which there can
be only one (centred) subject of speech: the superrace talks about the
subrace. Fanon’s discourse enables the (re)appearance of another protago-
nist. It reintroduces the colonized not only as a subject of history, but also
as the subject who tells this (counter-)history. For Fanon, such a ‘telling’
is a ‘self-telling’, and hence a ‘self-representing’. This power of repre-
sentation – dear to postcolonial theorists, from Edward Said to Gayatri
Chakravorty Spivak, and which brings us back to Marx’s  The Eighteenth
Brumaire – is the condition of the possibility of a ‘truth discourse’ of the
postcolonized about himself, the condition of a  postcolonial confession .
 Notes
 1 .  This paper is a revised and translated version of Renault, M. (2012) ‘La confes-
sione (anti)coloniale. Razza e verità nelle colonie: Fanon dopo Foucault’,
 materiali foucaultiani , 1(2), pp.49–68. I thank the editors of  materiali foucault-
iani for having kindly authorized the reproduction of this essay.
 2 .  This notion has been coined with Orazio Irrera in the frame of the seminar
we have been holding since 2012 at the Collège International de Philosophie
and at the Université Paris-Est Créteil. See http://decolonisationsavoirs.word-
press.com/.

 Foucault, M. (2011)  The Courage of the Truth: Lectures at the Collège de France 1983–

 Foucault, M. (2013)  Mal faire, dire vrai : Fonctions de l’aveu en justice . Louvain:
Presses Universitaires de Louvain.

Cambridge, MA: MIT Press.
