223
 In a couple of previous articles I have tried to show why it is possible,
interesting and philosophically crucial to tie together the perspectives
on ethics and politics developed by Pierre Hadot, Stanley Cavell and
Michel Foucault (Lorenzini, 2010a; 2010b). These three thinkers have
indeed in common the effort to consider ethics as a non-teleological
and non-deontological field (Cavell, 1990: 46), and they allow us to
explore the link between ethical subjectivation and a politics of resist-
ance – which is, in my opinion, one of the most urgent challenges for
contemporary philosophy as well as one of the most important stakes
of our global present. More specifically, in this chapter I will argue that
Foucault’s, Hadot’s and Cavell’s perspectives on ethics and politics –
or better, on ethics  as politics – provide us with fundamental tools
to conceive and practice philosophy as a ‘critical attitude’ (Foucault,
2007). 1
 During his 16 February 1983 lecture at the Collège de France, Foucault
raises a very important question: ‘What is the real of philosophy?’
Analysing Plato’s seventh letter, he answers that ‘the real of philosophy
is not, is no longer, anyway, is not merely  logos ’ (Foucault, 2010: 227–8,
translation modified). In fact, the seventh letter proves that Plato was
not satisfied with his all too theoretical construction of the ideal city in
the  Republic . Such a theoretical construction could receive a meaning
only thanks to a practical work: Plato had to try to realize it, to translate
it into the concreteness of reality. But we must not conclude that the real
of philosophy is simply the ‘reality’ of the external world, of the world
of things as opposed to the world of thoughts. Foucault says something
else: the real of philosophy is the ‘test [ épreuve ] by which philosophy will
manifest itself as real’. In the case of Plato, this  épreuve is not to be found
in the ‘game intrinsic to the  logos itself’, but in the fact that philosophy
14
 Ethics as Politics: Foucault, Hadot,
Cavell and the Critique of Our
Present
 Daniele  Lorenzini


224 Daniele Lorenzini
‘addresses itself, can address itself, and has the courage to address itself
to whoever it is who exercises power’ (Foucault, 2010: 228, translation
modified). Telling the truth to whoever it is who exercises power, thus
risking one’s own life – as Plato did, confronting Dionysius the tyrant of
Syracuse – is the  épreuve that every philosophical discourse has to face
in order to become ‘real’. Hence, at the very beginning of its (Western)
history, philosophical discourse shows an intrinsic heteronomy: it is
not self-sufficient; it does not find in itself the reason and justification
for its own existence. On the contrary, today, philosophical discourse is
(supposed to be) autonomous: philosophy is no longer a concrete atti-
tude, a choice of existence, or an ascetic work of self-transformation, as
it was in the Age of Antiquity (Hadot, 1995: 107–8, 270–1; Cavell, 2004:
237), but an institutional discipline to be taught, locked-up in the realm
of theoretical abstraction, since an epistemological ‘regime of truth’
has been built based on the faith in the possibility of human reason to
discover in itself the norms of its own functioning and the conclusive
proof of its own reality (Foucault, 2012: 95–7).
 The heteronomy of the ancient philosophical discourse, or better
the supremacy of philosophical life over discourse in Antiquity, is the
central thesis of every book written by Hadot. According to him, as well
as to Foucault, the ‘real’ of ancient philosophy is the  bios philosophikos ,
since even telling the truth to whoever it is who exercises power is
made possible thanks to only a more fundamental attitude one has to
assume towards his own life. Plato had the courage to risk his own life
in order to tell the truth to the tyrant, and it is precisely the acceptance
of such a risk – or, in other words, the construction of his life as the life
of a truth-teller – that constitutes the first and main  épreuve for Plato’s
theoretical discourse. Besides, in conceiving the philosophical life as
the touchstone of the philosophical discourse, Foucault closely follows
Hadot, who claims that, in ancient philosophy, theoretical discourse is
never considered an end in itself but always clearly and decidedly put
in the service of practice, that is, of a certain way of living and being
(Hadot, 2002: 271, 364, 371). In the first lecture of  The Hermeneutics
of the Subject Foucault defines ‘spirituality’ as ‘the search, practice and
experience through which the subject carries out the necessary trans-
formations on himself in order to have access to the truth’ (Foucault,
2005: 15) or, as he says in a later interview, in order to have access ‘to
a certain mode of being’ (Foucault, 1987: 125). Therefore, Foucault
speaks of ‘spirituality’ when it is the life of the subject – not in its
biological sense ( zoé ) but rather in its ethical, political and social sense
( bios ) – that represents the chief object of his care, as well as the real


Ethics as Politics 225
stake of his work of transformation and transfiguration practised on
himself.
 To describe the peculiar relation established between  logos and  bios ,
discourse and life, in ancient philosophy, it is useful to introduce what I
might call a twofold ‘principle of harmony’. To explain what I mean by
this expression, I will consider firstly the figure of Socrates and his prac-
tice of truth-telling as described by Foucault. In fact, the ‘style of life’, the
‘form that one gives to life’, constitutes the essential object of Socratic
 parrhesia (Foucault, 2011: 144): as Nicias explains, in the  Laches , Socrates’
interlocutor is always ‘led by the Socratic  logos into “giving an account”
[ ... ] of “himself, of the manner in which he now spends his days, and of
the kind of life he has lived hitherto”’ (Foucault, 2001a: 96). Giving an
account of oneself means, then, according to Socrates, giving an account
of the way one lives and, in order to do so, it involves ‘submitting’ one’s
life to a ‘touchstone, a test, which enables one to distinguish between
the good and bad one has done in life’ (Foucault, 2011: 145). In this way,
it is possible to examine and determine the true nature of the relation
between one’s words and one’s life since Socrates asks his interlocutor
precisely whether he is able to show the harmony between the rational
discourse he uses and the way in which he lives. The touchstone with
which to test such a harmony is constituted by Socrates himself, who,
through his everyday way of living, even in its minutiae, manifests a
perfectly harmonious relation between his words and his deeds.
 Socrates is able to use rational, ethically valuable, fine, and beautiful
discourse; but unlike the sophist, he can use  parrhesia and speak freely
because what he says accords exactly with what he thinks, and what
he thinks accords exactly with what he does. (Foucault, 2001a: 101)
 Twofold principle of harmony: Socrates’ thoughts accord exactly with
his discourse, and his discourse (in turn) accords exactly with his way
of living. These conditions are both necessary for Socrates’  parrhesia ,
allowing him to speak frankly and to call into question the way of life of
his fellow citizens – that is to say, the harmony between  logos and  bios
has a value which is, at the same time, ethical and political. This was true
for Foucault, who in 1983 claimed that what interested him was ‘politics
as an ethics’, as a way of behaving and living: ‘a demanding, prudent,
“experimental” attitude is necessary’, since ‘at every moment, step by
step, one must confront what one is thinking and saying with what
one is doing, with what one is’. In other words, according to Foucault,
‘the key to the personal political attitude of a philosopher is not to be


226 Daniele Lorenzini
sought in his ideas, as if it could be deduced from them, but rather in his
philosophy-as-life, in his philosophical life, his  ethos ’ (Foucault, 1984c:
374–5, translation modified).
 However, Plato’s  Laches is also ‘the starting point for a whole philo-
sophical practice and activity’ that leads directly to Cynicism (Foucault,
2011: 128). As Foucault explains, the Cynic philosopher radicalizes the
Socratic relation between  logos and  bios , building up his life in the form
of a paradoxical and shocking transfiguration of the traditional philo-
sophical life – that is, in the form of an ‘other life’ ( vie autre ) which
continuously calls into question the lives of others using the weapon of
a scandalous  bios , instead of that of a parrhesiastic discourse (Foucault,
2011: 244–5, 269–70). Thus, with Cynicism, the ‘nonconformist’ dimen-
sion of the twofold principle of harmony breaks out, and the philosoph-
ical life fully becomes a ‘manifestation of the truth’ (Foucault, 2010: 343),
which (again) does not take place in the realm of pure theory but takes
place in the everyday life of the Cynic philosopher, through his body,
his ordinary choices and acts as well as the way he ordinarily dresses,
speaks and conducts himself (Foucault, 2011: 172). In order to trans-
figure his life into a manifestation of the truth, the Cynic philosopher
undertakes an endless ethical work on himself, on his words and deeds,
on every single detail of his existence: the Cynic manifestation of the
truth consists precisely in the courageous, scandalous and public mani-
festation of the (extra)ordinary way of living of the Cynic philosopher.
 Hence, Foucault presents Cynicism as the paradigm of a ‘hyperbolic’
form of resistance in which a specific ethical subjectivation assumes a
clear political value since the Cynic philosopher is constantly engaged
in a struggle against conventions, laws, and society as a whole: his life is
a ‘militant’ practice of opposition to all established powers (Lorenzini,
2014: 248–52). The Cynic philosopher transfigures the everyday of his
life, making it the site of a perpetual risk (the risk of truth-telling or,
better, of truth- living ) and the core of his radical criticism of every form
of social conformity. As Foucault argues, Cynic (true) life is a form of
‘explicit, intentional, and constant aggression directed at humanity
in general’, whose horizon or objective is to change it – ‘to change its
moral attitude (its  ethos ) but, at the same time and thereby, its customs,
conventions, and ways of living’ (Foucault, 2011: 280). So the  bios
kunikos is an aggression toward society and the world as they are, which
tries to give rise to an ‘other’ society and to an ‘other’ world: Cynicism
is the matrix of a form of life that does not aim merely ‘to say and show
what the world is in its truth’, but also ‘to show that the world will be
able to reach its truth, will be able to transfigure itself and become other


Ethics as Politics 227
in order to attain what it is in its truth, only at the price of a change, a
complete alteration – the complete change and alteration in the relation
one has to oneself’ (Foucault, 2011: 315, translation modified).
 Strikingly, it is possible to find the same framework described by
Foucault and Hadot in relation to ancient philosophy also in the writings
that Cavell devotes to moral perfectionism – since moral perfectionism
cannot be understood in its philosophical, ethical and political dimen-
sions unless we trace its tradition back to the Ancient Greek conception
of philosophy as a way of life. It is not by chance that the fundamental
question of ancient philosophy re-emerges right at the beginning of
Emerson’s essay,  Fate , thus attesting to his ‘perfectionist’ will to take
into account the importance of one’s way of behaving and living. ‘To
me’, writes Emerson, ‘the question of the times resolved itself into a
practical question of the conduct of life. How shall I live?’ (Emerson,
1981b: 346). For Emerson as for Cavell every moral choice concerns the
kind of life one is willing to live, and the meaning and value one is
ready to attribute to it. Besides, the principle of harmony between  logos
and  bios and the related theme of criticism and of the struggle against
social conventions and conformity also play a crucial role in moral
perfectionism: Emerson’s ‘self-reliance’ is an attitude at once ethical and
political, which does not consist in the (unlikely) discovery of ‘the Self’,
but rather in the creation of a new relation of oneself to oneself, to
others and society as a whole. This attitude is put into practice through
a specific exercise of attention to the words one uses in ordinary life
(Cavell, 2003: 193) and through a concrete ‘self-transformation’ that
aims to attain ‘the further or higher self of each’ – which is a perpetual
and never-to-be-concluded task (Cavell, 1990: 53). At the same time,
this attitude is traversed by the contrast between imitation and – as John
Stuart Mill would have said – the ‘exercise of individuality’ (Mill, 1985:
119–40): ‘Insist on yourself; never imitate’ is, indeed, the indispensable
corollary of Emerson’s self-reliance (Emerson, 1981a: 160). According to
him, the fundamental task that thinking as an activity has to accom-
plish is a practical one, namely, the reversal of one’s way of living and
the perpetual struggle with (and within) the present, a struggle charac-
terized by the tension between society ‘as it stands’ and society ‘as it
may become’ (Cavell, 2004: 141). Such a tension is considered by Cavell
an essential feature of moral perfectionism:
 The very conception of a divided self and a doubled world, providing
a perspective of judgement upon the world as it is, measured against
the world as it may be, tends to express disappointment with the


228 Daniele Lorenzini
world as it is, as the scene of human activity and prospects, and
perhaps to lodge the demand or desire for a reform or transfiguration
of the world. (Cavell, 2004: 2)
 Thus, the political dimension of moral perfectionism becomes plain.
As Sandra Laugier convincingly argues in her writings on the ‘politics
of the ordinary’ (Laugier, 2010: 141–59), the question of democracy,
according to Cavell, is the question of the (harmonious or dissonant)
relation between one’s own personal voice and political discourse. The
perfectionist touchstone of this relation corresponds to one’s capacity
for taking responsibility for one’s own voice, which is possible if and
only if one is able to establish perfect harmony between one’s voice and
one’s everyday life – in order to transfigure the latter and make it the
highest practical example of the correct use of the former. Therefore, in
moral perfectionism, conformity will also be overcome through ethical
work on oneself and one’s life which implies the establishment of a
harmonious relation between  logos and  bios , as well as a critical relation
with others and society as they are.
 This is why we can conclude that Foucault, Hadot and Cavell all rely
on an ancient conception of philosophy and share the same interest for
a perspective in which the real of philosophy, the  épreuve of its reality, is
constituted by the philosopher’s ordinary way of living, built up through
an ethical work practised on himself that can be described as a spiritual
exercise, as a technique of the self, or as a ‘practice of the ordinary’
(Cavell, 1989: 46–7). But this perspective is not of only ‘historical’ value,
since – as Foucault shows in his writings of the seventies – it is precisely
our everyday life that constitutes the target of modern and contempo-
rary governmental powers and the main field of struggle between power
relations and practices of resistance. Indeed, the three principal instances
of the huge social form of power that emerged at the beginning of the
nineteenth century (surveillance, discipline and normalization) have
in common the same objective: the life of human beings, that is, the
human existence in its materiality, in its corporeity and in all its various
and ordinary dimensions – biological, social, cultural, and so on. Thus,
our everyday life matters not only in an ethical but also in a political
sense, and the work of oneself on oneself, applied even to the meanest
details of one’s words and deeds, today represents the fundamental core
of every socio-political strategy of resistance that aims to be effective.
 However, after having shown that it is possible to find a common
ground upon which to compare the perspectives of Cavell, Foucault and
Hadot on ethics and politics, the next stage is to highlight the different


Ethics as Politics 229
‘philosophical options’ that they put forward and then to pose the deli-
cate problem of the ethico-political value that each of these thinkers
assigns to the renewal of the ancient idea of philosophy as a way of
life.
 The key to Hadot’s interpretation of ancient philosophy is clearly
located in the concept of ‘spiritual exercise’. Spiritual exercises can be
‘physical, as in dietary regimes, or discursive, as in dialogue and medita-
tion, or intuitive, as in contemplation’ (Hadot, 2004: 6). Their aim is ‘a
transformation of our vision of the world’ and ‘a metamorphosis of our
personality’, and their chief peculiarity is that, ‘by means of them, the
individual raises himself up to the life of the objective Spirit; that is to say,
he re-places himself within the perspective of the Whole’ (Hadot, 1995:
82). Hadot exemplifies this peculiarity mainly in his analyses of Stoicism,
since the goal of Stoic philosophy was precisely to ‘make possible,
little by little, the inner transformation that is indispensable’ in order
to re-place oneself and each event ‘within the perspective of universal
nature’ (Hadot, 1995: 83, translation modified); in other words, it was
a matter of ‘training to die to one’s individuality and passions, in order
to look at things from the perspective of universality and objectivity’
(Hadot, 1995: 95). Therefore, according to Hadot, ancient philosophy
is a spiritual exercise first and foremost because it is a metamorphosis
of the subject resulting from and leading to ‘a complete change of the
point of view, a change that allows the individual to give up his partial
and one-sided vision, and to open himself to universality, to the magnif-
icent perspective of the  cosmos and humanity’ (Hadot, 2002: 210).
 In his book on Goethe, Hadot singles out three fundamental features
of spiritual exercises: the concentration on the present moment, the
view from above and the ‘Yes’ to life and world (Hadot, 2008: 10–11).
However, these three features are bound together and all rest, ultimately,
on what I might call the ‘universalizing jump’ of the self into cosmic
Reason. Firstly, to concentrate on the present moment, at the expense
of the past and the future, means ‘to re-place the present moment in the
perspective of the  cosmos ’, in order to acknowledge the ‘infinite value of
each instant of existence’ (Hadot, 2008: 42). Such a valorization of the
present is intimately linked to the theme of the view from above that
allows us to see ‘in a single glance, which means in a single moment,
a huge set of things’ (Hadot, 2008: 89): the view from above is an exer-
cise of the imagination through which ‘we figure that we look at things
from a high point we have reached after elevating from the ground,
usually thanks to a flight of the mind in the  cosmos ’ that allows us to
plunge ‘into totality and even beyond totality, into infinity’ (Hadot,


230 Daniele Lorenzini
2008: 99). Finally, it is this very re-placement of the present moment
in the perspective of the cosmic Whole, through the view from above,
that justifies ‘a “Yes” to the world and reality, even in its most atro-
cious aspects’ (Hadot, 2008: 155). Hence, the universalizing jump of the
subject, who transcends his individual self in order to plunge into the
‘oceanic sentiment’, into ‘the sentiment of his belonging to the infinity
of being’ (Hadot, 2008: 251), constitutes the heart of every spiritual exer-
cise in the Hadotian sense of the word. This makes Hadot’s critique of
Foucault’s insistence on the care of the self – interpreted by the former
as an ethics of the egoistic, narcissistic ‘refolding’ of the individual on
himself – perfectly intelligible. Against Foucault, in fact, Hadot argues
that the ‘movement of interiorization’, the ‘movement of conversion
toward the self’, the focusing of one’s own attention on oneself, consti-
tuted only a preliminary step in ancient spiritual exercises, whose true
aim was to overcome the particular self, to transcend one’s own indi-
viduality in the direction of cosmic universality: ‘a new way of being-
in-the-world, which consists in becoming aware of oneself as a part of
Nature, and a portion of universal Reason’ (Hadot, 1995: 211).
 It is undeniable that Foucault never focused his attention on the univer-
salizing jump of the self and that he never followed Hadot in suggesting
that the ‘best part’ of oneself is a ‘transcendent self’ – and therefore,
to take care of oneself means to elevate ‘from individuality to univer-
sality’ since ‘the true self of each individual transcends the individual’ in
the direction of the cosmic Whole (Hadot, 1998a: 249–50). What inter-
ested Foucault in ancient philosophy was something else – something,
nevertheless, completely different from a ‘new form of Dandyism, late
twentieth-century style’ (Hadot, 1995: 211). The notion of ‘aesthetics of
existence’, and even more explicitly the analyses of  parrhesia and ancient
Cynicism, clearly shows the political objective of Foucault’s ‘return to
the Greeks’: in the genealogical study of the ancient techniques of the
self, Foucault found a new way to confront the problem of resistance –
that is, the problem of ethical subjectivation as a form of resistance.
However, given the importance Foucault acknowledges in the complex
framework of personal and power relations in which every individual is
involved, such a genealogical study has nothing to do with the constitu-
tion of an individualistic ethics. While Hadot’s subject, thanks to spir-
itual exercises, opens himself up to universal Reason and says ‘Yes’ to
the  cosmos as it is, Foucault’s subject, thanks to the techniques of the
self, opens himself to society and says ‘no’ to every unreflected conven-
tion, to every power relation that he finds unacceptable. In other words,


Ethics as Politics 231
the goal of Foucault’s study of ancient philosophy is essentially critical,
while Hadot’s is not, or at least not directly, since the universalizing jump
of the self in the universe can certainly imply some sort of criticism as
a consequence, but it does not contain in itself any concrete or specific
political attitude – it does not assure any translation of the ethical into
the political. On the contrary, Foucault’s ethics of the self, at least in the
radical form it takes with Cynicism, is at the same time also a politics of
resistance, of provocation and of scandal, which aims at using the work
of oneself on oneself as a weapon for calling into question and refusing
every social rule and every power relation unreflectively accepted, thus
unmasking its contingency as well as its potential dangerousness.
 Besides, already in 1971 Foucault outlines a sort of ‘paradigm’ of polit-
ical resistance very close to a Cynic one: ‘It is good to be dirty and bearded,
to have long hair, to look like a girl when one is a boy (and vice-versa)’,
Foucault says, because we have to ‘unmask our rituals’, we have to ‘put
“into play”, exhibit, transform and overturn’ the systems that peacefully
govern us, in order to make them appear as what they are – that is, as
‘purely arbitrary things’ (Foucault, 2001b: 1061). This was quite literally
the critical task of the Cynic philosopher in ancient Greece: from his
position of essential marginality and through the public manifestation
of his scandalous behaviour, in fact, the Cynic shows to his fellow citi-
zens that everything they accept as universal, necessary and obligatory
is, on the contrary, singular, contingent and due to arbitrary constraints,
thus inciting them to try to change things, in order not to be, not to
do and not to think any longer what they are, what they do and what
they think (Foucault, 1984a: 46). Cynicism as an attitude, a ‘trans-his-
torical’ attitude (Foucault, 2011: 174, 179), is very close then to that
modern attitude, to that philosophical  ethos described by Foucault in
 What Is Enlightenment? as ‘a permanent critique of our historical being’
(Foucault, 1984a: 42, translation modified). On the contrary, in Hadot’s
work the permanent critique of our historical being is replaced by the
search for an ahistorical  élan , always available to every human being,
which pushes the subject to transcend his particular, individual self and
to reach the universal perspective of cosmic Reason. The universalizing
jump of the self in the  cosmos represents indeed, according to Hadot, an
‘eternal’ possibility for human beings (Hadot, 1995: 212; 1998b: 310).
The question is: what kind of (social and political) criticism does this
universalizing jump make possible?
 From a Foucaultian point of view, no real and effective criticism can be
elaborated and put into practice unless we assume a radically immanent


232 Daniele Lorenzini
position, taking into account the complex framework of power relations
that structures our lives. In other words, according to Foucault, criti-
cism never comes from outside or from above: it is always a struggle, a
concrete attempt to reverse one or more power relations that affect us
directly.
 [W]hat I mean by  power relations is the fact that we are in a strategic
situation toward each other. [ ... ] It means that we always have possi-
bilities, there are always possibilities of changing the situation. We
cannot jump  outside the situation, and there is no point where you
are free from all power relations. But you can always change it. So
what I’ve said does not mean that we are always trapped, but that we
are always free—well, anyway, that there is always the possibility of
changing. (Foucault, 1997: 167)
 This is why, even if Hadot tries to emphasize moral perfectionism’s
closeness to spiritual exercises, defining it as ‘a movement toward a
higher self’ (Hadot, 2002: 380), Cavell’s perspective on ethics and poli-
tics seems to me much closer to Foucault’s. Things are quite complex,
though, since Cavell gives a lot of different definitions and unassimi-
lable descriptions of moral perfectionism. In  This New Yet Unapproachable
America , for instance, he identifies moral perfectionism with ‘Emerson’s
conception of his work as a writer’, that is to say: ‘attracting the human
[ ... ] to the work of becoming human’ (Cavell, 1989: 10). Then, Cavell
quotes Thoreau: ‘Not till we are lost [or turned around], in other words,
not till we have lost the world, do we begin to find ourselves, and realize
where we are and the infinite extent of our relations’ (Cavell, 1989: 36).
Philosophy ‘begins in loss’ and ends with a recovery that assumes the
form of a ‘finding of the world’, of a ‘returning of it, to it’ (Cavell, 1989:
114), made possible by a turn, or return ‘to, of, from the self’ (Cavell,
1990: xxx). In short, moral perfectionism asks us to acknowledge that
we have a further or higher self not yet realized (‘unattained’), and
consequently, it urges us to start walking along the path of its realization
(Cavell, 1990: 12, 51). This task requires, first and foremost, the ‘courage
to be what we are’ (Cavell, 1990: 16) or, better, to  become what we are
and to be ‘true to ourselves’ – what Cavell also refers to as ‘the task of
accepting finitude’ (Cavell, 2004: 4, 11).
 These ideas seem dangerously close to what Foucault calls the
‘Californian’ or ‘contemporary cult of the self’ – whose goal is ‘to discover
one’s true self, to separate it from that which might obscure or alienate
it, [and] to decipher its truth thanks to psychological or psychoanalytic


Ethics as Politics 233
science’ (Foucault, 1984b: 362) – opposing it to the ancient culture of
the self which, on the contrary, aimed not at discovering the hidden
identity of the individual, but rather at equipping him with the tools
necessary to  create himself as the subject of his own actions. Indeed,
according to Foucault, the subject is not given: it is constituted in ‘real
and historically analyzable practices’ (Foucault, 1984b: 369, translation
modified). However, despite appearances, the ‘perfectionist’ dimension
of the moral life is very different from, and even radically opposed to,
the Californian cult of the self, since Cavell explicitly criticizes contem-
porary ‘false or debased perfectionisms’ and considers such a critique as
one of the essential tasks of Emersonian perfectionism (Cavell, 1990: 13,
16), whose core is not to be found in the return to a ‘true self’ that one
should rediscover but rather in the perpetual invention of new ‘selves’
and the creation of new ‘stages’ in one’s ‘being oneself’ – that is to say,
in a journey continually projected onwards (Cavell, 1989: 12), since the
human self ‘is always becoming, [ ... ] always partially in a further state’
(Cavell, 2004: 26). As Cavell clearly claims, ‘I assume no role for the idea
of a true (or a false) self. Such an idea seems rather something imposed
from outside oneself’ (Cavell, 1990: xxxiv).
 In sum, the inner dynamics of moral perfectionism is characterized
not by the discovery of a given self but rather by the conversion, meta-
morphosis, reformation, transfiguration and revolution of oneself and,
at the same time, of the others and society as a whole (Cavell, 1989: 43,
47; 1990: 46). This crucial link between the transformation of oneself
and the transformation of society, as well as the above-mentioned
tension between society as it stands and society as it may become, bears
witness of the political dimension of moral perfectionism, making it
much closer to Foucault’s perspective than to Hadot’s. Besides, Cavell
clearly and firmly denies any role to transcendence: ‘The direction out
from illusion is not up, at any rate not up to one fixed morning star;
but down, at any rate along each chain of a day’s denial. Philosophy (as
descent) can thus be said to leave everything as it is because it is a refusal
of, say disobedient to, (a false) ascent, or transcendence’ (Cavell, 1989:
46). Consequently, for Cavell as for Foucault, there is no ‘vertical jump’
in the direction of cosmic Reason: their perspectives on ethics and poli-
tics – or, better, on ethics  as politics – remain within the immanence of
this world, following the ‘horizontal’ dynamics of the passage from an
individual to a collective form of resistance (Lorenzini, 2010b: 26–30).
In other words, for them philosophy is a ‘critical attitude’, a specific
 ethos – at the same time political and moral – characterized by a perma-
nent critique of our present, which takes the form of the critique of


234 Daniele Lorenzini
ourselves as historical beings and is characterized by what Foucault calls
‘the art of not being governed quite so much’ (Foucault, 2007: 45).
 What is good, is something that comes through innovation. The
good does not exist, like that, in an atemporal sky, with people who
would be like the Astrologers of the Good, whose job is to determine
what is the favorable nature of the stars. The good is defined by us,
it is practiced, it is invented. And this is a collective work. (Foucault,
1988)
 Note
 1 .  A version of this chapter was given as a talk to the Contemporary European
Philosophy Workshop at the University of Chicago. I am grateful to Arnold
I. Davidson for that opportunity and for his helpful comments as well as to
Marie Satya McDonough, Tuomo Tiisala and Daniel Wyche for their attentive
reading of a first draft of this paper.

236
 — In your work you have often referred to Foucault. Do you want
to explain how his work has been of paramount importance for you?
What are the aspects of his thought that you feel are closest and most
relevant to your own, and what, conversely, are the areas where you
feel most distanced from Foucault’s positions? Are there specific junc-
tures in Foucault’s work that you find produce an impasse for your
reflections?
 Judith Butler : In the 1980s it was clearly the first volume of  The
History of Sexuality that was most important for me, since it gave me
a way to understand the clustering of various sexual and gendered
functions under the category of ‘sex’ but also to show how repressive
discourse produces and proliferates sexuality. We were all quite amazed
by that analysis, even though some intimations of that could be found
in Freud already. But perhaps most important for me was Foucault’s brief
discussion of homosexuality as a ‘reverse-discourse’. It was important
to understand that no matter how pathologizing original discourse on
homosexuality had been, reversing that pathologization could be the
point of a newer homosexual or gay/lesbian discourse. The point was
not to convert a negative value into a positive one, but to produce a new
field of values. Surely, we are living in such a time when that is actively
happening.
 I was certainly interested in the way the body and soul were described
in  Discipline and Punish , but I was sorry to see the lack of a sexual dimen-
sion in the analysis of incarceration. I probably took a break from
Foucault for a while, but became interested again when I finally had
access to  The Hermeneutics of the Subject and then a series of lectures on
parrhesia and on the relationship between speech and truth. I found the
last lectures quite engaging, and they have helped me to think about
15
 Resistance and Vulnerability: An
Interview with Judith Butler*
 Federica Sossi and Martina  Tazzioli


Resistance and Vulnerability 237
both the interlocutory conditions of autobiography, but also the way in
which political speaking works.
 — In the essay  Giving an Account of Oneself , you insist on the impos-
sibility of giving a full account of oneself; from this impossibility you
gesture towards an ethics that builds on a constitutive dissatisfaction,
that is, an ethics postulating that the other cannot give an account of
himself/herself, either: ‘In a sense, the ethical stance consists [ ... ] in
asking the question “Who are you?” and continuing to ask it without
any expectation of a full or final answer’ (Butler, 2005: 43). In other
essays you analyse claims by certain authors, in particular Foucault
and Arendt, where the philosophical claim is staged in such a way that
allows for it to be suggested although denied or not fully embraced at
the same time. Do you think there is a connection between these two
perspectives, that is, the one from  Giving an Account of Oneself and the
one in the essays about Foucault and Arendt? Does the philosophical
discourse, in some sense, need to be conveyed by ‘not fully giving an
account of oneself’?
 J.B .: It seems clear to me that the speaking subject in Arendt is one who
both appears and acts in public, and this is done with and for others.
So it is not a scene of an individual alone but rather a scene of address.
This is certainly close to the forms of self-exposition or demonstration
that Foucault talks about in his essays on medieval modes of self-con-
stitution. Neither of these subjects is primarily offering a story or narra-
tive account of what they have done or why. Rather, they are appearing
as a speaking being who is producing effects, constituting the self and
transfiguring the scene of address in which they appear. Foucault moves
away from confession, as we know, and also the presumption that it is a
judge or psychiatric representative of the police force to whom we have
to make our story known. There is a power in speech, and it the capacity
for self-constitution and self-care that exceeds the bounds of the scene
of interrogation and judgement.
 For Arendt, the situation is different only because Eichmann is so
central to her reflections on this issue. In this case, however, she is less
interested in a psychological or biographical account of why he has
done what he has done, but only that he has done it, and that he be
told, quite clearly, that his acts constitute crimes against humanity. She
savours judgement, whereas Foucault does not. But for her, judgement
has a Kantian dimension that is bound up with aesthetics, and so judge-
ment is an active exercise of autonomy. Eichmann lacked judgement
and acted from a lack of judgement, and it is on that basis that he is
judged. The court of law is important for her, although she wants it to


238 Federica Sossi and Martina Tazzioli
be international rather than national. And in the end, she wants judges
to act as Kantian subjects, so philosophy proves to be the true authority
behind good legal judgement.
 — In recent years, referring to authors like Levinas and Arendt, you
have been elaborating a conception of the ethical subject in which the
constitution of subjectivity itself as well as the ethical possibility of the
subject but also its capacity to think and the very possibility of a crit-
ical gesture are possible only through a scene ‘populated’ by others and
through the exposure of the subject to different forms of alterity. At
the same time, you also stress the capacity of agency of the subject in
constituting itself as an ethical subject. Do you think that the reflection
of the late Foucault concerning the work of transformation of the self
could be useful for thinking the ethical subject that you address and to
what extent do you make reference to it? In other words, if on the one
hand you radically challenge the reference to a self-mastery that the
last reflections of Foucault seem in part to postulate and to indicate,
are there any aspects of the Foucaultian ethics of the self that in your
view are relevant in order to articulate the ethical practice and its critical
function?
 J.B. : I am sure that the late Foucault is quite important for thinking
through this process, though I am wondering how, for instance, one
would approach a Foucaultian theory of vulnerability? It is my sense
that many of the most recent and effective public mobilizations involve
enacting a certain body politic in concert. The bodies on the street are
not only agentic and relational but also precarious. They are precarious
at the moment that they appear on the street, but they are also there
to bring attention to a more systematically induced precarity – the loss
of jobs, homes, health insurance, the accumulation of unpayable debt,
leading either to a sense of no futurity or one marked by what Hegel
would call ‘a bad infinity’.
 It seems clear that the late Foucault helps me to think about speech,
of speaking back to power, and to ways of moving beyond the proto-
cols of self-defence and self-justification. There is clearly a performa-
tive dimension to speaking in some of the late work and a presumption
that self-constitution happens in relation to others. So all this is most
helpful, but I wonder if the embodied dimension of speaking, including
the predicament of speaking as or from the body in a condition of
precarity can be thought well enough through that work. It is clear
that Foucault was himself speaking and writing as he was ill and surely
grasping the conditions of his own precarity. Understanding in a non-
reductive way how that sense of a historically induced evanescence


Resistance and Vulnerability 239
entered into his final reflections on law and  parrhesia would be of great
interest to me.
 — We would like to refer to your text ‘What is Critique? An Essay on
Foucault’s Virtue’ since it seems to us that your way of articulating the
Foucaultian notion of ‘regime of truth’ goes beyond the production of
a partition between true and false by showing instead the production of
a partition between lives that matter and lives that do not matter. You
have developed this last point in many of your following works. But
pointing at the very partition between lives that matter and lives that do
not matter, are you are also addressing a modality of recognition basi-
cally grounded on a binary division? And, if so, do you think that there
is a fundamental partition at stake in our present that is also a condition
for the production of all others?
 J.B. : I think that most recently I have started to talk about an unequal
distribution of grievability, which suggests that there are a variety of
gradations. Indeed, we would probably have to distinguish among lives
that are hyper-grievable, who come to stand symbolically for a nation,
those who are more or less grievable, those who are consistently less
grievable, and then those who are never grievable, foreclosed from the
possibility. I am not producing new categories as much as I am trying
to indicate that degrees of grievability shift depending on the historical
context and that some populations are hyper-grievable in one context
and quite ungrievable in another. Similarly, we might say that even
within a single, broadly delimited context, lives can be simultaneously
grievable and not, and public discourse can be confounded by the ques-
tion of whether and how to grieve certain losses. To say that grieva-
bility shifts in this way is to say as well that modes of disavowing and
discarding life shift in this way as well.
 — According to Foucault,  parrhesia is a way of truth-telling that
produces a scandal and that entails the exposure of the subject to a risk.
Defining  parrhesia as an attitude, Foucault seems to recall his definition
of critique that you have also largely analysed in your essay. However,
the production of the scandal is internal to a ‘parrhesiastic game’ – as
Foucault remarks – that ultimately defines the space and the perimeter
in which the parrhesiastic speech is uttered. Do you think that this space
could be effectively disrupted through the parrhesiastic scandal? And do
you envisage the practice of  parrhesia as pertaining to the regime of the
performative?
 J.B. : I certainly understand  parrhesia as part of the domain of perfor-
mativity, but I am not sure I would call it ‘a regime’. Nor am I alto-
gether clear whether the metaphor of the ‘game’ (and ‘game theory’)


240 Federica Sossi and Martina Tazzioli
is altogether helpful. Are we speaking about a game, a regime or some
more shifting and provisional speech situation? Is  parrhesia presumed
to be performed or exercised by an individual? What about groups
that sing or chant or engage in silent actions? Do they count as part of
parrhesiastic practice? If  parrhesia defines the speech situation in which
it occurs, then it is clearly performative but in a way that moves beyond
Bourdieu’s definition. My questions remain, though: does it matter that
it is a speaking body that engages in  parrhesia and that it is located some-
where and has the shifting of the terms of location as part of its aim?
Can there be concerted  parrhesia , that is, an alliance of speakers, and
even if they do not act or speak in unison, can that convergence of
voices, gestures and actions be considered parrhesiastic?
 — In your reading of Foucault, one of the sentences you often linger on
is the following: ‘How does it come to be that the human subject makes
himself into an object of possible knowledge, through which forms of
rationality, through which historical conditions, and, finally, at what
price?’ (Foucault in Butler, 2005: 120). In an interview with Vikki Bell
(2010), you come back to this sentence going beyond that, since you
relate it to the criteria of exclusion that determine what can be called
the ‘scales of productivity’ of subjects and their being recognizable as
subjects and bringing attention to the fact that the non-recognizability
of some is necessarily constitutive of the subjectivity of others. Here,
you recall the sentence: ‘At what price do any of us get produced as
subjects?’ Could you clarify this question? And is the ‘price to be paid’
also in this case the price that the subject that is produced allows to be
paid by others in terms of exclusion or as not being recognizable?
 J.B. : I do think that every regime of rationality has its own constitu-
tive outside, which means that it must produce and maintain the non-
rational or irrational outside of its boundary. The subject who emerges
through that regime of rationality establishes his or her intelligibility
within that regime (we might think of the ‘his or her’ as a key logical
operator of one binary regime of gender). The formation of the subject
is, however, not commensurate with the establishment of its intelligi-
bility. After all, it is not just that the non-rational is posited outside the
regime, but for every subject, the non-rational is produced as part of
its less intelligible constitution. In this way, it is important to see that
subject formation within a matrix of intelligibility always partially fails,
since it cannot happen without positing its ‘outside’ as interior to the
subject itself, the abiding spectre of its undoing.
 At the same time, we have to think about this risk and this loss in terms
of the managing of populations, of biopolitics and governmentality


Resistance and Vulnerability 241
both. Although I know Foucault’s work on neoliberalism less well, it is
clear that certain forms of ordering the subject clearly imply unequal
and uneven forms of assimilation to that rationality. My sense is that
precarity is induced by neoliberal regimes of rationality as its necessary
condition and outside. For the self-maximizing subject is a norm that
implies a differential realization of its aims. Foucault is generally less
concerned with recognition than I am. I bring that concern forward
from my engagement with Hegel. But he is the one who often under-
scores that escaping from prevailing norms of recognition can be the
condition of freedom itself. I am always working, it seems, with the need
for new forms of recognition to condition liveable life at the same time
as I am wary of forms of recognition, sometimes the very same forms,
that under certain circumstances can make life less than liveable.
 — In your last book,  Parting Ways (2012), you reflect on the possibility
of an ethical life through the notion of unchosen cohabitation, mainly
addressing Arendt’s work. In some passages of your essay you stress the
element of non-freedom that is constitutive of that condition. Instead,
Foucault defines ethics as ‘the reflected practice of freedom’. This last
aspect seemed to be at stake in some of your reflections on ethics which
stressed rather the element of the claim. Could you dwell upon this
aspect?
 J.B. : I appreciated Foucault’s definition, but I am led to wonder about
how the unchosen dimensions of life can themselves become the occa-
sion for a reflected practice of freedom. After all, what is ‘reflected’ is
not simply this self that I am and not simply the freedom that I exer-
cise, but also the limits on that exercise and the rather recalcitrant ways
that history works on any of us. As Foucault would doubtless agree,
freedom becomes possible only under certain conditions, and some of
those conditions are not freely chosen. So, it seems important to think,
for instance, about unchosen dimensions of sociality and proximity, of
embodiment and lineage. Reflecting on them does not transform them
into expressions of freedom but rather circumscribes the embodied and
historical conditions under which a certain version of freedom becomes
possible. Arendt and Foucault both clearly have a way of understanding
freedom as an exercise or a practice, thus moving us away from freedom
as a natural endowment or an  a priori condition. But where it seems that
Arendt specifies certain forms of political freedom, such as revolution,
as concerted action, I have a more difficult time finding an equivalent
to that notion in Foucault. Perhaps the category of the ‘subject’ or even
‘the self’ works across the individual/social distinction in Foucault, but
it does not seem to do enough to establish the unchosen, historical and


242 Federica Sossi and Martina Tazzioli
embodied dimensions of sociality or the forms of freedom that we might
understand as undertaken by alliances.
 — Recently you have insisted a lot on the idea of vulnerability as
the condition which constitutes ourselves as subjects and at once as the
condition which allows us to rethink our ethical being and our ethical
acting, as well. However, according to many scholars, vulnerability
works today as one of the main modalities through which subjects are
produced within the government of the humanitarian. How do you
situate yourself in comparison to this perspective? Don’t you think that
when categories become object of power’s discourse, there is the risk of
their weakening and subsumption?
 J.B. : It is clear that the identification of ‘vulnerable populations’ by
NGOs (non-governmental organizations) and humanitarian agencies
works to deny such groups their history and their agency, including their
powers of resistance. But my aim is to think vulnerability and resistance
together. In fact, that is the name of a group of feminists I am convening
in Istanbul in September 2013. In the United States, national military
policy is often governed by an ideal of radical invulnerability, and this
means that vulnerability ought to be distributed among other nations
and peoples. This way of exporting vulnerability is both justified and
enacted in military policy, such that the US secures the impermeability
of its own borders only by invading the borders of those whose land
or infrastructure it seeks to secure for its own military and economic
purposes. One might understand the humanitarian preoccupation with
managing ‘vulnerable populations’ as the necessary complement of that
military policy that targets and produces disproportionately vulnerable
populations. My own view is that vulnerability has to be rethought as
interdependency, and anti-militarist interventions at a global level have
to affirm the necessary interconnection of populations. So I do think my
view of vulnerability is part of a broader view of global mobilization and
seeks to criticize both militarism and forms of humanitarian power that
serve as its complement.
 — In your reflection on unchosen cohabitation it seems that the refer-
ence to the right to belong that you formulate in the sentence, by refer-
ring to Arendt, ‘everyone has a right to belong to a place, that right
belongs to everyone regardless of the place to which they belong’ plays
as an ethical possibility that, despite having to be rearticulated from
time according to circumstances, is in some sense trans-historical. It
seems to us that on this point there is a difference in comparison to your
works on recognition, where you stress the historicity of the normative
frameworks of the scene of recognizability, relating in this way to the


Resistance and Vulnerability 243
Foucaultian reflection addressed to underlining the historical dimen-
sion of the constitution of the subject.
 J.B. : Probably, it is important to note first that I read Foucault and I
read Arendt, but I am neither a Foucaultian nor an Arendtian. I think
Arendt’s formulation is at once historical and generalizable and that it
can only be understood through a kind of double-lens. She is making
a normative claim, namely, that everyone has a right to a place. But
she understands that, descriptively speaking, not everyone has a place
to which they belong. She is writing, let us remember, about refugees,
those who have been expelled from the nation-state or who have never
fully belonged (the Roma, for instance). So to say that everyone has a
right to a place (in the sense of ‘ought to have a right’) even when they
do not have a place means that no particular jurisdiction or locale ulti-
mately furnishes that right. When there is no local or national jurisdic-
tion that gives that right, the right still exists. But it is neither founded
in reason nor nature nor any particular regime of positive law. On the
contrary, Arendt is at this moment declaring the right through speech,
which means that her declaration, like the Declaration of the Rights
of Man, is a performative exercise. In a world increasingly full of refu-
gees and exiles, those without citizenship rights or dispossessed of prior
rights to land, the declaration of the right to belong is a kind of parrhe-
siastic speech. When Arendt makes the declaration, she is showing that
the right is a function of its exercise.
 — Finally, even though we do not want to make the mistake of asking
you to give an account of yourself, establishing a narrative linearity,
would you like to tell us what kind of connection you see between your
analysis situated within a feminist horizon and your more recent work?
 J.B. : Well, I certainly still work within a feminist horizon, to be sure,
and perhaps it is possible now to think about forms of feminist thinking
that do not at every moment have to be centred on the question of
women or on gender. The work on vulnerability clearly comes from a
long line of feminist thought but also the work on the public and private
spheres. My early work on gender was focused on performativity, and
that continues to be important to my understanding of political action
in an embodied form. That early work was also focused on unmarked
forms of loving and losing, especially under conditions of compulsory
heterosexuality and pervasive homophobia.
 Some of those same concerns with marking losses and establishing
new terms that would make embodied life more liveable continue in
my recent reflections on war and conflict. So I am perhaps still mili-
tating against imposed forms of melancholia and precarity, and this is


244 Federica Sossi and Martina Tazzioli
as important to think about for non-gender conforming people, trans
people, sex workers as it is for populations living under occupation and/
or subject to bombing, dispossession and destruction. I think perhaps
that my bias in favour of nonviolence recurs throughout my work, as
does the search for modes of recognition that make life more rather than
less liveable.
 Note
* This interview was originally published in materiali foucaultiani, 2(4), pp. 27–36.
We thank materiali foucaultiani’s editorial board for this kind concession.
